Psychological Assessment, Chapter 3

Final Textbook 4, Psychological Assessment, full-length edition: chapter 3 of 11. The book goes up chapter by chapter; the A4 colour and black and white PDFs come once all 11 chapters are in.

Chapter 3 Reliability: Consistency and the Standard Error of Measurement

In this chapter
  1. 3.1 What Reliability Means: True Scores, Error and Classical Test Theory: define reliability, separate random from systematic error, and explain Spearman's true-score model and the reliability coefficient as a ratio of variances.
  2. 3.2 Test-Retest Reliability: Stability over Time: explain how test-retest reliability is estimated, how the interval is chosen and why it suits stable traits but not changing states.
  3. 3.3 Alternate and Parallel Forms: Equivalence: explain parallel and alternate forms, the coefficients of equivalence and of stability and equivalence, and what makes two forms parallel.
  4. 3.4 Split-Half Reliability and the Spearman-Brown Prophecy Formula: split a test into halves, step the half-test correlation up with the Spearman-Brown formula, use the formula to predict the effect of test length, and know when splitting fails.
  5. 3.5 Internal Consistency: The Kuder-Richardson Formulas and Coefficient Alpha: compute and interpret KR-20, KR-21 and Cronbach's alpha, and explain what alpha does and does not tell you.
  6. 3.6 Inter-Rater and Scorer Reliability: measure agreement between scorers with percentage agreement, kappa, weighted kappa, the intraclass correlation and Kendall's W, and explain why scorer reliability matters.
  7. 3.7 The Standard Error of Measurement and Confidence Bands: compute the standard error of measurement, build confidence bands around a score, and judge when two scores really differ.
  8. 3.8 What Raises and Lowers Reliability, and How Much Is Enough: explain the factors that change a reliability coefficient, the standards for acceptable reliability, and how unreliability limits validity.
  9. 3.9 Beyond Classical Test Theory: Generalizability Theory and Item Response Theory: explain how generalizability theory splits error into facets, and how item response theory measures precision at each level of ability.

Weightage. 64 of the 148 questions mapped in this book (43.2%) test this chapter, 30 of them from real past papers. 4 of its 9 sections are High yield.

In a study published in 1912, two members of the staff of the University of Wisconsin, the psychologist Daniel Starch (1883 to 1979) and the educationist Edward Charles Elliott (1874 to 1960), put to the test something every teacher took for granted: that a mark means something. They obtained two answer papers written at the end of the first year's English course by two pupils at one of the largest high schools in Wisconsin. They had printing plates made from the originals, so that the copies reproduced every detail of the handwriting, the crossings-out, the spelling slips and the neatness, and they printed them on the same kind of foolscap paper the pupils had used. A copy of each paper, with the questions, went to 200 high schools belonging to the North Central Association, an association of schools and colleges in the American Midwest, with one request: the principal teacher of first-year English should mark the two papers according to the practices and standards of the school, exactly as he or she marked the school's own pupils. One hundred and fifty-two schools sent the papers back, and 142 of them, marked in the usual way as percentages, could be used.

Ask a teacher how far two markers might disagree about the same paper and, Starch and Elliott reported, the usual answer was "at most 10 points". The 142 teachers disagreed by far more. On the authors' charts the marks for paper A run from about 60 to 97, and those for paper B, the weaker of the two, from about 50 to 98; Starch and Elliott themselves wrote that the range of marks given to one paper "may be as large as 35 or 40 points". The pupil who wrote paper B had been given 75 by his own teacher, comfortably above that school's pass mark of 70, yet 22 of the 142 teachers failed him. Even the order of the two papers was not safe: on average paper B was marked 8 points below paper A, but 19 teachers marked it higher. Almost any ten of the marks, picked at random, covered nearly the whole range. The report appeared in The School Review in 1912 under a title that used the word this chapter is about: "Reliability of the grading of high-school work in English".

Teachers who read the report had an answer ready. English, they said, is a matter of taste; in an exact subject such as mathematics an answer is simply right or wrong, and the marks would agree. So in 1913 Starch and Elliott repeated the experiment with a final examination paper in geometry, again reproduced exactly and sent to about 180 high schools, 140 of which returned it marked ("Reliability of grading work in mathematics"). The geometry marks varied even more than the English ones, from as low as 25 to about 90. Some teachers took heavy marks off for the paper's untidy appearance and others ignored it; one gave 50 after deducting 4 points for spelling. Even the five geometry teachers in the pupil's own school, marking independently, gave 70, 65, 60, 70 and 59: with a pass mark of 70, the same pupil passed in two classrooms and failed in three. A history paper later that year told the same story. Starch and Elliott were not the first to worry. In England the economist and statistician Francis Ysidro Edgeworth (1845 to 1926) had already argued, in papers of 1888 and 1890 with titles such as "The element of chance in competitive examinations", that examiners' marks carry a large element of luck. Studies like these fed a movement, strong in American schools in the 1910s and 1920s, towards "new-type" objective examinations whose answers could be scored with a key. Starch himself later became better known for a quite different kind of measurement, research on how many readers notice and read advertisements, and Elliott went on to become president of Purdue University.

The lesson of the two English papers and the geometry paper is the starting point of this chapter. A mark, or a test score, is not the same thing as the quality it is meant to measure. Every score is partly the pupil's knowledge and partly something else: which teacher marked it, on which day, with which set of questions, in what frame of mind. That something else is error. The question Starch and Elliott forced on their readers is the question of reliability: if we measured again, with another marker, on another day or with another set of questions, would we get the same number? Anyone who has watched Class 10 or Class 12 board marks change on re-evaluation, or heard that a board moderates the marks of different examiners, has met the same problem in India. This chapter shows how psychologists put a number on it: how much of a score is error, how to estimate that error in several different ways, and how to draw an honest band of uncertainty around one person's score. Remember the two English papers and the geometry paper; you will return to them at the end of the chapter.

3.1 What Reliability Means: True Scores, Error and Classical Test TheoryHigh yield · 6 Qs

Each of the 142 English teachers in the opening story marked with care; nearly all of them wrote a separate mark and comment against every answer. None of them could see the error in the mark he or she gave, because error is invisible in a single measurement. It shows itself only when you measure again and the second number does not match the first. That simple fact runs through this whole chapter. This section builds the ideas you need before any method of estimating reliability makes sense: what reliability means and what it does not mean, how it differs from validity, the two kinds of measurement error, and the theory, classical test theory, that turns "error" from a vague worry into a quantity you can estimate. Sections 3.2 to 3.6 then describe the practical methods, one source of error at a time.

Measuring what cannot be seen

When you step on a bathroom scale, the thing you want to know, your weight, presses directly on the instrument. Psychological measurement is never so direct. Nobody can put verbal reasoning, anxiety or intelligence on a scale. What a psychologist can do is give a person a set of tasks or questions and record what the person does: which words she chooses, how many problems she solves in ten minutes, which statements she marks "true of me". From that sample of behaviour the psychologist infers the attribute behind it. This is why Anne Anastasi (1908 to 2001), whose Psychological Testing was a standard textbook of the field for half a century, defined a psychological test as "an objective and standardized measure of a sample of behavior". Chapter 1 unpacks that definition, and Book 1 (Sections 1.4 and 9.2) tells how psychologists learned to measure the mind at all, from the psychophysics laboratories to Galton and Binet.

Notice how many links there are in this chain of inference. The test contains these particular questions and not the many others that could have been written. It is given on this particular morning and not next week. It is given in this hall, by this examiner, with these instructions, to a person who slept well or badly the night before. It is scored by this marker or with this key. At every link something other than the attribute can push the score up or down, and the final score carries all those pushes along with it. Reliability is the study of how large those pushes are.

Meet the test that will follow us through the chapter. Rohini, a doctoral student in psychology in Mysuru, is building a 40-item Verbal Reasoning Test (VRT) for Class 12 students. Every item is scored right (1) or wrong (0), and the items range from easy to hard. She has tried the test on 200 Class 12 students from four colleges. Before anyone uses it, to choose students for a scholarship, to compare colleges, or to tell a student that verbal reasoning is a weak point, Rohini must be able to answer a plain question: how far can a score on her test be trusted? If a student who scores 30 today could just as easily have scored 22 or 36, then a scholarship decided on that score is closer to a lottery than to a judgement, exactly as the pass or fail of paper B was. So Rohini will ask the questions of the coming sections. Would her students get much the same scores if they took the test again in three weeks (Section 3.2)? Would a second, equivalent set of 40 items rank them in the same order (Section 3.3)? Do the two halves of the test agree with each other, and do all 40 items agree (Sections 3.4 and 3.5)? And how far, in points, is any one student's score likely to be from the score he or she deserves (Section 3.7)? Her answers come in those sections. This section supplies the ideas that make the questions precise.

What "reliable" means

In everyday speech a reliable friend, a reliable train or a reliable scooter is one you can count on: it behaves the same way each time. The technical meaning in psychology keeps exactly that core. Reliability is the consistency of measurement: the extent to which a test gives the same results when the same people are measured again, whether on another occasion, with another equivalent set of items, or by another scorer. Put the other way round, reliability is the extent to which scores are free from random measurement error, the part of a score that changes when nothing about the person has changed.

Because the idea can be approached from several sides, textbooks and examiners describe it in several words. It helps to see that they are all one idea, each word stressing a different face of it:

  • Consistency is the general word: repeated measurements of the same thing agree with one another.
  • Stability stresses agreement over time: the same people measured on two occasions keep much the same scores (Section 3.2).
  • Dependability stresses trust: a score can be depended upon to stand for the person, not for the day, the items or the marker.
  • Repeatability or reproducibility stresses that someone repeating the measurement would obtain the same result.
  • Precision stresses how small the scatter of repeated measurements is; Section 3.7 turns precision into a number of score points.
  • Freedom from measurement error is the theoretical definition you will meet later in this section: the proportion of the differences between people's scores that is not due to error.

Each named kind of reliability is the same idea applied to one kind of repetition. Test-retest reliability asks whether the test gives consistent results on two occasions; internal consistency asks whether its items give consistent results with one another; inter-rater reliability asks whether two scorers give consistent results. So "the test will produce consistent results" is the right description of test-retest reliability. "The test is measuring what it claims to measure" is not reliability of any kind (it is validity, the subject of the next subsection), and "the client will improve performance the second time round" is a practice effect, a nuisance for test-retest reliability that Section 3.2 discusses.

The research-methods textbooks use the word in the same sense when they speak of a reliable study. In the social sciences, for example in the widely used Social Research Methods of the British sociologist Alan Bryman (1947 to 2017), a study is reliable when the measures devised for its concepts are consistent, and the consistency has three aspects: stability over time, internal reliability (the items of a scale agree with each other) and inter-observer consistency. A reliable study, in other words, is one whose measures would give stable results on different occasions. Three other virtues of research are often offered as if they were reliability, and none of them is. That a study was conducted by a reputable researcher who can be trusted says something about the researcher's standing, not about the measures. That its findings can be generalised to other people and settings is generalisability, also called external validity. (Section 3.9 meets the word again in a narrower, technical sense: in generalizability theory it means how far a score obtained under one set of conditions, such as one rater on one occasion, stands for the scores the person would obtain under all the conditions that could have been used. Even there, the plain term for the consistency of a measure remains reliability.) That its methods are stated clearly enough for another researcher to repeat it is replicability. A study can have all three and still rest on an unreliable measure.

Table 3.1 collects the terms most often confused with reliability, including those that sit beside it as wrong options. Two of them belong to the business of giving and interpreting a test rather than to its consistency. A test is standardised when it is given and scored in the same way for everyone (the same instructions, time limits and scoring rules), and it is normed when a person's score can be read against the scores of a reference group; a test can be both and still give inconsistent scores. The word sensitivity has two meanings in assessment, and neither is reliability. In the accuracy of a diagnostic or screening test, sensitivity is the proportion of people who have a condition whom the test correctly identifies (Chapter 4 teaches it with its partner, specificity). In outcome measurement, sensitivity to change is a scale's ability to register real improvement or deterioration.

Table 3.1 Reliability and the terms most often confused with it.
TermWhat it meansWhere it is taught
ReliabilityConsistency of scores across occasions, items, forms or scorers; freedom from random errorThis chapter
ValidityThe scores measure what the test claims to measure and support the uses made of themChapter 4
Predictive validityHow well scores forecast later behaviour or a later criterionChapter 4
SensitivityProportion of people with a condition whom a test detects; or a scale's responsiveness to changeChapter 4
StandardisationThe same procedure for giving and scoring the test with everyoneChapter 1
NormsScores of a reference group against which one person's score is readChapter 5
FairnessScores mean the same for every group; no group is disadvantaged by biasChapters 1 and 4
GeneralisabilityHow far findings extend to other people, settings and times (external validity)Research Methodology book; Section 3.9
ReplicabilityMethods reported clearly enough for another researcher to repeat the studyResearch Methodology book
Exam trap

When a question asks what psychometric reliability refers to and offers "free from measurement error", "consistency" and "dependability" as separate options with "all of the above", the key is all of the above: the three phrases describe one property from three sides. The single-answer versions are just as predictable. The term for the consistency of a measure is reliability (not validity, generalizability or sensitivity). A test that gives consistent scores on repeated administration is reliable (not valid, standardised or normed). Reliability is "the consistency of test results over time or samples", not "measures what it is supposed to measure" (validity), "fair to all groups" (fairness) or "predicts future behaviour" (predictive validity). A reliable study is one whose measures are stable on different occasions, not one run by a reputable researcher, one whose findings generalise, or one that can be replicated.

Reliability and validity

Validity asks a different question from reliability. Reliability asks whether a test measures whatever it measures consistently; validity asks whether it measures what it claims to measure, and whether its scores support the decisions people make with them. Chapter 4 is devoted to validity. Here you need only the relation between the two, and the clearest way to see it is the set of targets in Figure 3.1. Imagine that each shot at a target is one measurement of the same person, and that the centre of the target is the person's real standing on the attribute.

In the first target the shots land close together and on the centre: the measurements agree with one another (reliable) and with the truth (valid). In the second they land close together but in the wrong place: consistent, but consistently wrong. A bathroom scale that always reads 3 kilograms too heavy behaves like this. So would a "verbal reasoning" test whose items really depended mostly on knowing English idioms: it might rank students in the same order every time, but the order would be an order of idiom knowledge. In the third target the shots are scattered and off centre, neither consistent nor accurate. The fourth target is the instructive one. The shots are scattered all round the centre, so their average falls on the bullseye, but no single shot can be trusted to be anywhere near it. Some research-methods books label this pattern "valid but not reliable". In testing that label is misleading, because a psychologist never uses the average of endless testings; she uses one score, and a single score that could have landed anywhere is not a valid indicator of anything. Validity in testing is a property of the scores actually used, so it is limited by their reliability.

The relation can therefore be put as a rule: reliability is a necessary but not a sufficient condition for validity. It is necessary because a score that is largely chance cannot correlate strongly with anything, including the attribute it is supposed to measure. It is not sufficient because a score can be perfectly consistent and still measure the wrong thing, as the second target shows. Section 3.8 turns the first half of the rule into arithmetic: a test's validity coefficient cannot exceed the square root of its reliability coefficient.

You have met one practical face of this relation already. Book 2, Section 2.1.5 described the trade-off between reliability and validity in clinical interviewing: a fully structured interview makes two clinicians agree, but agreement does not guarantee that what they agree on is true, and the rigid format may miss whatever the questions do not ask about. Everything said there follows from the rule above. Raising reliability removes one obstacle to validity; it does not by itself deliver validity.

Clinical link

Diagnosis is the clearest clinical example. From DSM-III in 1980 onwards the diagnostic manuals were rebuilt with explicit criteria so that two clinicians in different cities would give the same label to the same patient. Reliability came first because an unreliable diagnosis cannot be valid. But two clinicians who agree can both be wrong, and a category that is diagnosed consistently may still fail to pick out a distinct condition with its own causes, course and response to treatment. Book 3, Section 1.6.6 discusses the reliability and validity of diagnosis; read it with the targets of Figure 3.1 in mind.

Exam trap

A test can be reliable and valid, reliable but not valid, or neither. Its scores cannot be valid without being reliable. So "a test can be reliable without being valid" is true, and "a test can be valid without being reliable" is false. Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity. Watch for the reversed statement, "validity is necessary for reliability": it is false, because a test can be consistently wrong.

Random and systematic error

So far "error" has meant anything that pushes a score away from where it should be. More precisely, measurement error is any influence that makes an observed score differ from the score a perfect measurement would give. It comes in two kinds, and they behave so differently that confusing them is one of the commonest mistakes in this topic. Charles Spearman, whom you will meet properly in the next subsection, drew the distinction in 1904 in the vocabulary of the physical sciences: some inaccuracies are "accidental", "variable" or "compensating", and others are "systematic", "constant" or "non-compensating".

Random error (also called unsystematic or chance error) varies unpredictably in size and direction from one person to the next and from one occasion to the next. It is as likely to raise a score as to lower it, so over many measurements it averages out to zero; that is what "compensating" means. Examples are everywhere. Nandita guesses at two items she does not know and both guesses happen to be right. Aniket slept badly and loses concentration halfway through. A motorcycle starts outside the hall during the hardest item. A student misreads one question. A marker who is generous with one script after lunch is strict with the next. From the pupils' point of view, the error in the opening story was of this kind: nobody could have predicted which teacher a paper would land with, or whether that teacher would mark high or low.

Systematic error (also called constant or biased error) pushes scores in the same direction every time the measurement is made. A cloth tape measure that has stretched with use reads every length a little short, today and tomorrow alike. A marker who adds 5 marks to every script, a stopwatch that runs slow so that every candidate on a timed test gets an extra minute, a translated item that is harder for everyone than the original: all of these move scores, and they move them the same way each time. Repeating the measurement does not reveal such an error, because the repetition contains it too.

Now comes the point that examiners test again and again. Random error lowers reliability; a constant systematic error does not. Reliability is about consistency, and random error is exactly what makes repeated measurements disagree: test Nandita again and her two lucky guesses will not both recur. A constant error, by contrast, is reproduced faithfully every time. Suppose the stretched tape reads every pupil's height about 2 percent short. The tallest pupil is still the tallest, every pupil gets the same reading tomorrow, and the readings correlate perfectly with a second set of readings from the same tape. As an instrument for ranking pupils, the tape is perfectly reliable. What it has lost is accuracy: its readings are wrong in absolute terms, so they are not valid as statements of anyone's height. A constant error therefore damages validity while leaving reliability untouched.

There is a subtler case that matters a great deal for psychological tests. Some systematic errors are not the same for everyone but are stable within each person. A student who always misreads questions containing a double negative, a respondent who tends to agree with any statement put to her (the response set called acquiescence, Chapter 2), a candidate who has drilled this very type of item in a coaching class: each carries the same error into every testing. Because the error recurs, a reliability study counts it as part of the consistent score, and the reliability coefficient stays high. But the score now partly measures double-negative reading, readiness to agree or coaching, not only the attribute the test was built for. Such errors, too, harm validity and not reliability, which is why a high reliability coefficient is never evidence that a test is free of bias. Table 3.2 sets the two kinds of error side by side.

Table 3.2 Random and systematic error compared.
FeatureRandom errorSystematic error
Other namesUnsystematic, chance, variable; Spearman's "accidental" or "compensating"Constant, biased; Spearman's "non-compensating"
DirectionUp or down, unpredictablyThe same direction every time
Over many measurementsAverages out to zeroDoes not average out
ExamplesLucky guess, tired child, noisy hall, lapse of attention, a marker's passing moodStretched tape, a marker who is lenient with everyone, a slow stopwatch, a stable response style
Effect on reliabilityLowers itNone, because it is consistent
Effect on validityLowers it, by lowering reliabilityLowers it directly
How it is detectedReliability methods (Sections 3.2 to 3.6)Validity evidence and comparison with an outside standard (Chapter 4)
Exam trap

Random error lowers reliability; constant (systematic) error lowers validity but not reliability. A favourite question describes a scale or a marker that is "consistently" too high or too low and asks what suffers: the answer is validity (accuracy), not reliability. The reverse also appears: which kind of error does a reliability coefficient estimate? Random error only. A test can be perfectly reliable and still be biased.

Classical test theory: X = T + E

Errors that average out, errors that do not: how do you turn these ideas into numbers? The first person to do so was Charles Edward Spearman (1863 to 1945), the same Spearman whose general factor of intelligence, g, you met in Book 1, Section 9.4. Spearman came to psychology late. He served for years as an officer in the British Army before leaving to study experimental psychology in Wilhelm Wundt's laboratory at Leipzig, and he spent most of his later career at University College London. In 1904, in the American Journal of Psychology, he published two papers that shaped psychology for a century. One, on "general intelligence", introduced g. The other, "The proof and measurement of association between two things", asked how psychologists should measure the relation between two mental qualities, and in answering it Spearman laid the foundation of the theory of reliability.

Key study: Spearman and the "attenuation" of correlations (1904)

Spearman noticed that the correlations psychologists reported between mental measurements were often disappointingly small, and he saw one reason why. Every measurement contains accidental errors, and in a correlation these errors do not cancel out: they always weaken, or in his word attenuate, the apparent relation. His illustration was three balls rolled along a well-kept lawn with different forces: the distances they travel correspond almost perfectly to the forces. Cast the same balls with the same differences of force down a rough mountainside, and the distances bear only a faint relation to the forces. The relation between force and distance is just as real on the mountainside; the bumps hide it.

To recover the true relation, Spearman wrote, it is necessary to make "two or more independent series of observations" of each quality, so that the agreement between repeated measurements reveals how large the errors are. That is the seed of every reliability coefficient in this chapter. He set out the formulae more fully in 1907 ("Demonstration of formulae for true measurement of correlation") and returned to the problem in 1910 in the paper that gave us half of the Spearman-Brown formula (Section 3.4). His correction of a correlation for attenuation is taught in Section 3.8.

Spearman's ideas were taken up and extended by later psychometricians and eventually set out as a complete theory, most influentially by Harold Gulliksen (1903 to 1996) in Theory of Mental Tests (1950) and, in strict mathematical form, by Frederic M. Lord (1912 to 2000) and Melvin R. Novick (1932 to 1986) in Statistical Theories of Mental Test Scores (1968). The result is called classical test theory (CTT), "classical" to distinguish it from the newer theories of Section 3.9. Its central idea, the true-score model, is a single, almost disarmingly simple equation.

Remember: the true-score model

X = T + E

X is the observed score, the score a person actually obtains. T is the true score, the part of the score that would stay the same if the measurement were repeated without end. E is the error score, the difference between the two (E = X − T), which can be positive or negative.

Everything depends on what the true score means, and here intuition misleads almost everyone. In classical test theory the true score is not a person's "real" ability, still less an inborn one. It is defined statistically: a person's true score is the mean of the scores he or she would obtain over an infinite number of independent administrations of the same test. "Independent" means that no testing affects another: no memory of the items, no practice, no fatigue carried over, as if the person's mind were wiped clean before each sitting. Of course this cannot be done; the true score is a thought experiment. But the thought experiment tells you exactly what the true score is.

Suppose Nandita, a student in a coaching class, could take the same 50-item arithmetic test five times under those ideal conditions, and scored 28, 25, 26, 24 and 27. Her mean is (28 + 25 + 26 + 24 + 27) / 5 = 130 / 5 = 26. If five testings were enough to pin down the long-run mean (in theory it takes infinitely many), her true score would be 26, and her error on each testing would be the observed score minus 26: +2, −1, 0, −2 and +1. Notice two things. The errors add up to zero (2 − 1 + 0 − 2 + 1 = 0), because the true score is their centre. And on any real occasion the only number anyone sees is X: Nandita's tutor, looking at a score of 28, has no way of knowing that 2 of those marks were luck.

Three consequences of the definition deserve to be stated plainly, because each is a favourite source of confusion. First, the true score belongs to a particular test. Nandita's true score on this 50-item arithmetic test is not the same thing as her arithmetic ability; another arithmetic test, with different items, would give her a different true score. Second, the true score includes every influence that stays the same across testings, wanted or not. If Nandita always misreads questions with "not" in them, that habit lowers every one of her scores and so lowers her true score; it is systematic, not random, so in classical test theory it is not error at all. This is the subtler case of the previous subsection seen from the inside, and it is why a true score can be "true" and still be a biased measure of the attribute. Third, the true score is not fixed for life. If Nandita learns more arithmetic, her true score rises; it describes her as she is during the period of measurement.

Classical test theory rests on a small set of assumptions about the error score. Learn them in plain words, with an example of what would break each one:

  1. Errors average zero. Over an infinite number of testings a person's errors cancel out, so the mean error is 0 and the mean observed score equals the true score. This is really part of the definition of the true score.
  2. Errors are uncorrelated with true scores. People with high true scores are not systematically luckier or unluckier than people with low true scores, and do not have larger errors. A test that is too easy breaks this assumption: a student whose true score is close to the maximum can lose marks by bad luck but has almost no room to gain them, so near the ceiling the errors are squeezed and lopsided, and their size now depends on the true score.
  3. Errors on different testings are uncorrelated with each other. Luck on Monday tells you nothing about luck on Friday, and the errors on one test are unrelated to the errors, or the true scores, on another. Memory breaks this assumption: if a student remembers on Friday the answers she guessed on Monday, her Monday error is repeated on Friday, and the two testings look more consistent than they should (Section 3.2 discusses this carry-over).

From an equation this short and these three assumptions, a remarkable amount follows: the meaning of the reliability coefficient (next), the reason a correlation between two testings can estimate it, the Spearman-Brown formula of Section 3.4 and the standard error of measurement of Section 3.7.

Exam trap

The true score is the mean of an infinite number of independent testings with the same test. It is not the person's "real", "actual", innate or permanent ability, and it is not a score free of every kind of error: an error that is the same on every testing (a stable bias) is part of the true score. Options such as "the score on a perfectly valid test" or "the person's inborn potential" are wrong. Learn the symbols too: X observed, T true, E error, and E = X − T.

Reliability as a ratio of variances

For one person, the true score and the error can never be separated: you see X and nothing else. Classical test theory gets round this by turning from one person to a group. You cannot know Nandita's error on a particular Tuesday, but you can estimate how large errors are, on average, across a group of people, and that is enough to say how far the test can be trusted. The tool for the job is variance.

Variance measures how spread out a set of scores is. To find it, take each score's distance from the group mean, square the distances (so that distances below the mean do not cancel those above it), and average the squares. Its square root is the familiar standard deviation (SD). A variance of 0 means everyone has the same score; a large variance means the scores are widely spread. That is all the statistics this subsection needs; the Research Methodology book teaches variance in full. (When estimating from a sample, statisticians often divide by one less than the number of people. The demonstration below divides by the number of people, which keeps the arithmetic clean and does not change any of the ratios.)

Because every observed score is a true score plus an error, the differences among people's observed scores have two sources: real differences in their true scores, and differences in their luck. Because errors are uncorrelated with true scores (assumption 2), the two sources simply add.

Remember: the variances add

σ2X = σ2T + σ2E

Observed-score variance (σ2X) equals true-score variance (σ2T) plus error variance (σ2E). The Greek letter σ (sigma) is the usual symbol for a standard deviation, so σ2 is a variance; the subscripts X, T and E say whose variance it is.

If errors tended to be larger for people with high true scores, an extra term would appear in this sum, which is exactly why the assumption matters. Rather than take the addition on trust, watch it happen in Table 3.3. Five students, A to E, have true scores of 10, 12, 14, 16 and 18 on a short test. In real life no one knows true scores; this is a demonstration in which we play the part of an all-seeing examiner. In one version of the testing the errors are small, at most one mark either way; in the other they are large, up to three marks either way. In both versions the errors add up to zero and are unrelated to the true scores.

Table 3.3 Five students with known true scores, tested once with small errors and once with large errors.
Student (true score T)Small error EObserved X = T + ELarge error EObserved X = T + E
A (10)+111+313
B (12)−111−39
C (14)014014
D (16)−115−313
E (18)+119+321
Mean (of T: 14)014014
Variance (of T: 8)0.88.87.215.2
Reliability, 8 / variance of X0.910.53

Work through the true scores first. Their mean is (10 + 12 + 14 + 16 + 18) / 5 = 70 / 5 = 14. Their distances from 14 are −4, −2, 0, +2 and +4; squared, these are 16, 4, 0, 4 and 16, which add up to 40; and 40 / 5 = 8. So the true-score variance, σ2T, is 8.

Now the small errors: +1, −1, 0, −1 and +1. Their mean is 0, so their squared distances are simply 1, 1, 0, 1 and 1, adding to 4, and 4 / 5 = 0.8. The error variance is 0.8. Adding the errors to the true scores gives observed scores of 11, 11, 14, 15 and 19. Their mean is still 70 / 5 = 14, because errors that average zero leave the mean unchanged. Their distances from 14 are −3, −3, 0, +1 and +5; squared, 9, 9, 0, 1 and 25, adding to 44; and 44 / 5 = 8.8. Check the rule: 8 + 0.8 = 8.8. The observed variance is exactly the true variance plus the error variance.

The large errors are +3, −3, 0, −3 and +3: squares 9, 9, 0, 9 and 9, adding to 36, so the error variance is 36 / 5 = 7.2. The observed scores become 13, 9, 14, 13 and 21, still with a mean of 14; their distances from 14 are −1, −5, 0, −1 and +7; squared, 1, 25, 0, 1 and 49, adding to 76; so the variance is 76 / 5 = 15.2. And again 8 + 7.2 = 15.2.

Look at what the large errors did to the ranking. Student B, whose true score is 12, now has the lowest observed score of all, 9, below student A, whose true score is the lowest. Students A and D, whose true scores differ by 6 marks, are tied on 13. A teacher handed the right-hand column would draw several wrong conclusions about who is better than whom, and nothing in the column would warn her. This is what unreliability does: it does not merely add noise to each score, it scrambles the comparisons between people.

We can now say precisely what reliability is. The reliability coefficient is the proportion of the observed-score variance that is true-score variance.

Remember: the reliability coefficient

rxx = σ2T / σ2X = 1 − σ2E / σ2X

rxx is the reliability coefficient; σ2T is the true-score variance; σ2X is the observed-score variance; σ2E is the error variance. The two forms say the same thing, because σ2T = σ2X − σ2E.

The symbol rxx is read "r sub x x". The double subscript marks a correlation of a test with itself, or more exactly with an equivalent version of itself, which is how reliability is estimated in practice (as the next subsection explains). For the five students the arithmetic is short. With small errors, rxx = 8 / 8.8 = 0.909, or 0.91 rounded to two decimal places; equivalently, 1 − 0.8 / 8.8 = 1 − 0.091 = 0.909. With large errors, rxx = 8 / 15.2 = 0.526, or 0.53; equivalently, 1 − 7.2 / 15.2 = 1 − 0.474 = 0.526. In plain words: in the first testing about 91 percent of the differences among the students' scores reflect real differences between them; in the second, only about half do.

The limits follow at once. If there were no error at all, σ2E would be 0, the observed variance would be all true variance, and rxx would be 1. If the scores were all error, as if the students had answered by tossing coins, there would be no true differences, σ2T would be 0, and rxx would be 0. A reliability coefficient therefore runs from 0 to 1, and every real test lies somewhere in between.

Rohini's test gives a full-sized example. Her 200 students' scores on the 40-item VRT have a standard deviation of 6 points, so the observed-score variance is 6 × 6 = 36. As Section 3.5 will show, the reliability estimated from the agreement among its 40 items is 0.84. Then the true-score variance is 0.84 × 36 = 30.24, the error variance is 36 − 30.24 = 5.76, and the check, 30.24 / 36, gives back 0.84. Figure 3.2 draws this split as a bar and sets beside it a weaker test, with a reliability of 0.50, in which the same observed variance is half true and half error. Keep the number 5.76 in mind: its square root, 2.4 points, will reappear in Section 3.7 as the standard error of measurement of Rohini's test.

One more step, and it is where many students slip. In an ordinary correlation, such as the correlation between height and weight, the coefficient r (Pearson's product-moment correlation, named after the British statistician Karl Pearson, 1857 to 1936) runs from −1 to +1 and describes how closely two sets of scores go together. To find the proportion of variance the two measures share, you square it, so a correlation of 0.50 means 0.25, or 25 percent, shared variance. (The Research Methodology book teaches correlation in full.) A reliability coefficient is not squared. It is already a proportion of variance by definition: rxx = 0.84 means that 84 percent of the observed-score variance is true-score variance and 16 percent is error variance. Squaring it to 0.71 would be a mistake. The reason is that the reliability coefficient is itself a squared quantity. The correlation between observed scores and true scores is the square root of the reliability, √0.84 ≈ 0.92 for Rohini's test, and squaring that correlation gives back 0.84, the proportion of variance. This square root has a name of its own, the index of reliability, and Section 3.8 uses it to set a ceiling on validity.

Notice also what the 84 percent describes: the differences between people in a group. It does not mean that 84 percent of any one student's score is true and 16 percent is error. A student who scores 30 on the VRT does not have 25.2 "true marks" and 4.8 "error marks". How far one person's score may lie from his or her true score is a different question, and the standard error of measurement answers it in Section 3.7.

Exam trap

A reliability coefficient is read directly as a proportion of variance: rxx = 0.80 means 80 percent true-score variance and 20 percent error variance. Do not square it (0.64 is a wrong option papers like to offer); it is the ordinary correlation between two different variables that is squared to give shared variance. Learn both forms of the definition. If a question gives an observed variance of 40 and an error variance of 8, the reliability is 1 − 8 / 40 = 1 − 0.20 = 0.80, not 8 / 40 = 0.20.

Parallel tests: how a correlation can estimate reliability

There is an obvious difficulty with everything in the last subsection. The definition of reliability needs the true-score variance, and true scores can never be observed. Table 3.3 worked only because we pretended to know them. How can a real test developer like Rohini ever compute rxx? Spearman had pointed to the answer in 1904: measure twice. Classical test theory makes the answer exact through the idea of parallel tests.

Two tests are parallel tests, in the strict classical sense, if every person has exactly the same true score on both and the two tests have equal error variances. Picture two forms of an arithmetic test written so carefully that, for every student, the long-run average would be the same on either form, and the amount of luck involved would be the same on both. Several things follow from the definition. Parallel tests have equal means (the same true scores, plus errors that average zero), equal observed variances (the same true variance plus the same error variance), and equal correlations with any other variable. Those observable equalities are how test developers check whether two real forms come close to being parallel, as Section 3.3 explains.

The key result is that the correlation between two parallel tests equals the reliability of either test. The reasoning needs only one new idea. The covariance of two sets of scores measures how much they vary together: it is large and positive when the people who are high on one are also high on the other. A correlation is simply a covariance divided by the product of the two standard deviations, which puts it on the familiar scale from −1 to +1. Now write a person's scores on two parallel tests as X1 = T + E1 and X2 = T + E2. What do the two scores have in common? Only T, because the two errors are unrelated to each other and to T (assumptions 2 and 3). So the two sets of scores vary together exactly as much as the true scores vary, and their covariance equals the true-score variance, σ2T. Dividing by the product of the two standard deviations, which are equal, so that σX × σX = σ2X, gives the result.

Remember: parallel tests

r12 = σ2T / σ2X = rxx

The correlation between scores on two parallel tests (r12) equals the proportion of observed-score variance that is true-score variance, which is the reliability coefficient. A quantity that cannot be observed, σ2T, is estimated through one that can, a correlation.

This is the bridge from theory to practice, and it explains why every practical method in the chapter is a way of obtaining a second measurement that is as nearly parallel to the first as possible. Give the same test again after an interval and correlate the two sets of scores (test-retest reliability, Section 3.2). Build a second form to the same blueprint and correlate the forms (parallel or alternate forms, Section 3.3). Split one test into two halves and treat them as two short parallel tests (split-half reliability, Section 3.4). Treat every item as a tiny parallel test and look at how well they all agree (internal consistency, Section 3.5). Have a second scorer score the same answers (scorer reliability, Section 3.6). Each method makes a different kind of second measurement, and so, as the last subsection shows, each counts a different kind of variation as error.

Exam trap

The correlation between two parallel forms is the reliability estimate: if two parallel forms correlate 0.81, the reliability of each is 0.81. Do not square it and do not take its square root (the square root, 0.90, is the index of reliability, a different quantity). In the strict classical sense, "parallel" means equal true scores and equal error variances, and therefore equal means and equal variances. Alternate forms built to the same blueprint only approach this ideal (Section 3.3).

The domain-sampling model

Classical test theory pictures a person taking the same test over and over. A second way of thinking about reliability pictures something different: the test itself as a sample. Rohini's 40 items are not the only verbal reasoning items that could be written for Class 12 students. There are thousands of possible analogies, syllogisms and inference questions at that level, and she has chosen 40 of them. The domain-sampling model treats a test as a random sample of items drawn from this large domain, or universe, of possible items, in the same way that an opinion poll treats a few thousand voters as a sample from the whole electorate.

The score a person would obtain if she could answer every item in the domain is her universe score, the domain-sampling version of the true score. A spelling test makes the idea concrete. If a child could be tested on every word in the language, the percentage she spelled correctly would be her universe score; a 50-word spelling test estimates that percentage from a sample. Error, in this model, is content sampling error: the luck of which items happened to be drawn. Aniket, a keen cricket follower, may find that five words about cricket happened to be drawn into a vocabulary test; on another draw of words he would have scored lower. Nobody did anything wrong, and Aniket did not change; the sample simply favoured him. Figure 3.3 draws the model.

Three ideas follow from the model, and each sets up a later section. First, reliability in this model is a matter of how well a sample of items stands for the domain. It can be expressed as the correlation to be expected between two tests of the same length drawn at random from the same domain, which is why two such tests behave like the parallel tests of the previous subsection; the correlation between a person's score on one sample and the universe score is the square root of that reliability. Second, the larger the sample of items, the closer each person's score comes to the universe score, just as a larger poll comes closer to the election result. Longer tests are therefore more reliable, a relation that the Spearman-Brown formula of Section 3.4 puts into numbers. Third, if every item is a sample from the same domain, the items should agree with one another, and how well they agree tells us how well the test samples the domain; that is the logic of the internal consistency coefficients, KR-20 and Cronbach's alpha, in Section 3.5. Section 3.9 widens the idea: generalizability theory treats not only items but also raters, occasions and settings as samples from universes of their own.

Where error comes from: a map of the methods

Put the true-score model and the domain-sampling model together and you can list the places where random error comes from. It is useful to group them into five sources, because each method of estimating reliability is sensitive to some of them and blind to others.

  1. Content or item sampling. The particular items chosen may happen to suit some people better than others, as the cricket words suited Aniket. A different set of equally good items would reorder people a little.
  2. Time sampling. Scores fluctuate from one occasion to another for reasons that have nothing to do with the attribute being measured, so a person tested on Monday rather than Thursday gets a slightly different score. This is time sampling error: the luck of which occasion happened to be chosen.
  3. The examinee's temporary state. Fatigue, a headache, hunger, worry about a sick parent, low motivation on the day, a quarrel at home: all affect performance on one occasion and not on the next.
  4. Administration conditions. Noise, heat, poor light, a slip in timing, an examiner who reads the instructions faster or more warmly than another examiner.
  5. Scoring. Markers apply different standards, the same marker drifts as the pile of scripts goes down, and clerks make slips in adding up marks. Starch and Elliott's teachers showed this source at its largest.

The third and fourth sources usually show up as differences between occasions: a student who was unwell at the first testing and well at the second gets a different score each time. So they are detected by the same methods that detect time sampling. Table 3.4 is the map for the rest of the chapter. Read it as a list of questions: if you want to know whether scores survive a change of day, of items or of scorer, the table tells you which method asks that question and where it is taught.

Table 3.4 The sources of random error and the methods that treat each as error.
Source of errorExamplesMethod that detects itSection
Content (item) samplingWhich items happened to be chosenParallel forms at one sitting; split-half; KR-20 and alpha3.3, 3.4, 3.5
Content heterogeneityItems that measure somewhat different thingsKR-20, KR-21 and alpha3.5
Time samplingChanges between occasions; forgettingTest-retest; parallel forms with an interval3.2, 3.3
Examinee's temporary stateFatigue, illness, anxiety, low motivationTest-retest; parallel forms with an interval3.2, 3.3
Administration conditionsNoise, timing slips, examiner's mannerTest-retest; parallel forms with an interval3.2, 3.3
ScoringMarkers' standards and judgement; clerical slipsInter-rater and scorer reliability3.6
Several sources at onceItems, raters and occasions togetherGeneralizability theory3.9

The map carries a lesson that is easy to miss: there is no such thing as the reliability of a test. A test-retest coefficient counts changes between occasions as error but treats the particular items as fixed, so content-sampling error is invisible to it. An internal consistency coefficient, from a single sitting, counts disagreement among the items as error but is blind to anything that changes from one day to the next. A coefficient of 0.90 from one method and 0.75 from another need not contradict each other: they answer different questions. A good test manual says which coefficients it reports, from which groups and over what interval, and a good user chooses the coefficient that matches the error that matters for her purpose. If Rohini's test were used to select students in March on the strength of a sitting in January, stability over time would matter most; if two forms were used in alternate years, the equivalence of the forms would matter most.

The same reasoning shows that reliability is a property of scores, not of a test. A reliability coefficient is a ratio of true variance to observed variance, and both depend on the group tested. Give Rohini's test to a group of students who are all strong in verbal reasoning, and the true differences among them shrink while the errors stay much the same size; the ratio falls, and the coefficient comes out lower, although not a single item has changed. Give it in a noisy hall, and the error variance grows. Strictly, then, a reliability coefficient describes the scores of a particular group, tested on a particular occasion under particular conditions, and "this test is reliable" is shorthand for "scores from this test were reliable in groups like the one studied". Section 3.8 develops this idea and the other factors that raise and lower a coefficient.

Indian context

Many tests used in India were first built and standardised abroad and then translated or adapted. Neither half of the reliability ratio travels automatically. Translation changes the items (an idiom that is easy in English may be obscure in Hindi or Kannada, and a clear item can become ambiguous), and Indian examinees may differ from the original sample both in the spread of the attribute and in their familiarity with the test format. An adaptation therefore needs its own reliability evidence, gathered on Indian samples like the people it will be used with, and the coefficients in a foreign manual should not be quoted as if they applied here. When you read the manual of an Indian adaptation, look for coefficients from Indian groups, and note how large and how varied those groups were.

Exam trap

Reliability belongs to scores from a particular group under particular conditions, not to a test for all time: the same test can give different coefficients in different groups. Different methods detect different sources of error, so the test-retest, parallel-forms, split-half and alpha coefficients of one test need not agree. Content-sampling error is invisible to test-retest reliability; error that changes from day to day is invisible to every single-session method.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. In classical test theory every observed score is the sum of a true score and an score.
  2. The true score is defined as the of the scores a person would obtain over an infinite number of independent testings with the same test.
  3. Error that pushes every score in the same direction is error; it lowers validity but not reliability.
  4. A test can be reliable without being valid, but scores that are not reliable cannot be .
  5. In 1904 Spearman showed that accidental errors of measurement always , or weaken, the correlation between two measures.
  6. The reliability coefficient is the proportion of observed-score variance that is variance.
  7. A test's observed-score variance is 60 and its error variance is 15. Its reliability is .
  8. A test's observed-score variance is 25 and its reliability is 0.64. Its true-score variance is .
  9. A reliability coefficient of 0.75 means that percent of the observed-score variance is true-score variance.
  10. Two tests on which every person has the same true score, with equal error variances, are called tests, and their correlation equals the reliability.

Answers: (1) error, (2) mean (average), (3) systematic (constant), (4) valid, (5) attenuate, (6) true-score, (7) 0.75 (1 − 15 / 60 = 1 − 0.25 = 0.75), (8) 16 (0.64 × 25 = 16; the error variance is the other 9), (9) 75 (the coefficient is read directly, not squared), (10) parallel.

3.2 Test-Retest Reliability: Stability over TimeHigh yield · 9 Qs

Jhanvi works in the counselling centre of a large college in Bengaluru, and she has written a new 30-item anxiety scale for college students. Each item describes a feeling ("I worry that something will go wrong", "I find it hard to relax") and the student marks how often, in general, it is true of them. Before the counsellors start using the scale, Jhanvi wants to know whether a student's score can be trusted. So she gives the scale to 150 students in the first week of term, waits two weeks, gives the identical scale to the same 150 students, and correlates the two sets of scores. What has she established? If you answered "test-retest reliability", you already hold the most intuitive idea in this chapter: to find out whether a measurement is consistent, measure the same thing again and see whether it comes out the same. The idea is simple, but it raises questions that are not. How long should Jhanvi wait between the two testings? Which kinds of error can her method catch, and which can it never see? And why would exactly the same design have been a mistake if her scale had asked students how anxious they felt at that very moment? This section answers those questions one at a time.

The logic of retesting: one test, one group, two occasions

Section 3.1 showed that every observed score is a true score plus error, and that reliability is the share of the differences between people's scores that reflects true differences rather than error. The difficulty is that nobody can see a true score. Test-retest reliability gets round the difficulty with a simple argument. If a person's true score has not changed between two testings, then whatever stays the same from the first score to the second is probably true score, and whatever bounces about is error. Give the test twice, and see how much bounces.

Formally, test-retest reliability is estimated by giving the same test to the same group of people on two separate occasions and correlating the two sets of scores. The correlation is called the coefficient of stability, because it tells you how stable the scores are over the interval between the two testings. Lee Joseph Cronbach (1916 to 2001), whose coefficient alpha you will meet in Section 3.5, set this name out beside its relatives in a 1947 paper in Psychometrika, arguing that the single word "reliability" was being used for several different things: the coefficient of stability (the same test on two occasions), the coefficient of equivalence (two forms of a test at one sitting) and the coefficient of stability and equivalence (two forms on two occasions). Section 3.3 takes up the last two. Three ingredients define the test-retest design, and you should look for all three whenever a study is described to you: one test (the identical form, not a second version), one group (the same people both times) and two occasions separated by an interval. The statistic is a correlation between the first-occasion scores and the second-occasion scores.

What does that correlation actually measure? A correlation coefficient (Pearson's r, which the Research Methodology book teaches in full) runs from −1 through 0 to +1 and describes how closely two sets of scores keep step with each other. For a reliability study only one thing about it matters: it asks whether people keep their positions relative to one another. If the student who scored highest the first time scores highest again, the lowest stays lowest, and everyone in between keeps roughly the same place and the same distance from the average, the correlation is high. It does not ask whether the scores themselves are identical. That distinction will matter several times in this section, so it is worth seeing the arithmetic once.

In words, the recipe is this. For each person, find how far the first score lies above or below the first average, and how far the second score lies from the second average. Multiply the two distances together. Add up these products over everyone. Finally, divide by a number that keeps the answer between −1 and +1. As a formula:

r = Σ(d1 × d2) / √(Σd12 × Σd22)

Here d1 is a person's deviation (distance from the mean) on the first occasion, d2 is the same person's deviation on the second occasion, and Σ means "add up over all the people". The top line grows large when people who were above average the first time are above average again (a positive deviation times a positive deviation) and people who were below average stay below (a negative times a negative, which is also positive). The bottom line is the largest value the top line could possibly reach, so the ratio can never exceed 1.

Shrink Jhanvi's study to five students, called A to E, so that every step is visible. Table 3.5 shows their scores on the two occasions, with each deviation in brackets.

Table 3.5 A test-retest correlation worked by hand: five students take the same anxiety scale twice, two weeks apart.
StudentTime 1 score (d1)Time 2 score (d2)d1 × d2d12 and d22
A20 (−10)26 (−7)70100 and 49
B30 (0)27 (−6)00 and 36
C25 (−5)33 (0)025 and 0
D40 (+10)40 (+7)70100 and 49
E35 (+5)39 (+6)3025 and 36
Total150 (mean 30)165 (mean 33)170250 and 170

Step 1: the means. At time 1 the scores add up to 20 + 30 + 25 + 40 + 35 = 150, so the mean is 150 / 5 = 30. At time 2 they add up to 26 + 27 + 33 + 40 + 39 = 165, so the mean is 165 / 5 = 33. Step 2: the deviations. Student A scored 20 at time 1, which is 10 below the mean of 30, so d1 = −10; at time 2 A scored 26, which is 7 below the new mean of 33, so d2 = −7. The other deviations are found the same way. Step 3: multiply each pair. A gives (−10) × (−7) = 70, B gives 0 × (−6) = 0, C gives (−5) × 0 = 0, D gives 10 × 7 = 70 and E gives 5 × 6 = 30. The products add up to 170. Step 4: square the deviations and add them. At time 1 this gives 100 + 0 + 25 + 100 + 25 = 250; at time 2 it gives 49 + 36 + 0 + 49 + 36 = 170. Step 5: put the pieces into the formula: r = 170 / √(250 × 170) = 170 / √42,500 = 170 / 206.16 = 0.8246, which rounds to 0.82.

Now look at what moved. On average the students scored 3 points higher the second time (the mean rose from 30 to 33), but they did not all rise by 3. A rose by 6, C by 8 and E by 4, D stayed exactly where D was, and B actually fell by 3. It is these unequal changes, which shuffle the students' relative positions a little, that pulled the coefficient down from a perfect 1.00 to 0.82. To prove it, suppose instead that every student had gained exactly 3 points, so that the time 2 scores were 23, 33, 28, 43 and 38. Every time 2 deviation would then equal the time 1 deviation, every product would equal a square, the top line would be 250, and r = 250 / √(250 × 250) = 250 / 250 = 1.00. A gain that is the same for everyone does not lower a correlation at all, because a correlation is blind to a change in the average. Keep that fact ready: it explains why practice effects sometimes matter and sometimes do not (later in this section), and why a good manual reports the average change on retesting as well as the coefficient.

Real reliability studies use far more people than five, and the example that runs through this chapter is a real-sized one. Rohini, whose 40-item Verbal Reasoning Test (VRT) for Class 12 students you met in Section 3.1, gave the test to 200 students from four colleges and then, three weeks later, gave the identical test to the same 200 students. The two sets of scores correlated 0.80. How should she read that number? Section 3.1 explained that a reliability coefficient is read directly as a proportion of variance, without squaring it. So the coefficient of stability says that about 80 percent of the differences among the 200 students' scores reflect differences that held steady across three weeks, and about 20 percent reflect influences that changed from one occasion to the other: a headache on the first day, a better night's sleep before the second, a lucky guess on one occasion and not on the other. Figure 3.4 shows what a coefficient of that size looks like as a cloud of points, next to one that is much lower.

Notice how the result was stated: r = 0.80, after three weeks, in 200 Class 12 students. A coefficient of stability means little without its interval and its group. The same test retested after three days would probably correlate higher, because students would remember items; retested after a year, lower, because their verbal reasoning would really have grown. A careful manual therefore reports the interval, the sample and, ideally, the average change in scores beside every retest coefficient, and it often reports more than one interval. You will also meet a second number for the VRT later in the chapter, an internal consistency coefficient of 0.84 (Section 3.5). Neither number is "the" reliability of the VRT: each counts a different source of error, and Section 3.8 lines them up. One modern refinement is worth knowing as well. Because Pearson's r ignores a shift in the average, many researchers now analyse retest data with an intraclass correlation of the absolute-agreement kind (Section 3.6), which falls when scores change in absolute size as well as when people change places.

Exam trap

"What is test-retest reliability?" The answer is that the test will produce consistent results when the same people take it again. "The test is measuring what it claims to be measuring" is the definition of validity (Chapter 4), a different property altogether. "The client will improve performance second time round" describes a practice effect, which is a threat to a retest study, not its meaning. "All of the above" is therefore wrong. To recognise the design in a vignette, ask three questions. How many forms? (One.) How many occasions? (Two.) What is correlated with what? (The first scores with the second.) One form, two occasions, the scores correlated: that is test-retest reliability, whether the vignette describes an anxiety scale given to college students two weeks apart or an intelligence test given to children a month apart.

What retesting catches and what it misses

Section 3.1 sorted the sources of random error into families: the particular items chosen (content sampling), the particular occasion (time sampling), the examinee's temporary state, the conditions of administration, and the scoring. Every method of estimating reliability is a net that catches some of these families and lets others slip through, and the quickest way to understand any method is to ask which errors land in its net.

The test-retest net is built to catch time sampling error: the error that arises because a test is given on one particular occasion rather than another. Anything that affects a person's score on one day but not on the next shows up in a retest study as a change of position, and so lowers the coefficient. The list is long. Some causes lie in the person: illness, a poor night's sleep, a quarrel at breakfast, worry about a sick parent, hunger, low motivation, a sudden burst of confidence. Some lie in the conditions: a noisy hall, a hot afternoon, a different examiner, a power cut halfway through. And some are pure chance: a student who guesses on five items will guess right on some of them the first time and on different ones the second. Because these influences vary from one occasion to the next, they produce scores that change, and the coefficient of stability measures how much they matter.

Now consider an error that does not vary from one occasion to the next. Imagine a 40-item vocabulary test. There are thousands of words the test developer might have chosen, and the 40 that happened to be chosen suit some students better than others. A student who reads a lot about cricket may find that three of the words ("crease", "googly", "maiden") are old friends, while an equally able student who has never followed the game finds them strange. That is content sampling error (also called item sampling error): the luck of which items ended up on the test. On the retest, the cricket words are still there. The cricket fan benefits again, the other student is handicapped again, and the luck of the draw is repeated exactly. Because it is the same both times, it looks like consistency, and the retest coefficient counts it as true score. A coefficient of stability therefore answers a narrower question than you might think. It answers: "Would this person earn the same score on these particular items on another day?" It does not answer: "Would this person earn the same score on another set of items drawn from the same domain?" To catch content sampling error you need a second set of items, which is what alternate forms (Section 3.3) and the split-half and internal consistency methods (Sections 3.4 and 3.5) provide.

The same logic applies to any error that repeats itself. Section 3.1 distinguished random error from constant (systematic) error, like a weighing scale that always reads two kilograms heavy. A constant error is the same on both occasions, so a retest study cannot see it either; it is a problem for validity, not for reliability. If Jhanvi's scale contained an item that every student misread in the same way, they would misread it both times, and the coefficient of stability would be none the wiser.

Remember

Test-retest reliability: the same test, the same people, two occasions, the two sets of scores correlated. The result is the coefficient of stability. It detects time sampling error (changes in the person, the conditions and chance from one occasion to the next). It cannot detect content sampling error, because the same items are used both times, nor any constant error that repeats itself.

Choosing the interval: memory, practice and real change

Jhanvi waited two weeks. Why not two hours, or two years? The interval is the single most important decision in a retest study, because a wrong choice in either direction produces a coefficient that answers the wrong question.

Make the interval too short and the second testing is contaminated by the first. People remember their answers. On an ability test they remember which option they chose, or the solution to a puzzle that took them five minutes to crack the first time; on a questionnaire they remember how they described themselves and, wanting to seem consistent, simply say the same again. The general name for this problem is a carry-over effect: any influence of the first testing session on the scores obtained in the second. Memory for particular items is one carry-over effect. The other important one is the practice effect, the improvement that comes simply from having done the test before: familiarity with the format, less nervousness, better pacing, and strategies worked out on the first attempt.

Memory inflates the coefficient for a precise reason. Section 3.1 listed among the assumptions of the true-score model that the errors on two testings are uncorrelated: the luck that helped you on Monday should be fresh luck, good or bad, on the next occasion. A remembered answer breaks that assumption. If a student guessed an item correctly on the first occasion and remembers the guess, the lucky answer is repeated on the second; the error has been copied, and copied error looks exactly like consistency. The shorter the interval, the more is copied, and the more the coefficient overstates how consistent the test really is.

Practice effects are more subtle, and here the five students of Table 3.5 pay off. If practice raised everyone's score by the same amount, every deviation from the mean would stay the same and the correlation would not change at all, exactly as the "everyone gains 3 points" version of the example showed. A uniform, systematic carry-over of this kind leaves the coefficient untouched, though it does make the second scores misleading if anyone reads them against norms collected at a first testing. The damage comes from carry-over that differs from person to person. Some people gain a lot from practice and others gain nothing; some remember the items and others do not. A test made of puzzles is the classic case: a matrix problem that one student solved by a flash of insight on the first occasion is no longer a problem for that student on the second, while the student who failed it faces it fresh. Gains that are unequal and unrelated to the first scores shuffle the rank order, as C's gain of 8 points and B's loss of 3 did in Table 3.5, and they lower the coefficient for reasons that have nothing to do with the quality of the test.

Make the interval too long and a different problem appears: the people really change. Students learn; children develop, and fast (a six-year-old's vocabulary one year later is a different vocabulary); patients recover with treatment or decline with illness; life events reshape mood and outlook. When true scores change, and change by different amounts for different people, the rank order shifts for reasons that are not measurement error at all, and the coefficient falls. A low coefficient after a long interval is therefore ambiguous. It may mean the test is unreliable, or it may mean that the attribute has changed, and the retest design alone cannot tell you which.

The right interval is long enough for memory and practice to have faded and short enough that the attribute has not really changed. For most ability and personality tests, intervals of about two weeks to a month are usual; A. K. Singh's Tests, Measurements and Research Methods in Behavioural Sciences, a standard Indian textbook, recommends a fortnight as the most convenient gap. Young children need shorter intervals because they change faster, and an attribute that is expected to hold steady for years, such as general intelligence in adults, can tolerate longer ones. Figure 3.5 draws the trade-off.

There is a subtler way in which the first testing can contaminate the second. Sometimes the act of being measured changes the very thing being measured. This is reactivity, which in assessment research is often called assessment reactivity. A questionnaire about drinking makes a person count their drinks, perhaps for the first time; an attitude scale makes someone think about an issue they had never considered, and they form an opinion; a detailed checklist of worries may itself set off a little worrying. The person who arrives for the second testing is not quite the person who took the first. Reactivity is not merely a theoretical worry, as the following study shows.

Key study: can answering questions change behaviour? (McCambridge and Kypri, 2011)

In trials of brief interventions for heavy drinking, the "control" group receives no intervention, but it still answers detailed questions about its drinking at the start of the trial. Jim McCambridge and Kypros Kypri asked whether answering those questions might itself change drinking. They searched for trials in which chance had decided which participants were questioned and which were not, and found ten (two of them gave no usable figures). Combining the rest, they found that people who had answered the questions later reported drinking a little less: a small difference of roughly 14 grams of alcohol a week (about one and a half British units) and about one point on the Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test, a standard screening questionnaire. Their conclusion, published in PLoS ONE, was that being assessed appears to alter later self-reported behaviour. The lesson for reliability is direct. When the first testing changes people, and changes some more than others, the second scores differ for reasons that are neither the test's fault nor the kind of real change that would have happened anyway.

Finally, the retest design has practical costs that the formula hides. It needs two sessions, which doubles the testing time and the trouble of arranging it. Some people do not come back, and if those who drop out differ from those who return (the more anxious students may be exactly the ones who avoid a second session of an anxiety scale), the coefficient describes a different group from the one first tested. And people are often less motivated the second time: a test that was interesting once is boring twice, and boredom adds its own random error. None of this makes the method a bad one. It makes it a method whose details must be reported, and read, with care.

Exam trap

Get the directions right. Too short an interval inflates the coefficient of stability, because memory, practice and other carry-over effects make the second score copy the first. Too long an interval lowers it, because real change in the people is mistaken for unreliability. A practice effect that raises everyone by the same amount does not lower the correlation at all, because r ignores a change in the average; practice effects that differ between people do lower it. Reactivity means that the first testing changed the person, not that the test or the scorer changed.

Traits and states: when a low coefficient is not a defect

Every retest study rests on an assumption so obvious that it is easy to forget: that the thing being measured did not change between the two testings. The whole logic of the method (whatever stays the same is true score, whatever bounces about is error) works only if the true score itself stays put. That assumption is reasonable for some psychological attributes and absurd for others, and the distinction between them is one of the most important ideas in this chapter.

A trait is a relatively enduring characteristic of a person, one that shows itself consistently across time and situations: general intelligence, verbal ability, extraversion, conscientiousness, a habitual tendency to worry. A state is a temporary condition that rises and falls with circumstances: how anxious you feel in the minutes before a viva, your mood this evening, how tired, hungry or irritable you are right now. A trait measure should give much the same rank order this month and next. A state measure should not, because the people's states will have changed, and a good state measure must follow them. If you give a mood scale to a class on a Monday morning and again on a Friday afternoon, the correlation may well be low, but that tells you that moods changed, not that the scale is careless. Test-retest reliability is therefore appropriate for attributes that are expected to stay stable over the retest interval, such as intelligence and personality traits, and inappropriate for rapidly changing states, where a low coefficient is exactly what a good test should produce.

Key study: one questionnaire, two kinds of anxiety (Spielberger, 1970)

The clearest demonstration comes from the State-Trait Anxiety Inventory (STAI), published in 1970 by Charles Donald Spielberger (1927 to 2013) with Richard L. Gorsuch and Robert E. Lushene.
