Spielberger argued that the word "anxiety" had been used for two different things, and he built a 20-item scale for each. The state scale asks people how they feel right now, at this moment; the trait scale asks how they generally feel. In the retest studies reported for the inventory, on high-school and college students retested after intervals ranging from an hour to a little over three months, the trait scale's coefficients fell roughly between 0.65 and 0.86, while the state scale's ranged from as low as 0.16 to only about 0.6. Spielberger did not conclude that his state scale was unreliable. A scale designed to register the anxiety of the moment is supposed to give different scores on different days, because people's anxiety differs from day to day. Its quality has to be judged in other ways: whether its items agree with one another within a single sitting (Section 3.5), and whether its scores rise when people are put under stress and fall when they relax, which is a question of validity (Chapter 4). The STAI, revised in 1983, is still one of the most widely used anxiety measures in the world, and the contrast between its two scales is the textbook example of why the method of estimating reliability must suit the construct.

How, then, do you estimate the reliability of a state measure? You choose a method that does not require the state to hold still for weeks. One option is to measure consistency within a single sitting, by splitting the items or by internal consistency (Sections 3.4 and 3.5): if the items agree with one another at the moment of testing, the scale is measuring the momentary state consistently. Another is to give two alternate forms at one sitting (Section 3.3). A third is a retest after a very short interval, minutes or hours rather than weeks, so short that the state cannot plausibly have changed; this works best for physiological measures and for tasks where remembering the items is not a problem. Table 3.6 sets the two kinds of attribute side by side.

Table 3.6 Traits and states, and what each means for test-retest reliability.
FeatureTraitState
What it isAn enduring dispositionA temporary condition
ExamplesIntelligence, extraversion, trait anxietyCurrent mood, state anxiety, fatigue
Retest r over some weeksHigh, if the test is goodLow, even if the test is good
A low retest r suggestsThe test may be unreliableThe state has changed
Best reliability estimatesTest-retest and internal consistencyInternal consistency, forms at one sitting, a very short retest

Return now to Jhanvi. Her items ask how often each feeling is true of a student in general, so her scale is meant to measure trait anxiety, and a two-week retest is exactly the right check: a good trait scale should rank the students in much the same order a fortnight later. Had she written "Right now I feel tense", she would have been measuring a state, and a two-week retest would have punished her scale for doing its job. The first question to ask about any retest coefficient, then, is not "Is it high?" but "Should it be high, given what the test measures and how long the interval was?"

Exam trap

"Test-retest reliability is most appropriate for ..." The answer is stable trait measures: constructs expected to stay stable over the retest interval, such as intelligence and personality. The other options fail for different reasons. Measures of a rapidly changing state (current mood, state anxiety) are the classic wrong answer, because real change lowers the coefficient and would make a good state measure look bad. Single-item measures only is wrong because of the word "only": retesting is in fact one of the few ways to estimate the reliability of a single item (internal consistency needs several items to compare), but the method is in no way restricted to single items. Open-ended interviews only is wrong because the first worry with open-ended material is whether scorers agree (inter-rater reliability, Section 3.6), and again because of "only". In another version of the question, "there is no opportunity to administer twice" rules test-retest out completely, since the method needs two administrations, and "there is only one rater" is irrelevant: the number of raters concerns scorer reliability, not stability over time (one rater re-scoring the same protocols later gives intra-rater reliability, Section 3.6).

Clinical link

In therapy, change is the point. A client's score on a measure of current anxious or depressive symptoms is supposed to fall if the treatment works, so a low correlation between the scores of a treated group before and after therapy says nothing about the measure's reliability. The clinician's question is a different one: is this client's drop of, say, 8 points larger than the bouncing about that measurement error alone would produce? Answering it needs the standard error of measurement and the standard error of the difference between two scores, which Section 3.7 teaches. A trait measure behaves differently. If a client's score on a measure of a stable personality trait changes sharply over a fortnight, suspicion falls first on the testing (a hurried administration, misunderstood instructions, or a change in how the client wished to appear) before any conclusion is drawn about the person.

How stable are intelligence and personality over a lifetime?

A retest over a few weeks asks a question about the test. A retest over decades asks a question about the person. When the interval is long enough for real change to happen, the correlation between two testings stops being a reliability coefficient in the ordinary sense and becomes a measure of how stable the attribute itself is: how far people keep their places relative to one another as they grow up and grow old. Two lines of research give a sense of the numbers, and they are worth knowing because they show why intelligence and personality are treated as traits.

Key study: the Scottish children of 1932, retested at 77 (Deary and colleagues, 2000)

On 1 June 1932, almost every child born in 1921 and attending school in Scotland sat the same group intelligence test, the Moray House Test, as part of the Scottish Mental Survey. The score records survived. In 1998, Ian Deary, Lawrence Whalley and their colleagues traced 101 of those children, by then 77 years old, and gave them the identical test again; a group of them sat it on 1 June 1998, 66 years to the day after the first sitting. The correlation between the scores at 11 and at 77 was 0.63, and 0.73 after a statistical correction for the fact that the people retested were a less varied group than the whole cohort (restriction of range, Section 3.8). Across almost a whole lifetime, then, the rank order of intelligence held up remarkably well, though not perfectly: there was room for some people to rise and others to fall. The study was published in the journal Intelligence in 2000.

Personality shows a similar but more gradual picture. In 2000 Brent Roberts and Wendy DelVecchio combined 152 longitudinal studies, containing more than 3,000 retest coefficients for personality traits measured several years apart, in a meta-analysis published in Psychological Bulletin. Rank-order consistency rose steadily with age: from about 0.31 in childhood to about 0.54 in the college years, about 0.64 at age 30, and a plateau of about 0.74 between the ages of 50 and 70. Personality, in other words, becomes more stable as people mature. Neither set of figures is a reliability coefficient for any particular test, since each mixes measurement error with genuine change over years. But they tell a test user what to expect over a few weeks: because the traits themselves barely move in so short a time, a good intelligence or personality test should give a high coefficient of stability, and a low one points to the test.

The same retest logic is applied to clinical judgements. A diagnosis can be checked for stability by having patients interviewed twice, a short time apart, and asking how often the second interview gives the same diagnosis; Book 2, Section 2.10.5 describes how the field trials of the diagnostic manuals did this, and Book 3, Section 1.6.6 discusses what the results mean for the reliability of psychiatric diagnosis. Behavioural ratings by observers can be retested in the same way, although for them the first question is usually whether two observers agree (Book 2, Section 2.8.7).

Telling test-retest apart from its neighbours

Because the methods of estimating reliability differ from one another in only one or two ingredients (how many forms, how many occasions, how many scorers, what is correlated with what), they are easy to confuse with each other and with the different kinds of validity. Table 3.7 lists the descriptions you are most likely to find set beside "the same test given twice to the same people", says what each one actually describes, and points to where this book teaches it.

Table 3.7 Test-retest reliability and the methods it is confused with.
DescriptionWhat it isTaught in
Same test, same people, two occasions, scores correlatedTest-retest reliability (coefficient of stability)This section
Correlating two halves of a single testSplit-half reliabilitySection 3.4
How closely the items of one test agree (Cronbach's alpha, KR-20)Internal consistency reliabilitySection 3.5
Two raters scoring the same protocolInter-rater (scorer) reliabilitySection 3.6
Equivalence of two parallel formsAlternate (parallel) forms reliabilitySection 3.3
Correlating the test with a future criterionPredictive validityChapter 4
The test measures what it claims to measureValidity in generalChapter 4
The items fairly sample the domain to be coveredContent validityChapter 4
Scores behave as the theory of the construct predictsConstruct validityChapter 4
Modelling how each item behaves at each level of abilityItem response theoryChapter 2; Section 3.9

Two of these confusions deserve a sentence more. Predictive validity also involves two sets of scores collected at two times, which is why it is such a tempting distractor. But in a predictive validity study the second set of scores comes from a different measure, a criterion such as later examination marks or performance in a job, and the question is whether the test forecasts it. In a retest study the second set comes from the same test, and the question is whether the test agrees with itself. Content and construct validity, for their part, are not about consistency at all. Content validity asks whether a test's items fairly sample the domain it is meant to cover, and construct validity asks whether its scores behave as the theory of the attribute predicts; Chapter 4 teaches both. Item response theory is not a way of retesting but a whole model of how people respond to items, and Section 3.9 shows how it measures precision.

Two final points place test-retest among the other methods. First, it is one of the methods that work for a speed test (a test of easy items under a tight time limit), for which splitting a single timed sitting gives a spuriously high coefficient; Section 3.4 explains why. Second, it is one of the few methods available for a single-item measure, such as a one-question rating of life satisfaction, because there are no other items to compare that one item with. Its blind spot, content sampling error, is the reason the next method was invented.

Exam trap

A test-retest coefficient is most appropriate for assessing stability of scores over time. "Internal consistency of items" is Cronbach's alpha or KR-20 (Section 3.5); "inter-rater agreement" is kappa or the intraclass correlation (Section 3.6); "equivalence of two parallel forms" is the alternate-forms coefficient of equivalence (Section 3.3); "correlating the test with a future criterion" is predictive validity (Chapter 4); and "item response theory" is a model of item responses, not a reliability design. If a stem says "the same test ... the same people ... on two occasions ... correlated", the answer is test-retest reliability, and the coefficient is the coefficient of stability, not of equivalence. Construct and content validity, often offered beside it, concern what a test measures, not whether it measures consistently.

3.3 Alternate and Parallel Forms: EquivalenceLower yield · not asked yet

Komal is a clinical psychologist in the memory clinic of a hospital in Mysuru. A retired schoolteacher in his late sixties has been referred because his family has noticed that he forgets conversations, and among other tasks Komal tests his memory with a list of 15 words that she reads aloud and he then recalls. She will need to test him again in six months to see whether his memory is holding steady or declining. If she reads him the same 15 words, part of whatever he recalls the second time may be memory of the test itself, which is exactly the thing that is not supposed to count. So she needs a second list: different words, but just as hard as the first, measuring the same thing on the same scale, so that a change in his score means a change in the man rather than a change in the list. How such a second version is built, how you check that it really is interchangeable with the first, and what the correlation between the two versions tells you about reliability are the subjects of this section. They lead to a second kind of reliability coefficient, the coefficient of equivalence, and to a third, the strictest of all.

Why one form is not always enough

Section 3.2 ended with the blind spot of the retest design: because the same items are used twice, the luck of which items were chosen is repeated and counted as true score. That is a measurement problem, and it is one reason for building a second form. There are also practical reasons why test users want a second version of a test, and historically these came first. The four commonest are the following.

  • Retesting without memory for the items. When a person must be tested twice, to follow a child's development, a patient's recovery or decline, or the effect of a drug, a second form removes the problem of remembered items (though not, as you will see, every carry-over effect).
  • Measuring change before and after treatment. Outcome research in clinical psychology compares scores before and after therapy, and a client who has seen the items once may answer differently the second time simply because of that first exposure. Alternate forms reduce this source of contamination.
  • Make-up and repeat examinations. A student who missed an examination through illness, or who must sit it again, cannot fairly be given the paper that classmates have already seen and discussed.
  • Security in large-scale testing. When thousands of candidates sit an examination in several sessions or places, a single paper would leak. Indian board examinations, for example, commonly print several sets of each question paper, built to the same pattern of chapters, marks and question types, so that different candidates answer different questions. Each set is meant to be an equivalent form of the others.

In every one of these uses, the second form is valuable only if it is interchangeable with the first: a person's score should not depend on which form he or she happened to receive. How interchangeable is interchangeable enough? Psychometricians answer at two levels of strictness.

Parallel forms in the strict sense, alternate forms in the practical sense

The strict answer comes straight from the classical model of Section 3.1. Two forms are parallel forms in the classical sense when every person has the same true score on both and the two forms have equal error variances. Put more plainly, the two forms measure exactly the same thing, on the same scale, with the same precision; they differ only in the random errors that happen to fall on each. Section 3.1 showed the most important consequence: the correlation between two truly parallel forms equals the reliability of either one. That is why giving two parallel forms and correlating them is a legitimate way to estimate reliability: in classical theory it is the most direct estimate there is.

The definition sounds abstract, because nobody can see true scores. But it has consequences that can be checked with ordinary statistics. If two forms give everyone the same true score, they must have the same mean in any group of people. If they also have equal error variances, they must have the same standard deviation, since true variance plus an equal amount of error variance gives an equal observed variance. And because both forms carry the same true score with the same amount of error, each must correlate equally with any third measure, whether another test or a criterion such as examination marks. Harold Gulliksen (1903 to 1996), in his Theory of Mental Tests (1950), turned these consequences into a statistical criterion for parallel tests: equal means, equal variances and equal intercorrelations, checked with significance tests so that the small differences that always arise from sampling are not mistaken for real ones. He also insisted on a psychological criterion: the forms must contain items dealing with the same subject matter, in the same format and with the same kind of instructions, so that they are parallel as far as psychological judgment can tell. Statistics can tell you that two forms behave alike; only their content can tell you that they behave alike for the right reason.

Strict parallelism is an ideal that real forms can only approach, and psychometricians later named some looser relationships that are useful in their own right. In Statistical Theories of Mental Test Scores (1968), Frederic Lord and Melvin Novick described tau-equivalent forms (tau, τ, is the Greek letter used for the true score), which give every person the same true score but may differ in their error variances, so that one form is noisier than the other; and essentially tau-equivalent forms, whose true scores differ only by a constant, as if one form simply added two points to everyone's score. In 1971 Karl Jöreskog named the loosest useful relationship: congeneric forms measure the same attribute but may do so on different scales and with different precision. These distinctions matter most for coefficient alpha, which assumes that the items are at least essentially tau-equivalent, as Section 3.5 explains. For a test developer building two forms, the practical message is simpler: aim at strict parallelism, check how close you came, and report it.

In everyday use a looser standard applies. Two versions of a test built independently from the same blueprint are called alternate forms (also equivalent forms or comparable forms). The blueprint is the table of specifications, which fixes the content areas and the proportion of items in each, and the forms also share the number of items, the item formats, the range and spread of difficulty, the instructions, the time limit and the scoring. Alternate forms aim at parallelism without claiming to have reached it. Most writers use "parallel forms reliability", "alternate forms reliability" and "equivalent forms reliability" interchangeably for the method of correlating two forms, and you may do the same, as long as you remember that "parallel" in its strict sense is the stronger claim. A. K. Singh's textbook, for example, reproduces the practical criteria for equivalent forms listed by Frank S. Freeman (1898 to 1986) in his Theory and Practice of Psychological Testing (third edition, 1962). Table 3.8 summarises them beside Gulliksen's statistical checks.

Table 3.8 What makes two forms parallel: the classical definition and the practical criteria for alternate forms (after Gulliksen, 1950, and Freeman, 1962).
RequirementStrictly parallel formsAlternate (equivalent) forms
True scoresIdentical for every personIntended to be the same
Error varianceEqualSimilar
Means and standard deviationsEqualEqual or nearly so
ContentSame subject matter, same item formatSame blueprint: content areas and proportions
ItemsDifferent items, same propertiesSame number, format, difficulty range and spread, and homogeneity
AdministrationIdenticalSame instructions, time limit and mode
Correlation with other measuresEqualSimilar
Exam trap

Parallel forms must match in means, variances (standard deviations), content and format, and they should correlate equally with other measures. Covering the same topic is not enough: two forms with the same content but different difficulty will give different means and may rank people differently. Nor are two forms parallel just because they correlate highly with each other. Two forms that correlate 0.90 but differ by five points in their means are closely related tests, not interchangeable ones: a person's score would still depend on which form was taken.

Building and checking two forms

How does a test developer actually produce two forms that come close to the ideal? The standard method starts with more items than either form needs. Suppose Akash, who sets examinations for a coaching institute, wants two 30-item forms of an arithmetic reasoning test for Class 8 pupils. He writes an item pool of about 80 items to a single blueprint, tries them out on a sample of pupils, and runs an item analysis (Chapter 2): for each item he finds its difficulty (the proportion of pupils who answer it correctly) and its discrimination (how well it separates pupils who do well on the test as a whole from those who do badly). He discards the weak items and then pairs the survivors: two fraction items of about the same difficulty and discrimination, two percentage items, two time-and-distance items, and so on. Within each matched pair, a coin toss decides which item goes to Form A and which to Form B. The result is two forms that match item for item in content, difficulty and discrimination, which is about as close to parallel as careful construction can get.

Construction is a promise; the data are the proof. Akash gives both forms to 120 pupils at a single sitting and compares them. Form A has a mean of 18.4 and a standard deviation of 5.0; Form B has a mean of 18.1 and a standard deviation of 5.2; and the two forms correlate 0.86. Is that close enough? The means differ by 18.4 − 18.1 = 0.3 of a mark, which is 0.3 / 5.0 = 0.06 of a standard deviation, far too small to matter for any practical decision. The standard deviations differ by only 0.2, so the spreads are almost the same. And the correlation of 0.86 says that the two forms rank the pupils in very nearly the same order. Had the means differed by 3 marks, Form B would have been clearly harder, and a pupil's mark would have depended partly on the luck of which form he or she received. With significance tests (Gulliksen's statistical criterion; the Research Methodology book teaches the tests themselves), Akash can check formally whether differences as small as his could be accidents of sampling.

What if two forms turn out not quite equal? Rather than throw one away, test developers often turn to equating: converting scores on one form to the scale of the other, so that a converted score means the same level of performance whichever form was taken. Two methods are common. In linear equating, a score on Form B is converted to the Form A score that lies the same number of standard deviations from the Form A mean. In words: find how far the Form B score is from the Form B mean, counted in Form B standard deviations; then go the same distance from the Form A mean, counted in Form A standard deviations. As a formula:

Equated Form A score = MA + (SDA / SDB) × (XB − MB)

Here XB is the score obtained on Form B, MA and MB are the means of the two forms, and SDA and SDB are their standard deviations, all found in the same group of people or in groups known to be equivalent. Suppose two forms of a final examination came out with a Form A mean of 30 and standard deviation of 8, and a Form B mean of 26 and standard deviation of 6, so that Form B was harder and its scores less spread out. A student who scored 32 on Form B stood (32 − 26) / 6 = 6 / 6 = 1.0 standard deviation above the Form B mean. The Form A score that stands 1.0 standard deviation above the Form A mean is 30 + 1.0 × 8 = 38. So 32 on the harder Form B is worth 38 on Form A. In equipercentile equating, scores with the same percentile rank on the two forms are treated as equal (if 32 on Form B and 38 on Form A both have a percentile rank of 84, they are declared equivalent), which also corrects for differences in the shapes of the two score distributions. Percentile ranks and standard scores are taught in Chapter 5, and large testing programmes use more elaborate designs. Notice, though, what equating can and cannot do. It can remove a difference in difficulty and spread. It cannot remove a difference in what the two forms measure, or in how reliably they measure it; those have to be got right when the items are written.

Indian context

Large Indian entrance examinations meet the alternate-forms problem on a vast scale. When the National Testing Agency (NTA) holds the Joint Entrance Examination (JEE) Main in several shifts over several days, each shift answers a different question paper, and no team of paper setters can make the papers exactly equal in difficulty. The NTA's answer is a normalisation procedure based on percentile scores. Each candidate's raw marks are converted into a percentile within that candidate's own shift (the percentage of candidates in the shift who scored at or below that candidate), and candidates are then compared on these percentiles rather than on raw marks. The logic is that of equipercentile equating, and it rests on one assumption: that the candidates allotted to the different shifts are, on average, equally able, so that a candidate at the 95th percentile of a hard shift has done as well as one at the 95th percentile of an easy shift.

The coefficient of equivalence and the coefficient of stability and equivalence

Once two forms exist, there are two ways to give them, and the choice changes what the resulting correlation means. Figure 3.6 sets the two designs beside the test-retest design of Section 3.2.

In the first alternate-forms design, the same group takes Form A and then Form B at a single sitting, one straight after the other, perhaps with a short break. The correlation between the two forms is the coefficient of equivalence. Because both forms are taken on the same occasion, the person's state, the hall and the day are the same for both, so time sampling error has almost no chance to appear. What does differ between the forms is the items. A pupil who happens to find the Form A fraction items easy and the Form B fraction items awkward will score differently on the two forms, and that difference lowers the correlation. The coefficient of equivalence therefore measures content sampling error, the very error that the retest design could not see. Akash's 0.86 is a coefficient of equivalence: it says that about 86 percent of the score differences among his pupils would survive a change of items, and about 14 percent depend on which particular items each form happened to contain.

In the second design, the group takes Form A on one occasion and Form B after an interval, perhaps a few weeks. The correlation is the coefficient of stability and equivalence, sometimes called delayed alternate-forms reliability. Now both kinds of error have a chance to lower the correlation: the items differ (content sampling) and so does the occasion (time sampling). A person can score differently because the second form suits them less well, or because they are tired on the second day, or both. Because this design lets in more sources of error than any other classical design, it is the most demanding test of a score's consistency, and it usually gives the lowest coefficient of all the classical methods. It is also the most realistic estimate whenever scores will be compared across occasions and forms, which is exactly how tests like Komal's memory lists are used: the patient is tested on different days with different versions.

Giving two forms in sequence creates an order problem. Suppose every pupil took Form A first and Form B second. Any fatigue, boredom or warming up produced by the first form would fall entirely on the second, and a difference between the forms' means would be impossible to interpret: is Form B harder, or were the pupils simply tired by the time they reached it? The standard remedy is counterbalancing: half the group, chosen at random, takes Form A first and Form B second, and the other half takes Form B first and Form A second. Order effects then fall equally on both forms, and the two influences can be separated. A small example shows how. Suppose Form B is really 1 mark harder than Form A, and tiredness lowers whichever form comes second by another mark. Without counterbalancing, pupils taking A first and B second would score, say, 20 on A and 18 on B, and Form B would look 2 marks harder when it is really only 1 mark harder. With counterbalancing, the other half (B first) would score 19 on B (1 mark for difficulty, no tiredness) and 19 on A (no difficulty penalty, 1 mark of tiredness). Averaging over both halves, Form A's mean is (20 + 19) / 2 = 19.5 and Form B's is (18 + 19) / 2 = 18.5: a difference of exactly 1 mark, the true difference in difficulty. And comparing each form taken first with the same form taken second (20 against 19 for A, 19 against 18 for B) recovers the 1-mark order effect. Counterbalancing does not abolish order effects. It spreads them evenly, so that they cannot masquerade as a difference between the forms.

Having coefficients from more than one design also allows a rough piece of book-keeping on where the error comes from. In a second sample of Class 8 pupils, Akash gives Form A, waits six weeks, and then gives Form B: this time the forms correlate 0.76, the coefficient of stability and equivalence. Treat each coefficient, as Section 3.1 taught, as the proportion of score variance that is consistent, so that one minus the coefficient is the proportion that is error. Then:

  • Error from content sampling alone (two forms, one sitting) is about 1 − 0.86 = 0.14, or 14 percent of the variance.
  • Error from content and time sampling together (two forms, two occasions) is about 1 − 0.76 = 0.24, or 24 percent.
  • So the share due to time sampling is about 0.24 − 0.14 = 0.10, or 10 percent, which is the same as 0.86 − 0.76.

Of the 24 percent of the variance that is error when pupils are compared across both forms and occasions, about 14 points come from the items and about 10 from the occasion. This is only a rough accounting: it assumes that the error sources simply add up and that the two samples of pupils are comparable. Generalizability theory (Section 3.9) does the same job properly, estimating every source of error at once with analysis of variance. But the rough version already shows why the coefficient of stability and equivalence must be the lowest: it is the only classical design that charges the test for both kinds of error at once.

Remember

Error variance from content sampling ≈ 1 − req
Error variance from content and time sampling together ≈ 1 − rse
Error variance from time sampling ≈ req − rse

Here req is the coefficient of equivalence (two forms, one sitting) and rse is the coefficient of stability and equivalence (two forms, two occasions), each read as a proportion of variance. Because more error enters the second design, rse is normally the lower of the two.

Exam trap

Match each design to its coefficient and its error. Same form, two occasions: coefficient of stability; time sampling error. Two forms, one sitting: coefficient of equivalence; content sampling error. Two forms, two occasions: coefficient of stability and equivalence; both errors, so usually the lowest estimate. A stem that speaks of "the equivalence of two parallel forms" is describing alternate-forms reliability, not test-retest; a stem that describes "the same scale given again two weeks later" is test-retest, not parallel forms, because no second form is involved.

Strengths, weaknesses and tests published in alternate forms

The great strength of alternate forms is that they remove the most damaging carry-over effect of a retest, memory for particular items, while still allowing a person to be tested more than once. They let the developer estimate content sampling error directly. A second form is also useful in itself, for retesting, make-up examinations and test security, quite apart from any reliability study. And alternate forms are especially valuable for speed tests, for which splitting a single timed sitting gives a misleadingly high coefficient (Section 3.4).

The weaknesses are just as real. Building a genuinely parallel second form roughly doubles the work of test construction, since every item must in effect be written, tried out and analysed twice, and even then complete equivalence is rarely, if ever, achieved. Practice effects are reduced but not removed: a person who has done one form has learned the instructions, the format, the pace and often the strategy that the items call for, and that learning carries over to the other form. A child who has once worked out how matrix puzzles "work" is better at the next one, whatever its particular pattern. When the two forms are given at one sitting, fatigue and boredom can lower scores on whichever form comes second, which is why counterbalancing matters. And for some kinds of test, true alternate forms are almost impossible to build: a short test whose few items are each unique in what they demand cannot easily be duplicated. Table 3.9 summarises the comparison with the retest method.

Table 3.9 Test-retest and the two alternate-forms designs compared.
FeatureTest-retestTwo forms, one sittingTwo forms, two occasions
Forms neededOneTwoTwo
Sessions neededTwoOneTwo
CoefficientStabilityEquivalenceStability and equivalence
Error detectedTime samplingContent samplingBoth
Memory for itemsA serious problemNot a problemNot a problem
Other carry-overPractice on the taskWarm-up and fatigue (counterbalance)Practice on the task, reduced
Main costTwo sessions; drop-outsWriting two formsBoth

Many well-known tests have been published in alternate forms, and you will meet several of them in later chapters. Table 3.10 lists some examples. Two patterns stand out. Alternate forms are commonest in tests that are meant to be repeated, such as memory tests, cognitive screening tests and vocabulary tests used to follow children's development, because that is where memory for the items does most harm. And some tests have abandoned or neglected their second form, which shows how costly alternate forms are to maintain: the two forms of the 1937 Stanford-Binet were merged into a single form in 1960, and the second form of the original Wechsler Memory Scale was rarely used.

Table 3.10 Some tests published in alternate forms.
TestFormsNote
Stanford-Binet, 1937 revision (Lewis Terman and Maud Merrill)Forms L and MMerged into a single Form L-M in 1960 (Chapter 6)
Wechsler Memory Scale (David Wechsler, 1945)Form I; Form II (1946)Form II rarely used (Chapter 11)
Peabody Picture Vocabulary Test (Lloyd Dunn, with Leota Dunn and later Douglas Dunn)Forms A and B (1959); L and M (1981 revision); IIIA and IIIB (1997); A and B (2007)Receptive vocabulary; retesting children
Raven's Standard Progressive MatricesClassic (1938) and Parallel (1998) versionsNonverbal reasoning (Chapter 6)
Benton Visual Retention Test (Arthur Benton)Three forms, C, D and EVisual memory, repeated assessment (Chapter 11)
Holtzman Inkblot Technique (Wayne Holtzman, 1961)Forms A and B, 45 cards eachAn inkblot test built for reliability (Chapter 10)
Eysenck Personality Inventory (Hans and Sybil Eysenck, 1964)Forms A and BRetesting, for example before and after treatment (Chapter 9)
Montreal Cognitive Assessment (Ziad Nasreddine and colleagues, 2005)Original version 7.1; alternates 7.2 and 7.3Repeated cognitive screening (Chapter 11)
Clinical link

Komal's memory clinic is exactly where alternate forms earn their cost. When a patient is assessed repeatedly (before and after surgery for epilepsy, during treatment with a drug, or every six months to follow a possible dementia), alternate forms protect the scores from remembered items. But they do not make repeated testing free of practice. Patients still become familiar with the tasks, the room and the examiner, and many show small gains on retesting even with new items, gains that can hide a real decline. Good practice is to use alternate forms where they exist, to know the size of the practice effect reported for the test, and to judge whether a change is real with the standard error of the difference between two scores (Section 3.7), not by eye.

From two forms to two halves

Alternate forms solve the content sampling problem, but at a price: two forms to build and, usually, two administrations to arrange. Can the same information be had more cheaply? Look again at Akash's construction method. He wrote matched pairs of items and sent one member of each pair to Form A and the other to Form B. Suppose that, instead of printing two booklets, he printed all 60 items in one booklet with the two members of each pair next to each other, gave it once, and scored the odd-numbered and the even-numbered items separately. Each half would contain one member of every pair. He would have two short "forms" from a single sitting. That is the idea of split-half reliability: one administration, two halves treated as if they were miniature parallel forms. It inherits the coefficient of equivalence's strength (it detects content sampling error) and its weakness (it says nothing about stability over time), and it brings a new problem of its own. Each half is only half as long as the real test, and a shorter test is a less reliable one, so the correlation between the halves underestimates the reliability of the whole. Section 3.4 explains how Charles Spearman and William Brown corrected for that in 1910.

Exam trap

Do not confuse parallel forms reliability with split-half reliability. Parallel (alternate) forms reliability correlates two complete, separately built versions of a test, given as two administrations, at one sitting or with an interval. Split-half reliability correlates two halves of a single form from a single administration, and the half-test correlation must then be stepped up with the Spearman-Brown formula because each half is short. The two halves are a miniature version of parallel forms, which is why the methods resemble each other; but an option describing "two forms of a test given to the same group" is parallel forms, and one describing "correlating two halves of a single test" is split-half. Neither is Cronbach's alpha, which in effect averages over all the possible ways of splitting the items into halves and is an index of internal consistency (Section 3.5).

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The correlation between scores on the same test given to the same people on two occasions is called the coefficient of .
  2. The test-retest design detects sampling error, which arises from differences between occasions.
  3. Because the same items are used both times, test-retest reliability cannot detect sampling error.
  4. Too short an interval between testings the test-retest coefficient, because memory and practice carry over.
  5. Test-retest reliability is appropriate for stable , such as intelligence and personality, but not for rapidly changing states such as current mood.
  6. If every person's score rises by exactly 4 points on the retest, the coefficient of stability changes by .
  7. Two alternate forms given at a single sitting yield the coefficient of .
  8. Giving half the group Form A first and the other half Form B first is called .
  9. Form A has a mean of 50 and a standard deviation of 10; Form B has a mean of 45 and a standard deviation of 5. By linear equating, a score of 55 on Form B is equivalent to on Form A.
  10. Two forms correlate 0.85 when given at one sitting and 0.75 when given six weeks apart. The share of score variance due to time sampling error is roughly .

Answers: (1) stability, (2) time, (3) content (item), (4) inflates (raises), (5) traits, (6) nothing, zero (a change that is the same for everyone leaves a correlation unchanged), (7) equivalence, (8) counterbalancing, (9) 70 (the Form B score is (55 − 45) / 5 = 2 standard deviations above the Form B mean, and 50 + 2 × 10 = 70), (10) 0.10, or 10 percent (content sampling error is 1 − 0.85 = 0.15, content and time together are 1 − 0.75 = 0.25, so time sampling is 0.25 − 0.15 = 0.10).

3.4 Split-Half Reliability and the Spearman-Brown Prophecy FormulaHigh yield · 16 Qs

The two methods you met in Sections 3.2 and 3.3 share an awkward requirement: the same people must be tested twice. Test-retest reliability needs a second session days or weeks after the first, and alternate forms need a second form that somebody has had to write, try out and match to the first. Both cost time and money. Both lose people who do not turn up the second time. And both leave room for things to happen in between: students learn, patients recover, and memories of the first sitting linger. So test developers have long asked a tempting question. Can we estimate reliability from a single sitting, with a single form? The first answer, and still one of the most examined ideas in psychometrics, was to cut the test in two. This section explains how to cut it, why the correlation between the two halves must then be corrected with the Spearman-Brown prophecy formula, how the same formula predicts what happens when any test is made longer or shorter, which formulas avoid the correction altogether, and the one kind of test for which cutting in two gives a badly wrong answer.

One sitting, two halves

The logic of split-half reliability is simple. Give the test once. Then, instead of scoring each person once, score each person twice: once on one half of the items and once on the other half. Every person now has two scores, and the two sets of scores are correlated across the whole group. (The correlation coefficient, Pearson's r, is a number between −1 and +1 that shows how closely two sets of scores rise and fall together; the Research Methodology book teaches it in full.) If the two halves measure the same thing, people who do well on one half will do well on the other, and the correlation will be high.

Look at what this design really does. The two halves are, in effect, two short alternate forms of the test, printed in one booklet and taken at one sitting. In the language of Section 3.3, a split-half correlation is a coefficient of equivalence for two miniature forms given back to back. Because both halves are taken at the same time, nothing can change between them: there is no interval in which to learn, forget, recover or fall ill, so time-sampling error cannot enter the estimate. What does differ between the halves is the particular items each one contains. A student who happens to know the words that landed in one half but not those in the other will score unevenly, and that is exactly the content-sampling (item-sampling) error of Section 3.1. Split-half reliability therefore tells you how consistently a test measures across different samples of items. That is why it is counted as the oldest member of the family of internal consistency methods that Section 3.5 completes.

Rohini, whose Verbal Reasoning Test (VRT) you have followed since Section 3.1, did exactly this with her 40-item test. After her 200 Class 12 students had taken it, she scored each answer sheet twice: once for the 20 odd-numbered items (items 1, 3, 5 and so on up to 39) and once for the 20 even-numbered items (2, 4, 6 and so on up to 40). The odd-item scores and the even-item scores correlated 0.70. Hold on to that number. It is not yet the reliability of her test, and the next three subsections explain why not and what to do about it.

The attractions of the method are plain. It needs one administration, one form and no second appointment. Nobody can remember answers from an earlier sitting, because there is no earlier sitting. Nobody drops out between sessions, and the conditions of testing (the hall, the noise, the invigilator, the time of day) are identical for both halves. For a developer like Rohini, who had to travel to four colleges to test her students, these are not small advantages. But the method's simplicity hides a decision that is not simple at all: which items go into which half?

Dividing the test: first and second halves, odd-even and matched halves

The most obvious way to divide a 40-item test is to put items 1 to 20 in one half and items 21 to 40 in the other. This first-half and second-half split is almost always a bad idea, for four reasons. First, most ability tests arrange their items from easy to hard, so the first half would be the easy half and the second half the hard one; the halves would have different means and different spreads, and would even tap slightly different levels of the ability. Second, people change during a sitting. Some need a few items to warm up and settle their nerves, and most grow tired, bored or careless towards the end, so the second half catches more fatigue than the first. Third, if time is tight, some people never reach the last items at all, and their second-half score partly measures speed rather than ability. Fourth, many tests group their items by content (all the analogies together, then all the vocabulary items), so the two halves would sample different content. Each of these effects pushes a person's two half-scores apart for reasons that have nothing to do with how consistent the items are, and the correlation comes out too low.

The standard remedy is the odd-even split: the odd-numbered items form one half and the even-numbered items the other. Because the items alternate, each half receives an easy item for every easy item in the other half, an early item for every early item and a late item for every late item. Warm-up, fatigue and rising difficulty are now shared almost equally between the halves, so they no longer drive the two scores apart. This is why the odd-even split is the one textbooks recommend and the one used most often. One practical refinement matters: items that belong together, such as five questions on the same reading passage or several items built on one diagram, should go into the same half as a block. Such items share something (familiarity with that passage, say) that would otherwise inflate the correlation between the halves.

The most careful developers go a step further and build matched random halves. They first use the item statistics from a tryout, chiefly each item's difficulty (the proportion of people who pass it) and its discrimination (how well it separates high scorers from low scorers; Chapter 2 explains both), to arrange the items into pairs that are as alike as possible in difficulty and content. Then, for each pair, chance (a coin, or a table of random numbers) decides which member goes to half A and which to half B. The result is two halves matched in difficulty and content by design, not by the accident of how the items happened to be numbered. Figure 3.7 shows the three kinds of split side by side, and Table 3.11 sums up their strengths and weaknesses.

Table 3.11 Three ways of splitting a test into halves.
SplitHow the halves are formedStrengthWeakness
First half and second halfItems 1 to 20 against items 21 to 40Quick to scoreHalves differ in difficulty, warm-up, fatigue, speed and content, so the correlation is too low
Odd-evenOdd-numbered items against even-numbered itemsSpreads difficulty and position evenly; the usual choiceSpuriously high on speed tests; items sharing a passage must be kept together
Matched random halvesItems paired on difficulty and content; chance sends one of each pair to each halfThe most nearly equivalent halvesNeeds item statistics from a tryout; still only one of many possible splits

Notice the last entry in the table. Even the most carefully matched split is only one of a very large number of possible splits. A test of just 10 items can be divided into two halves of five items in 126 different ways, and each way gives a slightly different correlation. Which one is "the" split-half reliability? There is no single answer. This arbitrariness is the weakness that led G. Frederic Kuder, Marion Richardson and later Lee Cronbach to the coefficients of Section 3.5, which in effect average over every possible split. For now the practical lesson is the one examiners like to test: the hard part of split-half reliability is not the arithmetic but the division of the test into two halves that are nearly equivalent.

Exam trap

The major challenge of split-half reliability is dividing the test into two nearly equivalent halves. Computing the Spearman-Brown step-up is one line of arithmetic, so that option is bait. Giving the same test twice with an interval is the burden of test-retest reliability (Section 3.2), and giving two forms is the burden of alternate forms (Section 3.3); split-half was invented precisely to avoid both. And remember which split is the poor one: first half against second half. Odd-even is the usual split.

Why half a test underestimates the whole

Rohini's halves correlate 0.70. Why can she not simply report 0.70 as the reliability of the VRT? Because 0.70 describes the consistency of two 20-item tests, and the test her students will actually take has 40 items. Common sense already tells you that a longer test is a more dependable measure than a shorter one. Imagine judging a batsman's form from a single ball: one lucky edge or one unplayable delivery decides everything. Judge him over an over and the luck starts to even out; judge him over a whole innings and you learn a great deal about his real ability. A test item is one ball. Every item carries a little of the person's true ability and a little luck: a word they happened to read last week, a careless slip, a lucky guess. The more items you add up, the more the luck cancels out and the more the true ability shows through. This is the domain-sampling idea of Section 3.1: a longer test is a bigger sample from the universe of possible items, so it represents a person's standing in that universe more faithfully.

You can see exactly how much a test gains from being doubled if you follow the true part and the error part of the score separately, using the model of Section 3.1: observed score = true score + error (X = T + E). Suppose each half of a test has an observed-score variance of 6, of which 3.6 is true-score variance and 2.4 is error variance. (Variance is the average squared distance of the scores from their mean, a measure of how spread out they are; the Research Methodology book teaches it in full.) The reliability of each half is the share of its variance that is true: 3.6 / 6 = 0.60. Because the two halves are parallel, this is also the correlation you would find between them; Section 3.1 explains why the correlation between two parallel tests equals the reliability of each.

Now add the two halves together to make the full test, and watch what happens to each part of the score.

  • The true parts add up in step. Each person has the same true score on both halves, because parallel halves measure the same thing equally well. So the true part of the full score is simply twice the true part of one half. Doubling every number in a set doubles every distance from the mean, and since variance is built from squared distances, doubling multiplies the variance by four (2 × 2): 4 × 3.6 = 14.4.
  • The error parts only partly add up. The errors on the two halves are independent: being lucky on half A makes you no more likely to be lucky on half B. For some people the two errors point the same way; for others they point in opposite directions and cancel. When two independent quantities are added, their variances simply add: 2.4 + 2.4 = 4.8. That is only twice the error variance of one half, not four times.

The full test therefore has a true variance of 14.4, an error variance of 4.8 and an observed variance of 14.4 + 4.8 = 19.2. Its reliability is 14.4 / 19.2 = 0.75. Doubling the test has raised its reliability from 0.60 to 0.75, and the reason is now in plain view: when a test is lengthened, its true variance grows faster (here four times) than its error variance (here two times), so the true share of the total rises. Keep these numbers in mind (half variances of 6, a full-test variance of 19.2 and an error variance of 4.8); you will meet them again in Hrithik's data later in this section.

The Spearman-Brown prophecy formula

The formula that turns a half-test correlation into a whole-test reliability was published in 1910, in the same issue of the British Journal of Psychology (volume 3), by two men who reached it separately. One was Charles Spearman, whose true-score model you met in Section 3.1 and whose theory of general intelligence, g, is told in Book 1, Section 9.4. His paper, "Correlation calculated from faulty data", continued the project he had begun in 1904: working out how errors in psychological measurements (the "faulty data" of his title) distort the correlations between them. The other was William Brown (1881 to 1952), a young British psychologist whose paper, "Some experimental results in the correlation of mental abilities", grew out of his doctoral research on the use of correlation in psychology. Brown was no disciple of Spearman. His doctoral thesis criticised parts of Spearman's 1904 work on correlation, Spearman's 1910 paper in turn criticised Brown's, and for some years Brown questioned whether Spearman's evidence really proved a general factor. Historians describe the two as rivals rather than partners, even though their papers sit back to back in the journal and the formula carries both their names. Brown went on to a distinguished career at Oxford, where he was Wilde Reader in Mental Philosophy from 1921 and became the first director of the university's Institute of Experimental Psychology when it opened in 1936. The word "prophecy" in the formula's usual name describes what it does: it foretells the reliability of a test that nobody has yet given, whether that is the full-length test made by putting two halves together or a test lengthened or shortened by any amount.

For the split-half case the Spearman-Brown prophecy formula is short. In words: the reliability of the whole test equals twice the correlation between the halves, divided by one plus that correlation. Using it is called stepping up the half-test correlation, and the result is often reported as the corrected or stepped-up split-half coefficient.

Remember

The Spearman-Brown formula for split halves:

rfull = 2rhalf / (1 + rhalf)

where rhalf is the correlation between the two half-test scores (the reliability of a test half as long) and rfull is the estimated reliability of the whole, full-length test.

Rohini's VRT shows the formula at work. Her odd and even halves correlate 0.70, so rhalf = 0.70.

  • Step 1, the top line: 2 × 0.70 = 1.40.
  • Step 2, the bottom line: 1 + 0.70 = 1.70.
  • Step 3, divide: 1.40 / 1.70 = 0.8235, which rounds to 0.82 at two decimal places.

So the split-half reliability of the 40-item VRT is 0.82. Read it as Section 3.1 taught you to read any reliability coefficient, directly and without squaring: about 82 percent of the variance in students' total scores reflects true differences in verbal reasoning, as sampled by items of this kind, and about 18 percent reflects the luck of which items a student happened to meet.

The commonest examination version uses halves that correlate 0.60. Step 1: 2 × 0.60 = 1.20. Step 2: 1 + 0.60 = 1.60. Step 3: 1.20 / 1.60 = 0.75. This is exactly the 0.75 you reached in the last subsection by adding up true and error variances, which is reassuring: the formula is nothing more than that bookkeeping done in one line. Running a few more values through it shows a pattern. Halves correlating 0.30 step up to 0.46, 0.50 to 0.67, 0.60 to 0.75, 0.70 to 0.82, 0.80 to 0.89 and 0.90 to 0.95. The stepped-up value is always higher than the half-test correlation (unless that correlation is exactly 0 or 1), it never exceeds 1, and the boost is largest for middling correlations and smallest for those already near 1.

Exam trap

"Odd and even halves correlate .60; the full-test reliability is?" The answer is .75 (1.20 / 1.60). The other options are traps with a story: .60 forgets that the half correlation describes a half-length test; .30 halves it instead of stepping it up; .36 squares it (squaring belongs to ordinary correlations, never to reliability coefficients, Section 3.1); .82 is the step-up of .70, not .60; and .80 has no route to it at all. Quick check: the stepped-up value must lie between the half correlation and 1.

Making a test longer or shorter

Spearman and Brown's insight works for any change of length, not just for doubling. Call the change the length factor, n: the new number of items divided by the present number of items. A test lengthened from 20 to 60 items has n = 60 / 20 = 3; a test cut from 40 to 20 items has n = 20 / 40 = 0.5. Then the general form of the formula predicts the reliability of the new test from the reliability of the present one.

Remember

The general Spearman-Brown formula:

rnew = n r / [1 + (n − 1) r]

where r is the reliability of the present test, n is the length factor (new length divided by present length; many books call it k) and rnew is the predicted reliability of the lengthened or shortened test. With n = 2 it becomes the split-half formula, 2r / (1 + r). The added or removed items must be parallel to the existing ones.

Where does this come from? Exactly the bookkeeping of the doubled test. Make a test n times as long with parallel items and its true variance is multiplied by n × n, because the true parts add up in step, while its error variance is multiplied only by n, because independent errors partly cancel. If you write the new reliability as new true variance over new total variance and divide the top and bottom by the old total variance, the formula above drops out. You do not need to reproduce that algebra in an examination, but you should be able to say in words why lengthening helps: true score accumulates faster than error.

Take a test with a reliability of 0.50 that is made three times as long with parallel items, so n = 3.

  • Step 1, the top line: n × r = 3 × 0.50 = 1.50.
  • Step 2, the bottom line: (n − 1) × r = 2 × 0.50 = 1.00, and 1 + 1.00 = 2.00.
  • Step 3, divide: 1.50 / 2.00 = 0.75.

Tripling the test raises its reliability from 0.50 to 0.75. Notice what it does not do: it does not triple the reliability. Three times 0.50 would be 1.50, an impossible value for a coefficient that cannot exceed 1, and an option of 1.00 in a multiple-choice question is there to catch exactly that straight multiplication. An option of 0.83 is the answer to a different question: it is what five times the length would give (2.50 / 3.00). An option of 0.60 credits the tripled test with too small a gain: it is less than merely doubling the test would give (1.00 / 1.50 = 0.67).

The formula works just as well in reverse, and here it gives a warning. Devansh works for a test publisher in Delhi and has been asked to produce a 20-item short form of a 60-item vocabulary test whose reliability is 0.90, because schools complain that the long version takes too much time. The length factor is n = 20 / 60, one third (0.333).

  • Step 1: n × r = (1/3) × 0.90 = 0.30.
  • Step 2: n − 1 = −2/3, so (n − 1) × r = (−2/3) × 0.90 = −0.60, and 1 + (−0.60) = 0.40.
  • Step 3: 0.30 / 0.40 = 0.75.

Cutting the test to a third of its length drops its predicted reliability from 0.90 to 0.75. That may be acceptable for comparing classrooms in a research project, but, as Section 3.8 explains, it is too low for decisions about a single child. The commonest examination version is the half-length short form: a test with a reliability of 0.80 cut to half its length, so n = 0.5. Step 1: 0.5 × 0.80 = 0.40. Step 2: (0.5 − 1) × 0.80 = −0.40, and 1 − 0.40 = 0.60. Step 3: 0.40 / 0.60 = 0.667, or 0.67. You can check it by running it backwards: stepping 0.667 up with n = 2 gives 1.333 / 1.667 = 0.80, the reliability you started with. The tempting wrong answers are 0.40 (simply halving the coefficient), 0.89 (doubling the test instead of halving it) and 0.80 (assuming that length makes no difference).

Clinical link

Clinicians are always short of time, and short forms are everywhere: brief versions of long personality inventories (Chapter 9), short forms of the Wechsler intelligence scales built from a few subtests (Chapter 6), one-page screening questionnaires. The Spearman-Brown formula tells you the price before you pay it. A lower reliability means a wider band of error around each person's score (Section 3.7 shows how to compute it), so a short form can screen a group but should not, on its own, decide a diagnosis or a placement. Lengthening has a price too: a 200-item questionnaire may tire a depressed or elderly patient so badly that the last items are answered carelessly, which breaks the formula's assumption that every added item is answered as well as the first ones.

Figure 3.8 plots the general formula for three tests whose present reliabilities are 0.30, 0.50 and 0.70, for lengths from half the present length up to six times it. Every point on the curves was computed from the formula. The curves teach the most important practical lesson of this section: diminishing returns. Follow the middle curve, which starts at 0.50. Doubling the test lifts it to 0.67, a gain of 0.17. Tripling lifts it to 0.75, only 0.08 more. Four times the length gives 0.80, five times 0.83 and six times 0.86, each step adding less than the one before. Reliability creeps towards 1 but reaches it only when the test becomes infinitely long. The left end of the curves shows the reverse: halving the test that starts at 0.70 drops it to 0.54.

How many items are needed? Solving for the length

Test developers usually meet the formula the other way round. They know the reliability of their tryout version and the reliability they want, and they need to know how much longer the test must become. Rearranging the general formula to solve for n gives the answer directly.

Remember

The length needed to reach a target reliability:

n = rtarget (1 − r) / [r (1 − rtarget)]

where r is the present reliability, rtarget is the reliability you want and n is how many times longer the test must be (with parallel items). Multiply n by the present number of items to get the number of items needed, and always round up.

The classic problem: a test has a reliability of 0.60; how many times longer must it be made to reach 0.90?

  • Step 1, the top line: rtarget × (1 − r) = 0.90 × (1 − 0.60) = 0.90 × 0.40 = 0.36.
  • Step 2, the bottom line: r × (1 − rtarget) = 0.60 × (1 − 0.90) = 0.60 × 0.10 = 0.06.
  • Step 3, divide: 0.36 / 0.06 = 6.

The test must be made six times as long. Always check by putting the answer back into the general formula: 6 × 0.60 = 3.60 on top; 1 + (5 × 0.60) = 1 + 3.00 = 4.00 underneath; 3.60 / 4.00 = 0.90. If the original test had 20 items, the new one needs 6 × 20 = 120 items, that is, 100 new items all parallel to the first 20. The wrong options in such a question are worth working too: three times the length gives only 1.80 / 2.20 = 0.82, one and a half times gives 0.90 / 1.30 = 0.69, and nine times gives 5.40 / 5.80 = 0.93, more than is needed. The size of the answer carries a lesson of its own: a short screening tool with a reliability around 0.60 cannot be pushed to the 0.90 needed for decisions about individuals by adding a handful of items. It needs better items, not just more of them.

A smaller, more realistic case shows why you must round up. Sanya, a clinical psychology trainee in Chandigarh, has written a 10-item questionnaire on sleep problems among college students. Its reliability in a pilot study is 0.70, and she wants 0.80 for her research.

  • Step 1: 0.80 × (1 − 0.70) = 0.80 × 0.30 = 0.24.
  • Step 2: 0.70 × (1 − 0.80) = 0.70 × 0.20 = 0.14.
  • Step 3: 0.24 / 0.14 = 1.714.

Her questionnaire must be about 1.71 times as long: 1.714 × 10 = 17.1 items. Items come in whole numbers, so she rounds up to 18, which means writing 8 new items like the first 10. Rounding down to 17 would fall just short: with n = 1.7 the formula gives 1.19 / 1.49 = 0.799. Table 3.12 brings together every worked example in this section.

Table 3.12 The Spearman-Brown formula, worked seven ways (the first four are the classic examination problems).
ProblemPresent rnWorkingAnswer
Halves correlate 0.60: whole test?0.6021.20 / 1.600.75
Test made three times as long0.5031.50 / 2.000.75
Test cut to half its length0.800.50.40 / 0.600.67
How much longer to reach 0.90?0.60?0.36 / 0.066 times (20 items become 120)
Rohini's VRT, odd-even halves0.7021.40 / 1.700.82
Devansh's short form, 60 items cut to 200.901/30.30 / 0.400.75
Sanya's questionnaire, target 0.800.70?0.24 / 0.14 = 1.7118 items (from 10)

What the formula assumes, and what it cannot do

A prophecy is only as good as its assumptions, and the Spearman-Brown formula rests on four. The first, for its split-half use, is that the two halves are parallel tests in the classical sense of Section 3.1: each person has the same true score on both halves, and the halves have equal error variances. In data you can see this as halves with equal means and equal variances that measure the same content. Only then is the correlation between the halves a fair estimate of the reliability of each half, which is what the formula needs as its starting point. When one half is harder than the other, so that its scores bunch near the floor, or when one half simply has a much larger spread of scores, the halves are not parallel, and once their spreads differ the stepped-up value comes out too high; the next subsection shows by how much.

The second assumption, for its lengthening use, is that the new items are parallel to the old ones: the same content, the same range of difficulty and the same quality, so that each new item correlates with the others as strongly as the old items do. Add twenty easy general-knowledge questions to a verbal reasoning test and the prophecy fails, because the new items measure something else. The third is that the people answer the new items under the same conditions and in the same frame of mind as the old ones: a test made six times longer may bring fatigue, boredom and careless responding that the formula knows nothing about. The fourth is that the prediction applies to the same kind of group in which the present reliability was found; Section 3.8 shows how a coefficient changes when the group is more or less varied.

Exam trap

The Spearman-Brown correction of a split-half coefficient is accurate only when the two halves are parallel: equal means and equal variances, measuring the same content. Halves that differ in difficulty violate the assumption (they do not help by "widening the range"). Halves scored by two different examiners bring in scorer error, the subject of inter-rater reliability (Section 3.6). Halves given on two different days bring in time-sampling error, which turns the design into a kind of test-retest or alternate-forms study (Sections 3.2 and 3.3).

It is just as important to know what the formula does not do, because examiners build their wrong options from the other famous formulas of this chapter. The Spearman-Brown formula does one job in two guises: it estimates full-test reliability from a split-half correlation, and it predicts reliability when the length of a test changes. It does not estimate agreement between raters on categorical judgements; that is Cohen's kappa (Section 3.6). (A cousin of the idea does appear there: averaging several raters' scores raises reliability much as adding items does, but measuring how far two raters agree is a different job.) It does not correct a validity coefficient for the unreliability of the test and the criterion; that is the correction for attenuation (Section 3.8), which is easy to confuse because Spearman devised it too, in 1904. It cannot rescue a speed test from a single sitting (Section 3.4.9 below explains why). It does not predict validity: lengthening a test does raise its validity as well, but more slowly, and that needs a different formula (Chapter 4). And it says nothing about factor structure, which is found by factor analysis (Book 1, Section 9.4), or about effect sizes in an analysis of covariance, which belong to the Research Methodology book.

Exam trap

By the Spearman-Brown formula, reliability is improved by increasing the number of parallel items. Decreasing the number of items lowers it (n below 1). Making items more ambiguous adds random error, so it lowers reliability. Reducing the sample size does not change the size of the coefficient in any predictable way; it only makes the estimate less trustworthy. And when a question asks what the formula predicts, the answer is the reliability of a test whose length is changed by a factor n (or k), not the validity of a new test, not an effect size in ANCOVA and not the factor structure of the WAIS.

Formulas that need no step-up: Rulon, Flanagan and Guttman

The split-half procedure as Spearman and Brown left it has two steps (correlate the halves, then step up the correlation) and one fragile assumption (equal variances in the two halves). In the late 1930s two American psychometricians found a way to go straight from the two half-scores to the reliability of the whole test, without assuming equal variances. The first route was published by Phillip Justin Rulon (1900 to 1968), a psychometrician at the Harvard Graduate School of Education, in a 1939 paper in the Harvard Educational Review titled "A simplified procedure for determining the reliability of a test by split-halves".

Rulon's idea is beautifully direct. If the two halves measure the same true score, then a person's two half-scores can differ only because of error. So, for every person, subtract one half-score from the other to get a difference score, d. The spread of these differences across the group tells you how much error there is. In fact the variance of the differences is an estimate of the error variance of the whole test. You can see why from X = T + E. Write a person's half-scores as T + EA and T + EB. Their difference is EA − EB, because the true scores cancel, and their sum, the total score, has the error part EA + EB. When two errors are independent, subtracting them spreads the result exactly as much as adding them does, so the variance of the differences equals the error variance of the totals. Now recall the definition of reliability from Section 3.1: it is the proportion of observed variance that is not error, r = 1 − σ2E / σ2X. Put the variance of the differences in place of the error variance and you have Rulon's formula.

Remember

Rulon's formula (1939):

r = 1 − σ2d / σ2X

where σ2d is the variance of the differences between each person's two half-scores and σ2X is the variance of the total scores. The result is already the reliability of the full-length test: there is no Spearman-Brown step afterwards.

The second route belongs to John Clemans Flanagan (1906 to 1996), better remembered for the critical incident technique and for Project TALENT, a vast survey of American high-school students begun in 1960. The formula credited to him, usually dated to his 1937 paper in the Journal of Educational Psychology, uses the variances of the two halves instead of the variance of their differences. The Flanagan formula is r = 2[1 − (σ2a + σ2b) / σ2X], where σ2a and σ2b are the variances of the two halves and σ2X is the variance of the totals. The logic is this. The variance of a total is the two half variances plus twice the part the halves share (their covariance, the amount by which they rise and fall together). Subtract the two half variances from the total variance and what is left is twice the shared part, the consistent core of the test. Flanagan expresses that leftover as a proportion of the total variance and doubles it. Louis Guttman (1916 to 1987), the Guttman of the cumulative attitude scale among the attitude measures of Chapter 8, reached the same expression in a 1945 paper in Psychometrika, "A basis for analyzing test-retest reliability", as the fourth of six lower bounds to reliability that he derived, his lambda 4 (λ4). That is why the same formula is called the Flanagan formula, Guttman's split-half coefficient or simply lambda 4. And because Rulon's and Flanagan's formulas always give exactly the same number (they are two algebraic forms of one quantity), many books speak of the Flanagan-Rulon formula.

Remember

The Flanagan formula (1937), also Guttman's λ4 (1945):

r = 2 [1 − (σ2a + σ2b) / σ2X]

where σ2a and σ2b are the variances of the two half-scores and σ2X is the variance of the total scores. It always equals Rulon's coefficient, and needs no step-up.

A small data set lets you work all three formulas side by side. Hrithik, a school counsellor in Bhopal, gave a 36-item numerical ability test to ten Class 9 students. (A real tryout needs hundreds of students; ten keep every step visible.) He scored the 18 odd-numbered items as half A and the 18 even-numbered items as half B, and for each student worked out the difference d = A − B and the total X = A + B. Table 3.13 shows his results.

Table 3.13 Hrithik's split-half data: ten students' scores on the odd and even halves of a 36-item test.
StudentHalf A (odd items)Half B (even items)Difference d = A − BTotal X = A + B
11215−327
21613329
398117
41316−329
51110121
61512327
7811−319
81414028
9109119
101212024
Mean1212024
Variance6.06.04.819.2

Each variance in the bottom row is found the same way: take each score's distance from its column mean, square it, add the squares and divide by the number of students, 10. For half A the distances are 0, 4, −3, 1, −1, 3, −4, 2, −2 and 0; their squares add up to 60, and 60 / 10 = 6.0. For the differences the mean is 0, so the squares are simply 9, 9, 1, 9, 1, 9, 9, 0, 1 and 0, which add up to 48, and 48 / 10 = 4.8. For the totals the distances from 24 are 3, 5, −7, 5, −3, 3, −5, 4, −5 and 0; their squares add up to 192, and 192 / 10 = 19.2. (Some books divide by 9, one less than the number of people, instead of 10. That changes every variance by the same factor and leaves every ratio, and so every reliability below, unchanged.) Now the three formulas.

  • Rulon: σ2d / σ2X = 4.8 / 19.2 = 0.25, so r = 1 − 0.25 = 0.75.
  • Flanagan: σ2a + σ2b = 6.0 + 6.0 = 12.0; 12.0 / 19.2 = 0.625; 1 − 0.625 = 0.375; and 2 × 0.375 = 0.75.
  • Spearman-Brown: first find the correlation between the halves. The shared part (the covariance) is half of what is left when the half variances are taken from the total variance: (19.2 − 12.0) / 2 = 3.6. Dividing by the product of the two standard deviations (√6 × √6 = 6) gives rhalf = 3.6 / 6 = 0.60. Stepping up: 1.20 / 1.60 = 0.75.

All three agree on 0.75. Notice also that Hrithik's data contain the very numbers of Section 3.4.3: half variances of 6, a total variance of 19.2 and, in the variance of the differences, an error variance of 4.8. Rulon's difference scores really are the error of the test made visible.

Why bother with Rulon and Flanagan, then, if Spearman-Brown gave the same answer? Because it gave the same answer only because Hrithik's two halves happened to have equal variances (6 and 6). When the half variances differ, Spearman-Brown parts company with the other two. Suppose a test is split into first and second halves and the second half is so hard that most students score near zero on it: the easier half has a variance of 16 (a standard deviation of 4), the harder half a variance of only 4 (a standard deviation of 2), and the halves correlate 0.50. Their shared part is 0.50 × 4 × 2 = 4. The total variance is 16 + 4 + (2 × 4) = 28, and the variance of the differences is 16 + 4 − (2 × 4) = 12. Rulon gives 1 − 12 / 28 = 1 − 0.43 = 0.57. Flanagan gives 2 × [1 − (16 + 4) / 28] = 2 × (1 − 0.714) = 2 × 0.286 = 0.57. Spearman-Brown, blind to the unequal variances, steps 0.50 up to 1.00 / 1.50 = 0.67, an overestimate of 0.10. The rule is general: for halves that correlate positively, as the halves of any sensible test do, the Spearman-Brown value is never lower than the Flanagan-Rulon value, and the two are equal only when the two halves have equal variances. (For the algebraically minded: Flanagan's coefficient can be written as 4 times the covariance divided by σ2a + σ2b + 2 times the covariance, and Spearman-Brown's as the same thing with 2σaσb in place of σ2a + σ2b; since σ2a + σ2b is never smaller than 2σaσb, and equals it only when σa = σb, the rule follows.) Table 3.14 compares the three formulas, and Section 3.5 shows where the Flanagan-Rulon coefficient leads next: in 1951 Lee Cronbach proved that coefficient alpha is the average of the Flanagan-Rulon coefficients over every possible split of a test.

Table 3.14 Three ways to turn two half-scores into the reliability of the whole test.
FormulaWhat you needStep-up afterwards?Assumes
Spearman-Brown (1910): 2rhalf / (1 + rhalf)The correlation between the halvesIt is the step-upParallel halves with equal variances; too high when variances differ
Rulon (1939): 1 − σ2d / σ2XVariance of the half-score differences and of the totalsNoHalves measure the same true score; their variances may differ
Flanagan (1937), Guttman's λ4 (1945): 2[1 − (σ2a + σ2b) / σ2X]Variance of each half and of the totalsNoAs for Rulon; always gives the same value as Rulon

Two examination problems complete the picture. In the first, the variance of the difference scores (odd minus even) is 4 and the variance of the total scores is 20. Rulon: 4 / 20 = 0.20, and 1 − 0.20 = 0.80. In the second, the question gives standard deviations instead: the standard deviation of the difference scores is 3 and that of the total scores is 6. Rulon's formula needs variances, so square both first: 32 = 9 and 62 = 36. Then 9 / 36 = 0.25, and 1 − 0.25 = 0.75.

Exam trap

Rulon's formula estimates split-half reliability from the variance of the differences between the two half-scores, relative to the total-score variance. The correlation between two administrations of one test is test-retest reliability (Section 3.2); the correlation between the halves stepped up with Spearman-Brown is the very procedure Rulon made unnecessary; and the proportion of examinees passing each item is item difficulty, p, the raw material of KR-20 (Section 3.5). In the numerical versions, every wrong option has a story. With variances 4 and 20: .20 forgets to subtract from 1, .89 wrongly steps .80 up with Spearman-Brown (1.60 / 1.80), and .96 squares the ratio. With standard deviations 3 and 6: .50 plugs the SDs in unsquared (1 − 3 / 6), .25 is the error share, and .86 wrongly steps .75 up (1.50 / 1.75). Square SDs first; never step up a Rulon or Flanagan result.

Speed tests, power tests and the split that fails

Every method in this section so far quietly assumes something about how a test is taken: that each person has a fair chance to attempt every item, so that the score reflects which items they can answer. That is true of some tests and false of others, and the difference decides whether splitting works at all. Psychologists describe it with two ideal types.
