A pure speed test consists of items so easy that anyone would answer every one of them correctly given enough time (crossing out every letter "a" on a page of print, or deciding whether two strings of numbers are identical), set under a time limit so tight that nobody finishes. Since almost nobody makes errors, the score is simply the number of items done, and differences between people are differences in speed. A pure power test is the opposite: its items rise in difficulty until some are too hard for almost anyone, and the time limit is generous enough that nearly everyone attempts every item they are able to. The score shows how far up the ladder of difficulty a person can climb. A mathematics paper with ample time is close to a power test; a clerical checking test is close to a speed test. Most real tests lie somewhere between, and the degree to which scores on a test depend on the rate of working rather than on the level reached is called its speededness.

Now try an odd-even split on a pure speed test. Ranjit, an industrial psychologist in Kolkata, uses a 100-item clerical speed test to help a bank select clerks. Each item shows two long numbers, and the candidate marks whether they are identical; the time limit is ten minutes, and almost nobody makes a mistake. A candidate who reaches item 34 has answered items 1 to 34 correctly and nothing beyond, so her odd-item score is 17 (items 1, 3 and so on up to 33) and her even-item score is also 17 (items 2, 4 and so on up to 34). A candidate who reaches item 47 scores 24 odd and 23 even; one who reaches item 81 scores 41 odd and 40 even. Every candidate's two half-scores are equal or differ by one, whatever kind of day the candidate was having. When Ranjit correlated the odd and even scores of ten candidates, he got 0.998, and Spearman-Brown stepped it up to 1.00: apparently a perfectly reliable test.

It is nothing of the sort. The consistency was manufactured by the scoring. The two halves are not two independent samples of the candidate's speed; they are two halves of a single count of how far the candidate got, so they must agree. In a speed test the items are all alike, so content-sampling error, the only error a split from one sitting can detect, hardly exists. The real error lies in the rate of working: a slow minute, a moment of distraction, a stiff wrist, a bad night's sleep. A single timed sitting cannot see that error, because whatever slows a candidate down slows both halves together. The left panel of Figure 3.9 shows the result: every point lies on, or one point away from, the line of equal scores. The same objection applies to KR-20 and coefficient alpha computed from one timed sitting (Section 3.5): every unreached item counts as wrong in every subset of items, so they too come out spuriously high. Harold Gulliksen (1903 to 1996) analysed the problem formally in a 1950 paper, "The reliability of speeded tests". Splitting into a first half and a second half is no cure either: whether a person even gets into the second half depends on speed, so those halves are not parallel.

So how should the reliability of a speed test be estimated? The answer given in the standard textbooks, such as Psychological Testing by Anne Anastasi (1908 to 2001), is to make sure the two scores come from separately timed performances. Test-retest reliability (Section 3.2) does this by testing on two occasions, and alternate forms (Section 3.3) by giving two separately timed forms. If a split-half estimate is wanted, the test must be split by time, not by scoring: print the two halves separately and give each as a test of its own with half the time limit, the method of separately timed halves. Now how far a candidate gets on half A does not fix her score on half B, and a slow five minutes costs her on one half only. Ranjit did this with the same ten candidates, giving the odd items and the even items as two five-minute tests. The two half-scores correlated 0.76 (the right panel of Figure 3.9), and because each half is half the length of the full test, he stepped this up with Spearman-Brown: 2 × 0.76 = 1.52; 1 + 0.76 = 1.76; 1.52 / 1.76 = 0.86. That is an honest reliability, high as speed tests usually are, but not the impossible perfection of the single-sitting split. Indian textbooks such as A.K. Singh's Tests, Measurements and Research Methods in Behavioural Sciences also describe a quarter method: the total time is divided into four equal quarters, the work done in the first and fourth quarters forms one half-score and the work done in the second and third forms the other (so that warm-up and fatigue are balanced), and Rulon's formula is applied. Table 3.15 sums up what works and what does not.

Table 3.15 Estimating the reliability of a pure speed test.
MethodAcceptable?Why
Test-retestYesTwo full timed sittings; each score is its own performance
Alternate formsYesTwo separately timed forms
Separately timed halves, stepped up with Spearman-BrownYesProgress on one half cannot fix the score on the other
Odd-even split from one timed sittingNo: the least appropriateHalves equal by construction, so r is near 1.00 and spurious
KR-20 or alpha from one timed sittingNoUnreached items count as wrong everywhere, inflating the estimate
Clinical link

You can see this rule at work in the technical data of the Wechsler intelligence scales (Chapter 6). The fourth edition of the Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children (WISC-IV), for example, reports split-half reliabilities for all its subtests except the speeded ones (Coding, Symbol Search and Cancellation), for which it uses test-retest coefficients instead, because a split of a speeded subtest from one sitting would be spuriously high. When you read any test manual, check which method produced each coefficient before you trust it.

The same reasoning explains why the odd-even method suits some kinds of test and not others. It is most appropriate for power tests, where everyone has time to attempt every item they can and the odd and even items are two fair samples of the same ability. It is least appropriate for speed tests, for the reason you have just seen. It is also awkward for projective tests such as the Rorschach inkblots (Chapter 10): their ten cards are each different, a person's free responses cannot be divided into two equivalent halves, and the reliability question that matters most for them is whether two scorers agree (Section 3.6). And many neuropsychological tests (Chapter 11) consist of a single task, such as connecting numbered circles against the clock or copying a complex figure, often timed, which cannot sensibly be cut into two matching halves; their reliability is usually estimated by retesting or with alternate forms.

Exam trap

The odd-even method is most appropriate for power tests, not for speed, projective or neuropsychological tests. For a pure speed test, the least appropriate estimate is an odd-even split-half from a single timed sitting; test-retest, alternate forms and two separately timed halves are all acceptable. If a clerical speed test must be split, the acceptable procedure is to time the two halves separately, correlate them and step up with Spearman-Brown. Scoring odd and even items from one sitting and stepping up is the trap, and so are Cronbach's alpha and KR-20 from the single sitting, which are inflated in the same way. Spearman-Brown cannot rescue the single sitting: stepping up a spurious correlation only gives a bigger spurious number.

What one sitting cannot tell you

One limitation is shared by every method in this section (split-half with Spearman-Brown, Rulon, Flanagan and Guttman) and by KR-20 and alpha in Section 3.5. They all rest on a single administration, so the only error they can see is content-sampling error, the luck of which items a person met. Anything that affects the whole sitting, such as a fever, a sleepless night, a quarrel at breakfast or an unusually good mood, lifts or lowers both halves together, and these methods quietly count it as true-score variance. That is why a single-sitting coefficient is often a little higher than a test-retest coefficient for the same test, and why it says nothing at all about whether a person would obtain the same score next month. The choice of method should follow the question you need answered. If the scores will be used to compare people across time or to predict the future, you need evidence of stability from retesting (Section 3.2). If you need to know whether the items of a test hang together as samples of one ability, a single-sitting method answers it, and the next section shows how to do so without the arbitrariness of choosing one split. Section 3.8 then lines up all of Rohini's coefficients and explains why they differ.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The usual way of splitting an ability test, which spreads easy, hard, early and late items over both halves, is the split.
  2. The major challenge in split-half reliability is dividing the test into two nearly halves.
  3. The correlation between two halves underestimates the reliability of the whole test because each half is only as long.
  4. Halves that correlate 0.60 give a full-test reliability of by the Spearman-Brown formula.
  5. A test with a reliability of 0.50, made three times as long with parallel items, has a predicted reliability of .
  6. To raise a reliability of 0.60 to 0.90, a test must be made times as long.
  7. Rulon's formula estimates reliability from the variance of the between the two half-scores, relative to the variance of the total scores.
  8. If the standard deviation of the difference scores is 3 and that of the total scores is 6, Rulon's coefficient is .
  9. The Flanagan and Rulon formulas always agree, and Spearman-Brown agrees with them only when the two halves have equal .
  10. On a pure test, an odd-even split from one timed sitting gives a spuriously high coefficient.

Answers: (1) odd-even, (2) equivalent (parallel), (3) half, (4) 0.75 (2 × 0.60 = 1.20; 1.20 / 1.60 = 0.75), (5) 0.75 (3 × 0.50 = 1.50; 1 + 2 × 0.50 = 2.00; 1.50 / 2.00 = 0.75), (6) six (0.90 × 0.40 = 0.36; 0.60 × 0.10 = 0.06; 0.36 / 0.06 = 6), (7) differences, (8) 0.75 (square first: 9 / 36 = 0.25; 1 − 0.25 = 0.75), (9) variances, (10) speed.

3.5 Internal Consistency: The Kuder-Richardson Formulas and Coefficient AlphaHigh yield · 20 Qs

Section 3.4 left a loose thread hanging. Rohini split her Verbal Reasoning Test into odd and even items, correlated the two halves and stepped the result up to 0.82. But why odd and even? She could just as well have put items 1 to 10 and 31 to 40 in one half, or pulled twenty item numbers out of a hat. How many different halves could she have made? For a 40-item test the answer is 68,923,264,410: almost sixty-nine billion different ways of dividing the items into two sets of twenty. Every one of those splits gives its own half-test correlation, and because no two splits pair exactly the same items, the numbers differ a little, and sometimes more than a little. Which of them is "the" split-half reliability of the VRT? None has a better claim than any other. Lee Cronbach, looking back on the problem half a century later, put it bluntly: there were a vast number of possible splits of a test, "and therefore any split-half coefficient was to some degree incorrect".

The way out is to stop cutting the test into two big pieces and to look instead at its smallest pieces, the items themselves. If every item is a small sample from the same domain of content (the domain-sampling model of Section 3.1), then each item is a miniature parallel test of all the others, and the question "do the two halves agree?" becomes the better question "do all the items agree with one another?" This section teaches the three formulas that answer it, KR-20, KR-21 and coefficient alpha, along with the analysis-of-variance route that reaches the same number. They need one administration, one set of item scores and no arbitrary split. Alpha in particular is the reliability coefficient you will meet most often in test manuals and research papers, which is why examiners ask about it more than about anything else in this chapter.

One sitting, every item: the idea of internal consistency

The property this section measures is internal consistency: the degree to which the items of a test, given once to one group of people, agree with one another, that is, whether the people who do well on one item tend to do well on the others. A test whose items all tap the same ability or trait will show it; a test that mixes items on unrelated things will not. Think of a ten-item test of arithmetic reasoning. If a student's success on item 3 tells you nothing about his chances on items 4, 5 and 6, then each item is measuring something private to itself, and the total is a pile of unrelated scraps. If success on one item goes with success on the others, the items are pulling in the same direction and the total is a sturdy summary of one ability.

Which kind of error does this detect? Go back to the map of error sources in Section 3.1. Internal consistency methods catch content sampling error: the luck of which particular items happened to be on the paper. A student who happens to know the answer to three oddly specific items gains marks that a different selection of items would not have given him, and that luck shows up as disagreement among the items. What internal consistency cannot catch is time sampling error, the day-to-day wobble in a person's state, because all the items are answered at one sitting. If a student is feverish on the day, every item suffers together, the items still agree with each other, and the coefficient is none the wiser. That single fact answers a whole family of exam questions, as the trap at the end of this subsection shows.

How can agreement among forty items be squeezed into one number without computing hundreds of correlations? The trick lies in the variance of the total score. When you add item scores together, the spread of the totals depends on two things: how much each item spreads on its own (its item variance), and how much the items rise and fall together (their covariance, which is simply an unstandardised correlation: positive when high scores on one item go with high scores on the other). If the items are unrelated, a person's good luck on one item is unrelated to his luck on the next, highs and lows cancel out, and the totals spread only as much as the item variances added together. If the items agree, the person who passes one passes the others, highs pile on highs and lows on lows, and the totals spread far more widely than the item variances alone could explain. So the size of the total-score variance compared with the sum of the item variances tells you how strongly the items agree. (Variance and covariance are taught in full in the Research Methodology book; this is all you need here.)

Figure 3.10 shows the bookkeeping for a small four-item test. Every cell of the square holds one number. The four cells on the diagonal, shaded differently from the rest, are the item variances; every other cell holds the covariance between a pair of items, and each pair appears twice (item 1 with item 2, and item 2 with item 1). The variance of the total score is exactly the sum of all sixteen cells. Here the diagonal adds up to 4.0 and the whole square to 8.8, so 4.8 of the 8.8 units of total variance, a little over half, comes from the items moving together. That share is what every formula in this section is built on.

Two extreme cases show why this works. Suppose the four items were completely unrelated: every covariance would be zero, the total variance would equal the sum of the item variances, the ratio of the two would be 1, and one minus that ratio would be 0, which is exactly the right answer for a test with no internal consistency at all. Now suppose the items were perfect copies of one another. The covariances would then be as large as the variances, the total variance would be the item variance multiplied by 4 × 4 = 16, the diagonal would hold only 4 of those 16 parts, and one minus the ratio would be 1 − 4/16 = 0.75. That is short of 1 even though the items agree perfectly, because the item variances are themselves always part of the total. The correction is to multiply by k / (k − 1), here 4/3, and 0.75 × 4/3 = 1. Hold on to those two pieces, "one minus the item variances over the total variance" and "the k over k minus 1 correction": every formula in this section is made of them.

Exam trap

"Which is a potential limitation of the internal consistency method?" The key is that it measures consistency within a single administration only, so it says nothing about stability over time; for that you need test-retest reliability (Section 3.2). The other options are not limitations in the examiners' sense. "It is affected by differences in test content" describes what the method is designed to detect: content sampling error, and a test that mixes unrelated content rightly earns a lower coefficient. "It can only be used with tests that have multiple items" is true, but it is a condition of use, not a weakness (a single-item rating has no items to compare, so its reliability must come from retesting). So "all of the above" is not keyed; choose the single administration.

Be aware that the item is not watertight. Some books do count option (b) as a weakness: on a test that deliberately samples varied content (a general-knowledge paper, or the life-events checklist in the Clinical link at the end of this section), the items agree only weakly, so alpha comes out low even when the total score is perfectly stable from one occasion to the next, and it then understates the consistency that matters. That is the case for "all of the above". The standard answer, and the key, is still the single administration, because it is the one limitation every internal consistency coefficient shares whatever the test contains.

Item difficulty and item variance: p, q and pq

Before we can add up item variances we need to know what the variance of a single item is. For an item scored right (1) or wrong (0), the answer turns out to be beautifully simple, and it depends only on how hard the item is. The item difficulty, written p, is the proportion of examinees who answer the item correctly. (The name is back to front, a famous oddity of testing: an item with p = 0.90 is an easy item, because 90 percent pass it. Chapter 2 teaches item difficulty and the rest of item analysis in full.) The proportion who fail is q = 1 − p.

Take an item that 30 of 100 students get right, so p = 0.30 and q = 0.70. What is its variance? Work it the long way once. The item's mean is 0.30, because 30 ones and 70 zeros average to 0.30. Each student who passed scored 1, which is 0.70 above the mean; squared, that is 0.49. Each student who failed scored 0, which is 0.30 below the mean; squared, that is 0.09. The variance is the average squared distance from the mean: 30 percent of the students contribute 0.49 each and 70 percent contribute 0.09 each, so the variance is (0.30 × 0.49) + (0.70 × 0.09) = 0.147 + 0.063 = 0.21. Now notice that 0.30 × 0.70 is also 0.21. That is no coincidence: for any right-wrong item the long calculation always collapses to the product of p and q.

Remember

Variance of a right-wrong item = p × q

p is the proportion who answer the item correctly (its difficulty index), q = 1 − p is the proportion who answer it wrongly, and their product pq is the item's variance. Summed over all the items of a test it is written Σpq ("sigma p q", the sum of p times q).

Table 3.16 runs through the whole range of difficulty, and Figure 3.11 draws the result as a curve. Two features are worth fixing in memory. First, pq is largest, 0.25, when p = 0.50: an item that half the group passes spreads people out as much as a right-wrong item possibly can. Second, pq falls to zero at both ends. An item that everyone passes (p = 1) or everyone fails (p = 0) has no variance at all; it cannot tell one examinee from another, adds nothing to the differences between people and contributes nothing to reliability. That is why test builders prefer items of middling difficulty, and why a test full of giveaway items is less reliable than its length suggests (Section 3.8 and Chapter 2). Notice also that the curve is symmetrical: an item passed by 30 percent has the same variance, 0.21, as an item passed by 70 percent.

Table 3.16 Item difficulty and item variance for right-wrong items. The variance pq peaks at 0.25 when half the group passes and vanishes when everyone passes or everyone fails.
p (proportion passing)q = 1 − ppq (item variance)What the item is like
0.001.000.00Nobody passes: no information
0.100.900.09Very hard
0.200.800.16Hard
0.300.700.21Fairly hard
0.400.600.24A little harder than average
0.500.500.25Middling: the maximum variance
0.600.400.24A little easier than average
0.700.300.21Fairly easy
0.800.200.16Easy
0.900.100.09Very easy
1.000.000.00Everyone passes: no information
Exam trap

"On a right-wrong item, 30 percent answer correctly. What is the item variance that goes into KR-20?" The answer is 0.21 (0.30 × 0.70). The wrong options are built from the same numbers: 0.09 is p squared (0.30 × 0.30), 0.25 is the largest variance any right-wrong item can have (only at p = 0.50), and 0.30 is p itself, the difficulty, not the variance. If an option shows 0.49, that is q squared. Multiply p by q, and nothing else.

KR-20: Kuder and Richardson's formula (1937)

In September 1937 the young journal Psychometrika, then only in its second volume, carried a ten-page paper called "The theory of the estimation of test reliability". Its authors, both then at the University of Chicago, were G. Frederic Kuder (1903 to 2000), later famous for the Kuder Preference Record, the interest inventory you will meet in Chapter 8, and Marion Webster Richardson (1896 to 1965), one of the founders of the Psychometric Society and of Psychometrika itself. Their aim was to cut through the confusion of split-half estimates by deriving a reliability coefficient from the statistics of the items, with no splitting at all. They worked out a series of numbered formulas, and two of them became famous. They are still known by their equation numbers in the paper: the twentieth, the Kuder-Richardson formula 20 (KR-20), and the twenty-first, KR-21.

In words, KR-20 does what Figure 3.10 suggested. It adds up the variances of the separate items (for right-wrong items, the pq values), divides that sum by the variance of the total scores, subtracts the result from 1 to get the share of total variance that comes from the items agreeing with each other, and finally multiplies by k / (k − 1) so that perfectly consistent items would score 1.

Remember

KR-20 = [k / (k − 1)] × [1 − Σpq / σ2X]

k is the number of items; p is the proportion passing each item and q = 1 − p; Σpq is the sum of the item variances pq over all k items; σ2X ("sigma squared X") is the variance of the total scores. KR-20 is used when every item is scored dichotomously (right or wrong, yes or no, 1 or 0), whether or not the items differ in difficulty.

Let us push a real set of answers through it, so that you can see where p and σ2X come from. Nupur teaches psychology at a junior college in Nagpur. She writes a six-item right-or-wrong quiz on research methods and tries it on a tutorial group of ten students, A to J. (A real tryout would use a hundred students or more; ten keeps every step of the arithmetic on the page.) Table 3.17 shows each student's answers, coded 1 for right and 0 for wrong, with the totals.

Table 3.17 Nupur's six-item quiz: ten students' answers (1 = right, 0 = wrong), their totals, and the working for the variance of the totals (mean 3.5).
StudentItems 1 to 6TotalTotal − meanSquared
A1 1 1 1 1 16+2.56.25
B1 1 1 1 1 05+1.52.25
C1 1 1 1 0 15+1.52.25
D1 1 1 0 1 04+0.50.25
E1 1 0 1 1 04+0.50.25
F1 1 1 1 1 05+1.52.25
G1 1 1 0 0 03−0.50.25
H1 0 0 0 0 01−2.56.25
I1 1 0 0 0 02−1.52.25
J0 0 0 0 0 00−3.512.25
Sum35034.50

Step 1: the variance of the totals. The ten totals add up to 35, so the mean is 35 / 10 = 3.5. Subtract the mean from each total, square the result and add the squares: the last column of Table 3.17 sums to 34.50. Divide by the number of students: σ2X = 34.50 / 10 = 3.45.

Step 2: p, q and pq for every item. Count how many of the ten students got each item right (read down the columns of answers). Item 1 was answered correctly by nine students, so p = 0.90; item 6 by only two, so p = 0.20. Table 3.18 does the rest. The items range from very easy to hard, and their variances add up to Σpq = 1.15.

Table 3.18 Nupur's quiz: the difficulty and variance of each item.
ItemRight (of 10)pqpq
190.900.100.09
280.800.200.16
360.600.400.24
450.500.500.25
550.500.500.25
620.200.800.16
Sum35Σpq = 1.15

Step 3: the ratio. Σpq / σ2X = 1.15 / 3.45 = 0.333. One third of the total-score variance is the items' own private variance.

Step 4: subtract from 1. 1 − 0.333 = 0.667. Two thirds of the total variance comes from the items agreeing with each other.

Step 5: the correction. With k = 6 items, k / (k − 1) = 6 / 5 = 1.2, and 1.2 × 0.667 = 0.80.

Nupur's six-item quiz has a KR-20 of 0.80. One practical warning before moving on: the item variances and the total variance must be computed in the same way. The pq values divide by the number of students (they are proportions), so the total variance must also divide by the number of students, as we did, and not by one fewer. Mixing the two conventions nudges the answer, and with ten students it nudges it noticeably.

The past papers usually spare you the raw data and hand you the ingredients. "A 10-item right-wrong test has Σpq = 1.8 and a total-score variance of 6. What is its KR-20?" Step 3: 1.8 / 6 = 0.30. Step 4: 1 − 0.30 = 0.70. Step 5: 10 / 9 = 1.111, and 1.111 × 0.70 = 0.778, which rounds to 0.78. Notice how much the correction mattered: on a 10-item test it adds about 11 percent, while on a 100-item test the factor 100/99 = 1.0101 adds only about 1 percent, so the shorter the test, the more you must not forget it.

Now Rohini's VRT, with its canonical numbers: 40 items, Σpq = 6.5 and a total-score variance of 36 (a standard deviation of 6). Step 3: 6.5 / 36 = 0.1806. Step 4: 1 − 0.1806 = 0.8194. Step 5: 40 / 39 = 1.0256, and 1.0256 × 0.8194 = 0.8405, which rounds to 0.84. Read it in the language of Section 3.1: about 84 percent of the variance in VRT scores (30.24 of the 36 units) is consistent across items, true-score variance as far as content sampling is concerned, and about 16 percent (5.76 units) is error arising from the particular items Rohini happened to write. It is a little higher than her split-half estimate of 0.82 and her three-week retest of 0.80; Section 3.8 explains why different methods give a test different coefficients, and why that is no contradiction.

When is KR-20 the right tool? Whenever the items are scored dichotomously. That covers the right-wrong items of ability, aptitude and achievement tests, and equally the true-false or yes-no items of many personality inventories, such as the true-false items of the MMPI (Chapter 9). KR-20 does not care what the items are about; it cares only that each is scored 0 or 1. Nor does it assume that the items are equally difficult. That assumption belongs to its simpler sister, KR-21.

Exam trap

KR-20 is the appropriate estimate when items are scored right or wrong (dichotomously) and vary in difficulty. The same test given twice, two weeks apart, calls for test-retest reliability (Section 3.2); a pure speed test calls for separately timed halves, retesting or parallel forms, never a single-sitting internal consistency formula (Section 3.4); items on a five-point Likert scale call for coefficient alpha (later in this section). In the arithmetic, the wrong options are stopping points: for Σpq = 1.8, variance 6 and 10 items, 0.30 is the ratio before subtracting from 1, 0.70 forgets the k / (k − 1) correction, and 0.63 applies the correction upside down (0.70 × 9/10). The answer is 0.78.

KR-21: the shortcut that assumes equal difficulty

KR-20 needs a p value for every item, which before computers meant counting by hand the passes on every single item. Kuder and Richardson saw that a rougher estimate could be had from numbers any teacher already keeps in a mark register: the number of items, the mean and the variance of the totals. The price is an assumption, that all the items are equally difficult. If they were, every item would have the same p, and that p would simply be the average difficulty of the test, p̄ = M / k, the mean score divided by the number of items. (A class that averages 24 out of 40 has passed, on average, 24 / 40 = 60 percent of the items.) Replace every item's own pq with the average p̄q̄, and the sum Σpq becomes k × p̄ × q̄ = k × (M / k) × ((k − M) / k), which tidies up to M(k − M) / k. Put that into KR-20 and you have KR-21.

Remember

KR-21 = [k / (k − 1)] × [1 − M(k − M) / (kσ2X)]

k is the number of items, M is the mean total score, and σ2X is the variance of the total scores. Like KR-20 it is for dichotomously scored items only, and it assumes that every item has the same difficulty.

Work the past-paper version first. "A 20-item test has a mean of 12 and a variance of 16. What is its KR-21?" Step 1: M(k − M) = 12 × (20 − 12) = 12 × 8 = 96. Step 2: kσ2X = 20 × 16 = 320. Step 3: 96 / 320 = 0.30, and 1 − 0.30 = 0.70. Step 4: 20 / 19 = 1.0526, and 1.0526 × 0.70 = 0.737, which rounds to 0.74.

Now Rohini's VRT: 40 items, mean 24, variance 36. M(k − M) = 24 × 16 = 384; kσ2X = 40 × 36 = 1,440; 384 / 1,440 = 0.2667, and 1 − 0.2667 = 0.7333; finally 1.0256 × 0.7333 = 0.752, which rounds to 0.75. KR-20 on the same test gave 0.84. And Nupur's quiz, with mean 3.5 and variance 3.45: M(k − M) = 3.5 × 2.5 = 8.75; kσ2X = 6 × 3.45 = 20.7; 8.75 / 20.7 = 0.4227; 1 − 0.4227 = 0.5773; and 1.2 × 0.5773 = 0.69, against a KR-20 of 0.80.

Why does KR-21 come out lower both times? Look again at Figure 3.11. Because the pq curve bends downward like an arch, two items of different difficulty always have less variance between them than two items of the same average difficulty: items at p = 0.20 and p = 0.80 contribute 0.16 + 0.16 = 0.32, while two items at p = 0.50 contribute 0.25 + 0.25 = 0.50. So whenever item difficulties differ, the true Σpq is smaller than the k p̄q̄ that KR-21 assumes. For the VRT, KR-21 behaves as if Σpq were 384 / 40 = 9.6, when the real sum is 6.5; for Nupur's quiz it uses 8.75 / 6 = 1.458 instead of the real 1.15. Subtracting a bigger number from 1 leaves a smaller coefficient. The gap is exact and predictable: the real Σpq always equals k p̄q̄ minus k times the variance of the item difficulties. Nupur's six p values (0.9, 0.8, 0.6, 0.5, 0.5 and 0.2) have a variance of 0.0514, and 6 × 0.0514 = 0.308, which is precisely the gap between 1.458 and 1.15. Only when every item has exactly the same difficulty does the variance of the p values vanish, and only then do KR-20 and KR-21 agree.

So KR-21 is a conservative estimate: a floor, not a ceiling. That makes it genuinely useful. Siddhant teaches mathematics at a school in Jaipur. At the end of term he has his class's marks on a 50-item objective unit test, with a mean of 30 and a standard deviation of 8, but no item-by-item record. Is the test reliable enough to grade on? First square the standard deviation, because the formula needs the variance: 8 × 8 = 64. Then M(k − M) = 30 × 20 = 600; kσ2X = 50 × 64 = 3,200; 600 / 3,200 = 0.1875; 1 − 0.1875 = 0.8125; and 50 / 49 = 1.0204, so KR-21 = 1.0204 × 0.8125 = 0.83. Since KR-21 can only underestimate KR-20, Siddhant can say that his test's KR-20 is at least about 0.83, without counting a single item. What he must not do is use KR-21 on a speeded test, where, like every single-sitting formula, it would be inflated (Section 3.4).

Exam trap

When the items of a test differ in difficulty, KR-21 gives a lower estimate than KR-20, never a higher one; the two are equal only when every item is equally difficult. It is not "an estimate of test-retest stability instead": both formulas come from one administration. In the 20-item problem (mean 12, variance 16) the wrong options are again stopping points: 0.30 is the ratio, 0.70 forgets the 20/19 correction, and 0.85 matches no step at all. The answer is 0.74. If a question gives the standard deviation, square it first.

Hoyt's analysis of variance (1941)

Four years after Kuder and Richardson, a quite different road led to the same place. Cyril Hoyt, in a 1941 Psychometrika paper called "Test reliability estimated by analysis of variance", laid a test's results out as a table with one row for each person and one column for each item, exactly like Table 3.17, and analysed it with the analysis of variance (ANOVA), the technique Ronald Aylmer Fisher (1890 to 1962) had developed for agricultural experiments (the Research Methodology book teaches it). ANOVA divides the total variation in such a table into three parts: variation between persons (some people score higher on all the items than others do), variation between items (some items are easier for everybody), and what is left over, the residual, which is the person-by-item inconsistency: this person did unexpectedly well on that item and unexpectedly badly on another. Hoyt's insight was that the differences between persons contain true differences plus error, while the residual is error alone, so reliability is the share of the between-person variation that is not error.

Remember

Hoyt's reliability = (MSpersons − MSresidual) / MSpersons = 1 − MSresidual / MSpersons

MS stands for mean square, a sum of squared deviations divided by its degrees of freedom. MSpersons measures how much people differ from one another; MSresidual measures the person-by-item inconsistency, which is treated as error.

Run Nupur's quiz through it. The between-persons sum of squares is the sum of squared deviations of the totals from Table 3.17 divided by the number of items, 34.5 / 6 = 5.75, with 10 − 1 = 9 degrees of freedom, so MSpersons = 5.75 / 9 = 0.639. The residual sum of squares for these data also happens to be 5.75, with (10 − 1) × (6 − 1) = 45 degrees of freedom, so MSresidual = 5.75 / 45 = 0.128. Hoyt's coefficient is 1 − 0.128 / 0.639 = 1 − 0.20 = 0.80, exactly the KR-20 we found by counting passes. That is not luck: Hoyt's method always gives the same value as KR-20 for right-wrong items, and as coefficient alpha for any items. Its importance lies in the framing. Hoyt treated reliability as a matter of variance components in a persons-by-items design, the same way of thinking that lies behind the intraclass correlation (Section 3.6) and generalizability theory (Section 3.9).

Cronbach's coefficient alpha (1951)

KR-20 has one limitation built into its very symbol: pq exists only for items scored 0 or 1. Yet a great many psychological measures are not scored that way. An attitude or personality scale asks people to rate statements from 1 ("strongly disagree") to 5 ("strongly agree"); a depression questionnaire such as the PHQ-9 rates each symptom from 0 ("not at all") to 3 ("nearly every day"); an essay examination gives marks out of 10 for each answer; an achievement test may give partial credit. What is the internal consistency of a scale like that?

The general answer came from Lee Joseph Cronbach (1916 to 2001). Born in Fresno, California, Cronbach was as a boy one of the gifted children followed in Lewis Terman's long-term study of gifted children. He taught for many years at the University of Illinois and then at Stanford University, was president of the American Psychological Association in 1957 (his presidential address, "The two disciplines of scientific psychology", urged experimental and correlational psychologists to work together), and is a name you will meet three times in this chapter: alpha here, the bandwidth-fidelity dilemma in Section 3.8 and generalizability theory in Section 3.9. In 1951 he published in Psychometrika a long paper, "Coefficient alpha and the internal structure of tests". Its central formula keeps the logic of KR-20 and simply replaces pq, the variance of a right-wrong item, with the variance of each item whatever its scoring. The result is coefficient alpha, now universally called Cronbach's alpha.

Remember

α = [k / (k − 1)] × [1 − Σσ2i / σ2X]

α is coefficient alpha; k is the number of items; σ2i is the variance of the scores on item i, and Σσ2i is the sum of the k item variances; σ2X is the variance of the total scores. It works for items with any number of score points: two (right or wrong), four, five, seven or more.

Here is alpha computed from start to finish. Amala is a counsellor at a college in Kochi. Worried by the number of students who freeze in examinations, she writes a six-item Exam Stress Scale on which students rate each statement from 1 (never) to 5 (almost always). Item 6, "I stay calm during examinations", is worded in the opposite direction, so before adding up she reverse-scores it (a rating of 5 becomes 1, 4 becomes 2, and so on: the scored value is 6 minus the rating), so that a high score on every item means more stress. She tries the scale on ten students. Table 3.19 shows their scored ratings and totals, and Table 3.20 the mean and variance of each item.

Table 3.19 Amala's six-item Exam Stress Scale: ten students' scored ratings (1 to 5, item 6 reverse-scored), their totals, and the working for the variance of the totals (mean 17).
StudentItems 1 to 6TotalTotal − meanSquared
11 2 2 1 3 211−636
21 2 1 2 2 19−864
32 2 1 3 3 213−416
43 1 2 3 2 314−39
51 4 3 2 2 214−39
64 2 3 3 3 318+11
75 4 2 3 4 321+416
84 3 4 2 5 321+416
94 5 4 3 4 525+864
104 5 3 4 4 424+749
Sum1700280
Table 3.20 Amala's Exam Stress Scale: the six items, their means and their variances (ten students).
ItemStatementMeanVariance
1I lose sleep the night before an examination2.92.09
2My mind goes blank when I first read the question paper3.01.80
3My heart pounds while I wait outside the examination hall2.51.05
4I keep worrying about my marks for days after a paper2.60.64
5I cannot concentrate on revision because I feel tense3.20.96
6I stay calm during examinations (reverse-scored)2.81.16
Sum17.0Σσ2i = 7.70

Step 1: the variance of each item. Take item 1. The ten students' scores on it are 1, 1, 2, 3, 1, 4, 5, 4, 4 and 4, which add to 29, so the item mean is 2.9. The deviations from 2.9 are −1.9, −1.9, −0.9, +0.1, −1.9, +1.1, +2.1, +1.1, +1.1 and +1.1; their squares are 3.61, 3.61, 0.81, 0.01, 3.61, 1.21, 4.41, 1.21, 1.21 and 1.21, which add to 20.90; and 20.90 / 10 = 2.09. The other five items are done the same way (Table 3.20), and the six variances add up to Σσ2i = 7.70.

Step 2: the variance of the totals. The totals add to 170, so the mean is 17. The squared deviations in Table 3.19 add to 280, and 280 / 10 gives σ2X = 28.0.

Step 3: the ratio. 7.70 / 28.0 = 0.275.

Step 4: subtract from 1. 1 − 0.275 = 0.725.

Step 5: the correction. 6 / 5 = 1.2, and 1.2 × 0.725 = 0.87.

Amala's scale has an alpha of 0.87: the six items hang together well. Alpha also catches two common mistakes in building a scale. Suppose she had forgotten to reverse-score item 6. The item variances would not change at all (reversing a scale does not change its spread), but item 6 would now pull against the other five, the totals would bunch together, and their variance would fall from 28.0 to 11.84. Alpha would collapse to 1.2 × (1 − 7.70 / 11.84) = 1.2 × (1 − 0.650) = 1.2 × 0.350 = 0.42. With several unreversed items alpha can even fall below zero, which is impossible for a true reliability and is always a sign that something is wrong with the scoring or the scale. The second check is alpha if item deleted: recompute alpha leaving out each item in turn. Dropping item 6 lowers alpha to 0.81, so item 6 is pulling its weight. Dropping item 4 leaves alpha virtually unchanged (0.874 against 0.870), a hint that item 4, the one with the smallest variance, adds little. Statistical packages print this column, alongside each item's correlation with the total of the other items, as a routine part of item analysis (Chapter 2).

What happens if you use alpha on items scored 0 or 1? For such an item, the variance σ2i is exactly pq, as we showed earlier in this section. So Σσ2i becomes Σpq and the alpha formula turns into the KR-20 formula, term for term. Alpha computed on Nupur's quiz is 0.80, the same number as KR-20 and Hoyt's coefficient. KR-20 is simply alpha for dichotomous items; alpha is KR-20 set free to handle any scoring.

Cronbach never claimed to have discovered the formula alone. Hoyt's analysis of variance (1941) and Louis Guttman's "lambda 3" (1945), the third of six lower bounds to reliability that Guttman proposed, give the same number, and several other writers working with Fisher's methods had reached it in their own notation. Cronbach's contribution was to state it simply, prove its link to the split halves and show that it applies to any kind of item. Late in life he wrote that "it is an embarrassment to me that the formula became conventionally known as 'Cronbach's α'", and recalled the old joke that "deriving K-R 20 in new ways is the second favorite indoor sport of psychometricians". He had called it alpha because he expected it to be the first of a family of coefficients: "alpha was only the beginning".

Key study: Cronbach's "Coefficient alpha and the internal structure of tests" (1951)

The question. By 1950 testers had a jumble of internal consistency formulas, each derived from its own set of assumptions, and no agreement on what any of them meant. Cronbach asked whether one general coefficient could replace them. The work. A long and densely argued paper in Psychometrika (volume 16, pages 297 to 334). The findings. One formula, alpha, applies to items scored in any way; it equals KR-20 when items are scored 0 or 1; and it equals the average of all the split-half coefficients a test could yield, so it removes the arbitrariness of choosing a split. The impact. Alpha became the default reliability statistic of psychology, and the paper has been cited tens of thousands of times. The second thoughts. In "My current thoughts on coefficient alpha and successor procedures", prepared with the editorial help of Richard Shavelson and published after his death in Educational and Psychological Measurement (2004), Cronbach remarked drily that the paper's many citations did not mean that the people citing it had read it, and wrote: "I no longer regard the alpha formula as the most appropriate way to examine most data." He argued that the standard error of measurement (Section 3.7), not a coefficient, "is the most important single piece of information to report regarding an instrument", and that alpha is best seen as one small case of generalizability theory (Section 3.9).

Exam trap

KR-20 differs from alpha in that KR-20 is only for dichotomously scored items (correct or incorrect, yes or no); alpha handles those and multi-point items too. KR-20 certainly can estimate internal consistency (that is all it does), it is not identical to alpha in use (alpha is the general case), and it is not "only for personality items": it suits any right-wrong ability test as well as any yes-no inventory. Alpha is identical to KR-20 when every item is scored 0 or 1. It is not identical "when the test is a speed test" (no single-sitting coefficient should be used then), "when the test is given twice" (that is test-retest) or "when the two halves have equal variances" (that is the condition under which Spearman-Brown and the Flanagan-Rulon formulas agree, Section 3.4).

Alpha as the average of every possible split

Cronbach's most quoted result connects alpha back to the problem this section began with. He proved that alpha is the mean of all the split-half coefficients you would get by dividing the items into two equal halves in every possible way, when each split-half coefficient is computed with the Flanagan-Rulon formula of Section 3.4, r = 2[1 − (σ2a + σ2b) / σ2X], where σ2a and σ2b are the variances of the two half-scores. Sixty-nine billion splits of the VRT are far too many to check, but a four-item test has only three, so we can watch the theorem at work with the numbers in Figure 3.10.

The variance of a half made of two items is found exactly as Figure 3.10 found the variance of the whole: add the two item variances and twice their covariance. For the split items 1 and 2 against items 3 and 4, the first half has variance 1.0 + 1.2 + 2 × 0.6 = 3.4 and the second 0.8 + 1.0 + 2 × 0.5 = 2.8. Their sum is 6.2, so the coefficient is 2 × (1 − 6.2 / 8.8) = 2 × 0.295 = 0.59. For the split items 1 and 3 against items 2 and 4, the halves have variances 1.0 + 0.8 + 2 × 0.3 = 2.4 and 1.2 + 1.0 + 2 × 0.2 = 2.6; their sum is 5.0, and the coefficient is 2 × (1 − 5.0 / 8.8) = 2 × 0.432 = 0.86. For the split items 1 and 4 against items 2 and 3, both halves have variance 2.8 (1.0 + 1.0 + 0.8, and 1.2 + 0.8 + 0.8); their sum is 5.6, and the coefficient is 2 × (1 − 5.6 / 8.8) = 2 × 0.364 = 0.73.

Three honest splits of one small test give 0.59, 0.86 and 0.73. Which is right? The spread is the arbitrariness Cronbach complained of. The first split happened to put the two most closely related pairs of items (1 with 2, covariance 0.6, and 3 with 4, covariance 0.5) in the same halves, so each half was internally tight but the two halves had little in common; the second split separated those pairs, and the halves looked alike. Now average them: (0.591 + 0.864 + 0.727) / 3 = 2.182 / 3 = 0.727. And alpha, computed directly from the whole square in Figure 3.10, is (4/3) × (1 − 4.0 / 8.8) = 1.333 × 0.545 = 0.727. Identical. Notice one more detail: in the third split the halves have equal variances (2.8 and 2.8), and for that split the Spearman-Brown step-up of the half-test correlation gives the same 0.73 as the Flanagan-Rulon formula, exactly as Section 3.4 predicts. When the half variances differ, the two disagree slightly, which is why the theorem is stated for the Flanagan-Rulon coefficients.

Why did Cronbach care about averaging? Because any single split is a sample of one from the population of possible splits, and like any sample of one it can be unlucky. Averaging over every split removes the luck of the split and leaves a single, stable value that does not depend on how the tester happened to divide the items. The aim was accuracy. It also protects against a quieter abuse: a tester who tries several splits and reports the highest is capitalising on chance, and alpha leaves nothing to choose.

Exam trap

"What is the purpose of averaging several splits, according to Cronbach?" To increase the accuracy of the reliability estimate, by removing its dependence on one arbitrary split. Averaging does not increase the number of dimensions in the test, does not reduce the number of items (the test is unchanged), and certainly does not decrease accuracy. Remember the exact statement: alpha is the mean of all possible split-half coefficients.

Standardised alpha and the number of items

Alpha can also be written in terms of how strongly the items correlate with one another. If every item is first converted to a standard score (so that every item has a variance of 1), alpha becomes standardised alpha, and it depends on just two things: the number of items and the mean inter-item correlation, the average of the correlations between every pair of items.

Remember

αstandardised = k r̄ / [1 + (k − 1) r̄]

k is the number of items and r̄ ("r bar") is the mean inter-item correlation. This is the Spearman-Brown formula of Section 3.4, with the correlation between two single items playing the part of the reliability of a one-item test and k playing the part of the length factor. Standardised alpha equals ordinary (raw) alpha when all the items have the same variance and is usually close to it otherwise; it is the version to use when items are measured on different scales.

Put some numbers through it. Ten items with a mean inter-item correlation of 0.30 give 10 × 0.30 / (1 + 9 × 0.30) = 3.0 / 3.7 = 0.81. Twenty such items give 6.0 / 6.7 = 0.90. Now take forty items whose correlations average only 0.10, items that each share barely one percent of their variance with any other: 40 × 0.10 / (1 + 39 × 0.10) = 4.0 / 4.9 = 0.82. Forty weak items reach the same alpha as ten good ones. Going the other way, a three-item subscale with respectable items (r̄ = 0.40) manages only 1.2 / 1.8 = 0.67.

Figure 3.12 draws the whole family of curves. It carries one of the most important lessons in this chapter: alpha rises with the number of items even when the items are no more closely related. A high alpha can therefore come from strongly related items or simply from many items, and alpha alone cannot tell you which. Jose Cortina made this point forcefully in 1993 in "What is coefficient alpha? An examination of theory and applications" (Journal of Applied Psychology), showing with hypothetical tests that long scales can exceed 0.70 even when their items correlate weakly or measure more than one thing. For that reason many scale builders look at the mean inter-item correlation as well as alpha; Lee Anna Clark and David Watson (1995) recommended a mean inter-item correlation of roughly 0.15 to 0.50, towards the lower end for a broad trait such as extraversion and towards the upper end for a narrow one.

Exam trap

Alpha is not the average correlation between items. It depends on both the mean inter-item correlation and the number of items, so a long test of weakly related items can have a high alpha, and a short subscale of good items a modest one. Adding items of the same quality raises alpha, just as the Spearman-Brown formula predicts; that is why a two- or three-item subscale rarely reaches 0.70.

What alpha measures, and what it does not

No question in this chapter is asked more often than "Cronbach's alpha is a measure of what?" It arrives in a dozen disguises: "is a measure of", "is an index of", "assesses", "is most commonly used to estimate", "is a measure of which type of reliability". The answer never changes. Alpha measures internal consistency reliability: the extent to which the items of a multi-item scale, answered on one occasion, agree with one another. It is a reliability coefficient, so it describes the consistency of the scores and not their meaning; and it is the internal consistency kind of reliability, so it describes agreement among items, not agreement across occasions, forms or scorers. The phrase "of a multi-item scale" that some options add is a clue, not a trap: internal consistency needs several items, and alpha is used precisely for scales made of many.

The examiners surround that answer with the names of everything alpha is not. Each wrong option is a genuine concept that is the right answer somewhere else, and next year one of them will be the key to a different question, so learn them as a set (Table 3.21).

Table 3.21 The options that sit beside "internal consistency" in questions about Cronbach's alpha: what each really is, how it is estimated and where it is taught.
Option offeredWhat it really isHow it is estimatedTaught in
Validity (in general)Whether scores mean what they are claimed to mean and support the uses made of themEvidence of several kinds: content, criterion, constructChapter 4
Criterion validity: concurrent or predictiveAgreement with an outside criterion measured now (concurrent) or later (predictive)Correlating the test with the criterion, such as a diagnosis or later gradesChapter 4
Construct validityWhether the test measures the psychological construct it was built forConvergent and discriminant correlations, factor analysis, group differencesChapter 4
Test-retest reliabilityStability of scores over timeCorrelating the same test given twice to the same peopleSection 3.2
Parallel forms reliabilityEquivalence of two versions of a testCorrelating Form A with Form BSection 3.3
Inter-rater reliabilityAgreement between scorers or observersPercentage agreement, kappa, intraclass correlationSection 3.6
VarianceThe spread of scores around their mean; an ingredient of alpha, not what it measuresThe average squared deviation from the meanResearch Methodology book
RegressionPredicting one variable from another with a line or equationA regression equation fitted to the dataResearch Methodology book

Two of these deserve a further word. Alpha is not validity of any kind, because a scale can be beautifully consistent and still measure the wrong thing. Twenty items that all ask, in slightly different words, whether a student enjoys mathematics will have a high alpha, but offered as a "test of mathematical ability" they measure liking, not ability. Reliability is necessary for validity but never sufficient (Section 3.1; Section 3.8 shows the ceiling it places on validity). And variance is not what alpha measures but what alpha is made of: the formula is a ratio of variances, in the same way that a recipe is not the same thing as one of its ingredients.

Exam trap

Whatever the wording, Cronbach's alpha measures internal consistency reliability (also written "internal consistency of a multi-item scale" or "reliability (internal consistency)"). Strike out every option containing the word validity (plain validity, criterion, concurrent, predictive or construct validity), every other kind of reliability (test-retest, parallel forms, inter-rater), and the statistical terms variance and regression. If two options both mention reliability, choose the one that says internal consistency. The same reasoning answers "KR-20 measures": internal consistency, for right-wrong items.

How high should alpha be?

An alpha of 0.87 sounds good and 0.40 sounds bad, but where exactly are the lines? The most quoted standards come from Jum Nunnally's textbook Psychometric Theory (second edition, 1978), repeated in the third edition written with Ira Bernstein (1994). They apply to any reliability coefficient, not to alpha alone, and they depend on what the scores will be used for. In the early stages of research, while you are still finding out whether a construct is worth measuring, reliabilities of 0.70 or higher will do. For basic research comparing groups, about 0.80 is the target, and pushing much beyond it is often a waste of time and money, because at that level measurement error weakens the correlations a researcher computes only a little. But where important decisions are made about individual people on the basis of their scores, 0.90 is the minimum that should be tolerated, and 0.95 the desirable standard. Section 3.8 sets these standards out for every kind of reliability coefficient. Remember them as "0.70 to explore, 0.80 to compare groups, 0.90 to decide about a person".

A second set of labels circulates in statistics guides and mock-paper explanations: 0.90 and above "excellent", 0.80 to 0.89 "good", 0.70 to 0.79 "acceptable", 0.60 to 0.69 "questionable", 0.50 to 0.59 "poor" and below 0.50 "unacceptable". It is a rule of thumb rather than a law, but it explains the wording of many questions.

So what does an alpha of 0.40 mean? On either scheme it is poor. The items share little: knowing a person's answer to one item tells you not much about the next. There are three usual reasons, and the cure depends on which applies. The items may measure different things, so the scale needs splitting into subscales; some items may be badly written, ambiguous or wrongly keyed, which item analysis will expose, as it exposed Amala's unreversed item; or there may simply be too few items (Figure 3.12). An alpha of 0.40 is nowhere near acceptable for high-stakes clinical use, where about 0.90 is the floor; it is certainly not "excellent"; and it says nothing at all about construct validity, good or bad, because alpha is not a validity coefficient.

Can alpha be too high? Surprisingly, yes. Imagine a loneliness scale whose items read "I feel lonely", "I often feel lonely", "I feel alone" and "Loneliness is something I feel a lot". People answer them almost identically, alpha soars above 0.95, and the scale is no better for it. It measures one narrow sliver of loneliness over and over again while missing the rest of the construct: missing particular friends, feeling left out, having nobody to confide in. Very high alphas from short scales often signal redundant, near-duplicate items rather than an excellent test. Raymond Bernard Cattell (1905 to 1998) called the narrow factor that a cluster of near-synonymous items produces a bloated specific: impressive in its consistency, poor in its coverage. The exception is a long test built for decisions about individuals, where an alpha above 0.90 comes from many different items, each sampling a different corner of the domain, and is exactly what you want.

Exam trap

A Cronbach's alpha of 0.40 indicates poor internal consistency. It is not acceptable for high-stakes clinical use (decisions about an individual need about 0.90 or more), not excellent (that label starts at about 0.90), and not evidence of high construct validity (alpha is not a validity coefficient at all). At the other extreme, an alpha above about 0.95 on a short scale is not automatically better: it may mean redundant items.

What alpha cannot tell you: dimensionality, lower bounds and omega

The title of Cronbach's paper spoke of "the internal structure of tests", and generations of researchers have read a high alpha as proof that a scale measures one thing. It is not. Kartik, a postgraduate student in Lucknow, writes a 20-item "student wellbeing" questionnaire. Without realising it, he has written two unrelated scales: ten items about sleep habits, which correlate 0.50 with one another, and ten items about friendships, which also correlate 0.50 with one another, while every sleep item correlates zero with every friendship item. Count the pairs. Twenty items make 20 × 19 / 2 = 190 pairs. The 45 pairs within the sleep items and the 45 within the friendship items, 90 in all, correlate 0.50; the other 100 pairs correlate zero. So the mean inter-item correlation is (90 × 0.50) / 190 = 45 / 190 = 0.237, and standardised alpha is 20 × 0.237 / (1 + 19 × 0.237) = 4.74 / 5.50 = 0.86. Two completely separate scales glued together earn a "good" alpha, because twenty items are enough to lift a mean correlation of 0.24 to respectability. (Each ten-item half, scored on its own, would have an alpha of 5.0 / 5.5 = 0.91.)

Cortina's 1993 paper and Neal Schmitt's "Uses and abuses of coefficient alpha" (Psychological Assessment, 1996) drove the lesson home. Alpha measures the interrelatedness of the items, and interrelatedness is not the same as unidimensionality, the property of measuring a single underlying trait. A set of items can be well interrelated on average and still fall into two or three separate clusters, as Kartik's did. Whether a scale is unidimensional is a question for factor analysis (Chapters 2 and 4), not for alpha. Schmitt added a practical warning: when a measure is multidimensional, using its alpha to correct correlations for unreliability (Section 3.8) gives corrected values that are too high.

The second misunderstanding concerns what alpha estimates. Alpha equals the true reliability of the total score only when the items meet a condition with a forbidding name, essential tau-equivalence. In plain words: every item must reflect the same true score equally strongly (items may differ in difficulty, which only shifts their scores up or down by a constant, but each must measure the trait to the same degree), and the items' errors must be unrelated to one another. Melvin Novick and Charles Lewis proved in 1967 that, provided the errors are unrelated, alpha can never exceed the true reliability, and equals it only under essential tau-equivalence. Real items rarely measure a trait equally well; some are better indicators than others, and items like that are called congeneric. Then alpha is a lower bound to reliability: it underestimates, usually by a little. The guarantee, however, rests on unrelated errors. When items share error, because they share an odd wording, sit together in a block or run out of time together on a speeded test, alpha can overestimate reliability instead.

The modern alternative is McDonald's omega (ω), set out by Roderick McDonald in Test Theory: A Unified Treatment (1999). Omega is computed from a factor analysis in which each item has its own loading (λ, lambda: how strongly the item reflects the common factor) and its own error variance (θ, theta).

Remember

ω = (Σλ)2 / [(Σλ)2 + Σθ]

Σλ is the sum of the items' factor loadings, so (Σλ)2 is the part of the total-score variance that comes from the common factor; Σθ is the sum of the items' error variances. Omega is the share of total-score variance due to the common factor, and it does not assume that the items measure the factor equally well.

Take four standardised items with loadings of 0.8, 0.7, 0.6 and 0.5. The loadings add to 2.6, and 2.6 squared is 6.76. Each item's error variance is 1 minus its squared loading: 0.36, 0.51, 0.64 and 0.75, which add to 2.26. So ω = 6.76 / (6.76 + 2.26) = 6.76 / 9.02 = 0.75. What would alpha say about the same items? In this model the correlation between two items is the product of their loadings (0.8 × 0.7 = 0.56, and so on), so the six inter-item correlations are 0.56, 0.48, 0.40, 0.42, 0.35 and 0.30, their mean is 2.51 / 6 = 0.418, and standardised alpha is 4 × 0.418 / (1 + 3 × 0.418) = 1.673 / 2.255 = 0.74. Alpha comes in just under omega, as the lower-bound result says it must. Had all four loadings been equal (0.65 each), the items would be tau-equivalent, and alpha and omega would agree exactly at 0.75. In practice the gap is usually small, but omega is increasingly reported beside alpha, and some psychometricians, notably Klaas Sijtsma in a 2009 Psychometrika paper pointedly titled "On the use, the misuse, and the very limited usefulness of Cronbach's alpha", argue that alpha should give way to better estimates altogether.

Cronbach himself would not have been surprised. As the Key study box above recounts, his last paper pointed past alpha to the standard error of measurement (Section 3.7) and to generalizability theory, which separates the error due to items, occasions and scorers instead of lumping it together (Section 3.9). It also named one case in which alpha "simply should not be used": a test given with a time limit so tight that many people never reach the last items, the speed-test problem of Section 3.4.

Exam trap

A high alpha does not prove that a test is unidimensional (two unrelated ten-item clusters gave 0.86), does not prove validity, and does not show that the items correlate strongly (length alone can raise it). With unrelated item errors, alpha is a lower bound to reliability, equal to it only under essential tau-equivalence; with correlated errors or speeding it can overestimate. McDonald's omega, computed from a factor analysis, drops the assumption that all items measure the trait equally well.

Choosing an internal consistency coefficient

Table 3.22 brings together the single-administration methods of Sections 3.4 and 3.5, and it can be read as a decision guide. If the items are scored right or wrong and you have item-by-item data, use KR-20, or alpha, which gives the same number. If you have only a mark sheet, KR-21 gives a quick floor. If the items have more than two score points, use alpha, and if you doubt that they measure the trait equally well, report omega beside it. Split-half methods remain useful when a test falls naturally into two comparable halves or when, as on a speed test, the halves must be timed separately. For Rohini's VRT the single-sitting estimates were 0.82 (odd-even split, stepped up), 0.84 (KR-20, and alpha) and 0.75 (KR-21); Section 3.8 sets them beside her retest coefficient of 0.80 and explains why each method gives its own value. Whatever you compute, remember the limitation all these methods share: one sitting tells you nothing about stability over time.

Table 3.22 The single-administration reliability methods compared.
MethodItem scoringWhat you needMain assumptionTypical use
Split-half with Spearman-Brown (Section 3.4)AnyThe correlation between two half-scoresThe halves are parallel: equal means and variances, same contentQuick estimate for a power test; separately timed halves for a speed test
Rulon or Flanagan-Guttman (Section 3.4)AnyVariances of the two halves, or of their difference, and of the totalsThe halves measure the same thing; their variances need not be equalSplit-half when the halves differ in spread; no step-up needed
KR-20Dichotomous (1 or 0)p for every item and the variance of the totalsItems sample one domain; test not speededAbility and achievement tests; true-false and yes-no inventories
KR-21Dichotomous (1 or 0)Number of items, mean and variance of the totalsAll items equally difficult; otherwise it underestimatesA quick, conservative check from a mark register
Coefficient alpha (and Hoyt's ANOVA)Any number of score pointsThe variance of every item and of the totalsItems measure the trait equally well; errors unrelated; not speededLikert, rating and partial-credit scales: the general case
Indian context

When a scale written in English for students elsewhere is translated into Hindi, Tamil or Kannada and tried on Indian students, its published alpha does not travel with it. A translated item can drift in meaning, an idiom can fall flat, and a group with a narrower or wider spread of scores gives a different coefficient (Section 3.8). That is why Indian adaptation studies and test manuals report alpha, or KR-20 for right-wrong tests, afresh in the new sample, usually scale by scale and alongside the item-total correlations used to spot items that did not survive translation. When you read such a paper, check three things: which sample the coefficient came from, whether it is given for each subscale or only for the total, and whether the items were dichotomous (KR-20) or graded (alpha).

Clinical link

Two habits protect your patients. First, check the alpha of the score you are actually interpreting. A personality inventory may report 0.90 for its total, yet one of its six-item subscales may sit near 0.65, and the subscale is the score on which you are about to write "markedly elevated". Below about 0.90, treat one person's score as a rough indication and look at its band of error (Section 3.7). Second, ask whether the items are supposed to agree at all. A checklist of stressful life events (a death in the family, the loss of a job, a move to a new city) adds up separate happenings that need not go together; a low alpha there is no flaw, and the right reliability question is whether people report the same events on a second occasion (Section 3.2). Alpha belongs to scales whose items are all meant to reflect one underlying state or trait, such as a depression or anxiety questionnaire.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  7. Cronbach showed that alpha is the mean of all possible coefficients, which makes the estimate more accurate than any single split.

Answers: (1) internal consistency, (2) 0.24 (0.40 × 0.60), (3) dichotomously (right or wrong, 1 or 0); more than two, (4) difficulty; lower, (5) 0.86 (2.8 / 14 = 0.20; 1 − 0.20 = 0.80; 15 / 14 = 1.071; 1.071 × 0.80 = 0.857, rounded to 0.86), (6) analysis of variance, (7) split-half, (8) items, (9) 0.90 (0.95 desirable), (10) unidimensional.

3.6 Inter-Rater and Scorer ReliabilityMedium yield · 5 Qs

Every method in Sections 3.2 to 3.5 treated the score as something the test produces on its own: the student circles an option, the key says right or wrong, and any clerk or machine would produce the same number. For many psychological measures that is not true. Between the response and the number stands a second person, the scorer, who must decide what an essay is worth, whether a Rorschach response uses the colour of the blot, how agitated a patient looked, or whether a child's screening record is worrying enough to refer. Whenever judgement enters, the scorer becomes one more source of error, and the opening of this chapter showed how large it can be: Starch and Elliott's teachers gave the same English and geometry papers marks so far apart that one script could pass in one school and fail in the next. This section asks how agreement between scorers is measured, which statistic suits which kind of judgement, and how agreement can be raised.

When the scorer is part of the measurement

Recall the true-score model of Section 3.1: an obtained score is the true score plus error, X = T + E, and one of the sources of random error listed there was scoring. If two qualified examiners mark the same script 62 and 71, at least one number contains scorer error, and the student's score depends partly on whose desk the script landed on. Scorer reliability is the consistency of scores across scorers: the degree to which different people, scoring the same responses independently by the same rules, arrive at the same scores (A. K. Singh adds the same scorer on two occasions).

Several names are used for this family of ideas, often interchangeably. Inter-rater reliability is the degree to which two or more raters (scorers, judges, clinicians) who independently judge the same performance give the same scores or categories. When the raters are watching behaviour as it happens, the same idea is called inter-observer agreement (Book 2, Section 2.8.7). Intra-rater reliability is the consistency of one rater with himself: a psychologist rescores a set of protocols a few weeks later without looking at his first scores. A rater who cannot agree with himself cannot be expected to agree with anyone else.

The opposite of judgement-based scoring is objective scoring. A test is objective, in the technical sense, when its scoring requires no judgement at all, so that anyone who applies the key gets the same score. Objectivity is therefore a property of the scoring procedure, not of the content: a multiple-choice personality inventory is as objective as a multiple-choice arithmetic test. Objective scoring removes scorer error almost completely, one reason objective items became popular in the decades after experiments like Starch and Elliott's. But a test scored perfectly by a machine still has content sampling error and time sampling error. Objectivity solves one problem in the error table of Section 3.1, not all of them.

Scorer reliability matters wherever a person must decide what a response is worth: essay examinations and the viva voce; projective techniques such as the Rorschach, the Thematic Apperception Test and projective drawings (Chapter 10); the verbal items of individual intelligence tests, scored 2, 1 or 0 by the examiner (Chapter 6); creativity tests, where someone must judge originality (Chapter 8); rating scales and observation schedules (Chapter 11); and clinical interviews that end in a diagnosis (Book 2, Section 2.10.5).

The RML 2021 paper asked which of four statements describes inter-rater reliability. The answer is "the degree to which two clinicians will agree on interpretation or scoring of a test", and each wrong option names a concept that is the right answer elsewhere. "The degree to which two tests measure the same construct" is convergent validity (Chapter 4), or parallel-forms reliability if the two tests were built as equivalent forms of one test (Section 3.3). "The degree to which a clinician can predict future behaviour" is predictive validity (Chapter 4). "The degree to which the items in the test relate to each other" is internal consistency, measured by KR-20 or coefficient alpha (Section 3.5).

Exam trap

Inter-rater (scorer) reliability is agreement between two or more people who score or interpret the same responses. Two tests measuring one construct is convergent validity (or parallel forms, if the tests are built as equivalent forms); predicting future behaviour is predictive validity; items relating to each other is internal consistency. One rater scoring the same material twice is intra-rater reliability, not inter-rater. And an "objective" test is one whose scoring needs no judgement; objectivity removes scorer error but not content or time sampling error.

Correlating two scorers: consistency is not agreement

When scores are numbers on a continuous scale, such as marks out of 100, the oldest method is the one Singh describes: two scorers mark the same responses independently, and the correlation between their marks is the scorer reliability coefficient. The correlation used is Pearson's r (the Research Methodology book teaches it in full). Here you need two facts: it runs from −1 through 0 to +1, and it measures how closely two sets of scores rise and fall together along a straight line, in the same order and with proportional spacing.

That second fact contains a trap. Rajat and Hiral, two lecturers at a college in Mysuru, independently mark the same five essays out of 100. Rajat gives 58, 64, 71, 76 and 85. Hiral, a stricter marker, gives 48, 54, 61, 66 and 75: exactly 10 marks less on every essay. The correlation between their marks is exactly 1.00, because they put the essays in the same order with the same gaps, and a correlation is blind to a constant shift. Yet on not one essay do they give the same mark. Figure 3.13 shows the five essays on a perfect straight line that runs 10 marks below the diagonal of identical marks.

Does the gap matter? That depends on how the marks are used. To rank the essays for a prize, Rajat and Hiral are interchangeable. But suppose the pass mark is 60. Rajat passes four essays (64, 71, 76 and 85) and fails one; Hiral passes three (61, 66 and 75) and fails two. The second essay passes or fails depending on who marks it. Psychometricians therefore distinguish two kinds of scorer reliability. Consistency (relative agreement) is agreement about the order and spacing of scores, which is all that matters when people are compared with each other. Absolute agreement is agreement about the actual numbers, which matters whenever a score is compared with a fixed standard: a pass mark, a referral cut-off, a clinical threshold, a criterion such as "an IQ below 70".

Hiral's strictness is a constant error, the same 10 marks on every script: a systematic error of the stretched-tape-measure kind described in Section 3.1, biasing every score without disturbing anyone's position, which is why the correlation cannot see it. A rater error that varies from script to script (generosity to neat handwriting, irritation with long answers) changes the order of the essays and does lower the correlation. To catch both kinds we need a statistic that compares the actual numbers, the intraclass correlation of Section 3.6.6. Pearson's r has a practical limit too: it handles only two scorers at a time.

Exam trap

A high correlation between two raters shows consistency (same order, same spacing), not agreement (same numbers). Two raters who always differ by the same amount correlate perfectly yet never agree. When scores are compared with a cut-off or pass mark, absolute agreement is what counts, and the right statistic is the intraclass correlation for absolute agreement, not Pearson's r. A constant leniency or severity difference between raters is a systematic error: invisible to r, but very visible to the student who fails.

Agreement on categories: percentage agreement and Cohen's kappa

Many clinical judgements are categories, not numbers. Is a diagnosis present? Should this child be referred? Is this Rorschach response scored for the whole blot or a detail? The obvious index is percentage agreement: the number of agreements divided by the number of cases, times 100. Book 2, Section 2.8.7 showed its versions in behavioural observation (interval-by-interval, total count and occurrence agreement) and the convention that it should reach at least 80 percent. Its weakness is that it gives raters credit for agreements they would have reached by luck.

Imagine two raters who never look at the cases and simply say "do not refer" for most children, because most children are developing normally. Their "no" decisions will often land on the same child, and they will agree on a large share of cases without having judged anything. The rarer one category is, the larger this chance agreement becomes, so a raw agreement of 85 percent can mean excellent judgement or very little.

The American psychologist and statistician Jacob Cohen (1923 to 1998), the same Cohen whose effect-size index d you will meet in the Research Methodology book, solved the problem in a 1960 paper in Educational and Psychological Measurement, "A coefficient of agreement for nominal scales". He asked a sharper question: of all the agreement possible above what chance would give, how much did the raters achieve? The result is Cohen's kappa (κ). In words: take the agreement reached, subtract the agreement chance alone would produce, and divide by the most agreement above chance that was available.

Remember

κ = (po − pe) / (1 − pe)

where po is the observed proportion of agreement (agreements divided by cases), pe is the proportion of agreement expected by chance from how often each rater uses each category, and 1 − pe is the largest possible agreement beyond chance. Kappa is 1 for perfect agreement, 0 when agreement is exactly what chance would give, and negative when the raters agree less often than chance.

Book 2, Section 2.10.5 worked a kappa for two clinicians diagnosing depression, with a second example showing how kappa falls when a diagnosis is rare. Here is a new case, from screening, in which the two raters do not use the categories equally often, as usually happens. Gargi and Bhuvan, school psychologists in a programme in government primary schools near Mysuru, independently go through the same 100 children's screening records and decide for each child whether to refer for a full assessment of a possible learning difficulty. Table 3.23 shows their decisions.

Table 3.23 Gargi's and Bhuvan's decisions on the same 100 screening records. The first number in each cell is the observed count; the number in brackets is the count expected by chance (row total × column total / 100).
GargiBhuvan: referBhuvan: do not referGargi's total
Refer15 (chance 5)5 (chance 15)20
Do not refer10 (chance 20)70 (chance 60)80
Bhuvan's total2575100

Push the numbers through in three steps.

  1. Observed agreement. The two psychologists agree on the 15 children they would both refer and the 70 they would both leave alone, so po = (15 + 70) / 100 = 85 / 100 = 0.85. Raw agreement is 85 percent, which sounds excellent and comfortably passes the 80 percent convention.
  2. Agreement expected by chance. Gargi refers 20 of the 100 children, a proportion of 0.20; Bhuvan refers 25, a proportion of 0.25. If their decisions had nothing to do with each other, the chance that both would refer the same child is 0.20 × 0.25 = 0.05, and the chance that both would decline to refer is 0.80 × 0.75 = 0.60. So pe = 0.05 + 0.60 = 0.65. In counts, chance alone would have produced 5 joint referrals and 60 joint non-referrals, 65 agreements out of 100, as the bracketed numbers in Table 3.23 show.
  3. Kappa. κ = (0.85 − 0.65) / (1 − 0.65) = 0.20 / 0.35 = 0.5714, which rounds to 0.57.

In plain words: 65 of the 85 agreements would have happened even if Gargi and Bhuvan had decided at random in their own proportions.
