Above that chance level, 35 percentage points of agreement were available (65 to 100) and they achieved 20 of them (65 to 85); 20 out of 35 is 57 percent. An impressive 85 percent agreement becomes a kappa of 0.57, only moderate (Section 3.6.4). The table also shows where the trouble lies: of the 15 disagreements, 10 are children Bhuvan would refer and Gargi would not. Bhuvan uses the referral category more readily, and a meeting at which the two compare those 15 records and agree on what counts as "worrying" would probably raise their kappa more than anything else.

Kappa assumes that the same two raters judge every case, independently; that the categories are mutually exclusive and exhaustive; and that they are nominal, so every disagreement counts as equally serious (wasteful for ordered categories, Section 3.6.5). Kappa can also measure agreement between two occasions, as in diagnostic test-retest studies (Book 2, Section 2.10.5), but its home and its exam identity is agreement between raters.

Exam trap

Cohen's kappa (Jacob Cohen, 1960) measures inter-rater agreement on categorical (nominal) judgements, corrected for chance agreement. It is not test-retest reliability (usually Pearson's r between two occasions), not internal consistency (Cronbach's alpha), not criterion, predictive or convergent validity (correlations with other measures, Chapter 4), and not an effect size for analysis of variance (that is eta squared; Cohen's d, the other famous index named after the same Jacob Cohen, is an effect size for the difference between two means). Kappa is always lower than percentage agreement when any chance agreement is possible, so a high percentage can hide a modest kappa.

How much agreement is enough? Interpreting kappa

A kappa of 0.57: is that good? Kappa has no natural meaning beyond its two anchors (1 is perfect, 0 is chance), so researchers rely on conventions. The most quoted are those of the biostatisticians J. Richard Landis and Gary Koch, in a 1977 paper in Biometrics, "The measurement of observer agreement for categorical data". They attached a verbal label to each range of kappa, and they were candid that the divisions were arbitrary, offered as convenient benchmarks rather than as laws. The statistician Joseph Fleiss (1937 to 2003) later proposed a simpler three-band scheme, in the 1981 edition of his book Statistical Methods for Rates and Proportions. Table 3.24 sets the two side by side.

Table 3.24 Two conventions for interpreting kappa. The cut-offs differ slightly, so read each column on its own.
KappaLandis and Koch (1977)Fleiss (1981)
Below 0Poor (less than chance)Poor
0.00 to 0.20SlightPoor
0.21 to 0.40FairPoor (below 0.40)
0.41 to 0.60ModerateFair to good (0.40 to 0.75)
0.61 to 0.75SubstantialFair to good
0.76 to 0.80SubstantialExcellent (above 0.75)
0.81 to 1.00Almost perfectExcellent

A practice paper asked what a kappa of 0.85 between two clinicians indicates. It falls in the Landis and Koch band 0.81 to 1.00, so the answer is "almost perfect agreement" (Fleiss would say excellent); poor, fair and moderate are the labels for values below 0, 0.21 to 0.40 and 0.41 to 0.60. Gargi and Bhuvan's 0.57 is moderate, or fair to good on Fleiss's scale.

Two cautions keep these labels in their place. First, kappa depends on more than the raters' skill. It falls when one category is rare, because chance agreement on the common category is then high (the base-rate effect worked in Book 2, Section 2.10.5), and it is affected by how differently the raters use the categories, as Bhuvan's extra referrals showed; a kappa from a specialist clinic cannot be compared directly with one from a school screening. Second, the labels do not say whether agreement is good enough for a purpose: moderate agreement may do for deciding who gets a second look, but not for a diagnosis that decides treatment (Book 3, Section 1.6.6).

Exam trap

Learn the Landis and Koch bands exactly: below 0 poor, 0 to 0.20 slight, 0.21 to 0.40 fair, 0.41 to 0.60 moderate, 0.61 to 0.80 substantial, 0.81 to 1.00 almost perfect. A kappa of 0.85 is "almost perfect", not "excellent" (that is Fleiss's word, used above 0.75) and not "substantial" (0.61 to 0.80). A kappa of 0 means chance-level agreement, not "no agreement at all": two raters at chance still agree on many cases.

Ordered categories and more than two raters: weighted kappa and Fleiss' kappa

Ordinary kappa treats all disagreements alike. That is right for unordered categories such as different diagnoses, but wasteful when the categories are ordered. If two clinicians rate depression as mild, moderate or severe, "mild" against "moderate" is a near miss and "mild" against "severe" a bad disagreement, yet ordinary kappa scores both the same. In 1968 Cohen published the remedy in Psychological Bulletin: weighted kappa, which gives each cell of the agreement table a weight between 0 and 1 according to how far apart the two ratings are, and then applies the kappa formula to the weighted observed and chance agreements.

Madhuri and Rajat, two clinical psychologists at a hospital outpatient department, independently watch the same 50 recorded assessment interviews and rate each client's depression as mild, moderate or severe. Table 3.25 shows their ratings.

Table 3.25 Madhuri's and Rajat's severity ratings of the same 50 clients. The diagonal cells (14, 12 and 8) are exact agreements; cells next to the diagonal are near misses one category apart; the two corner cells are two categories apart.
MadhuriRajat: mildRajat: moderateRajat: severeMadhuri's total
Mild145120
Moderate312318
Severe04812
Rajat's total17211250

First the ordinary kappa. Exact agreements are 14 + 12 + 8 = 34, so po = 34 / 50 = 0.68. Chance agreement in each diagonal cell is the product of the two raters' proportions for that category: (20 × 17 + 18 × 21 + 12 × 12) / (50 × 50) = (340 + 378 + 144) / 2500 = 862 / 2500 = 0.3448. So κ = (0.68 − 0.3448) / (1 − 0.3448) = 0.3352 / 0.6552 = 0.51.

Now give partial credit. With the simplest, linear, weights an exact agreement earns 1, a disagreement of one category earns 0.5 and a disagreement of two categories earns 0. Madhuri and Rajat have 34 exact agreements, 15 near misses (5 + 3 + 3 + 4) and 1 disagreement of two categories (the client Madhuri called mild and Rajat severe). Their weighted observed agreement is (34 × 1 + 15 × 0.5 + 1 × 0) / 50 = (34 + 7.5) / 50 = 41.5 / 50 = 0.83. The weighted chance agreement is worked out the same way from the chance counts (row total × column total / 50). Chance would put 6.8 + 7.56 + 2.88 = 17.24 cases on the diagonal and 8.4 + 6.12 + 4.32 + 5.04 = 23.88 cases one step off it, so the weighted chance agreement is (17.24 × 1 + 23.88 × 0.5) / 50 = (17.24 + 11.94) / 50 = 29.18 / 50 = 0.5836. Then weighted κ = (0.83 − 0.5836) / (1 − 0.5836) = 0.2464 / 0.4164 = 0.59.

Weighted kappa (0.59) is higher than ordinary kappa (0.51) because most of the two psychologists' disagreements were near misses, and weighted kappa gives them half credit. With quadratic weights, which shrink the penalty for small disagreements even further (a near miss earns 0.75 instead of 0.5 here), the same table gives 0.68. Because the choice of weights changes the answer, a report must say which weights were used. A useful fact for later reading: with quadratic weights, weighted kappa is essentially equivalent to the intraclass correlation of Section 3.6.6, as Fleiss and Cohen showed in 1973.

Cohen's kappa, weighted or not, compares exactly two raters. For more, such as five psychiatrists diagnosing the same 30 patients, Fleiss published in 1971 (again in Psychological Bulletin) a generalisation now called Fleiss' kappa. It keeps the logic of observed agreement corrected for chance, and it even allows different raters for different cases, provided each case gets the same number of judgements. You need to know what it is for, not how to compute it.

Exam trap

Match the statistic to the data. Cohen's kappa: two raters, nominal categories. Weighted kappa (Cohen, 1968): two raters, ordered categories, partial credit for near misses (linear or quadratic weights). Fleiss' kappa (1971): three or more raters, nominal categories. Intraclass correlation: continuous scores, two or more raters. Kendall's W: several judges producing rankings. "Fleiss' kappa for two raters" and "Cohen's kappa for ten raters" are wrong options.

Continuous ratings: the intraclass correlation coefficient

Go back to Rajat and Hiral, who correlated perfectly yet never gave the same mark. We need a coefficient that treats the two lecturers not as two variables to be correlated but as two interchangeable measurements of one thing, the quality of each essay. That coefficient is the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC). Pearson's r is an interclass correlation, between two different classes of measurement such as height and weight; the ICC is a correlation within one class, among repeated measurements of the same thing.

The ICC is a reliability coefficient in exactly the sense of Section 3.1: the proportion of the variance in the scores that is true variance. The "true" part is the variance between the things rated (essays, patients, children); the error part is everything else, namely differences between raters in general strictness and the leftover disagreements that change from essay to essay.

Remember

ICC (absolute agreement, one rater) = σ2targets / (σ2targets + σ2raters + σ2error)

ICC (consistency, one rater) = σ2targets / (σ2targets + σ2error)

where σ2targets is the variance of the true differences between the people or products rated, σ2raters is the variance due to raters being generally stricter or more lenient, and σ2error is the leftover, inconsistent disagreement. The consistency form leaves the rater variance out, which is why it cannot see a constant difference between raters.

In real data the three variance pieces are estimated by analysis of variance, which the Research Methodology book teaches; the same pieces return in generalizability theory in Section 3.9. Rajat and Hiral's tidy example lets you see them with simple arithmetic, because Hiral's marks are exactly Rajat's minus 10 and there is no leftover error at all. Work through it.

  1. Variance between essays. Average the two marks for each essay: (58 + 48) / 2 = 53, then 59, 66, 71 and 80. The mean of these five averages, the grand mean, is 65.8. The deviations from it are −12.8, −6.8, 0.2, 5.2 and 14.2; their squares are 163.84, 46.24, 0.04, 27.04 and 201.64, which add up to 438.8. Dividing by one less than the number of essays (5 − 1 = 4) gives 109.7.
  2. Variance due to raters. Rajat's average mark is 70.8 and Hiral's is 60.8, each 5 marks from the grand mean of 65.8. The squared deviations are 25 and 25, which add up to 50; dividing by one less than the number of raters (2 − 1 = 1) gives 50.
  3. Leftover error. Once the essay differences and Hiral's constant 10 marks are allowed for, nothing is left over: every mark is predicted exactly. So σ2error = 0.
  4. The two ICCs. Consistency: 109.7 / (109.7 + 0) = 1.00, just like Pearson's r. Absolute agreement: 109.7 / (109.7 + 50 + 0) = 109.7 / 159.7 = 0.69.

The two answers say what Figure 3.13 showed. As judges of which essay is better, the lecturers are perfectly consistent; as sources of the actual mark, they agree only moderately, because almost a third of the variance in the marks (50 out of 159.7) comes from who did the marking.

These ICCs describe a single lecturer's mark. If the college averages the two marks, the rater and error variances are shared between two people and divided by 2: 109.7 / (109.7 + 50 / 2) = 109.7 / 134.7 = 0.81. This is the average-measures ICC, and it is the Spearman-Brown formula of Section 3.4 applied to raters instead of items: with the unrounded single-rater value of 0.687, 2 × 0.687 / (1 + 0.687) = 1.374 / 1.687 = 0.81, the same answer.

Because there are several questions one can ask, there are several ICCs, and using the wrong one is a common error in published research. Patrick Shrout and Fleiss set out the main forms in 1979 in Psychological Bulletin, "Intraclass correlations: uses in assessing rater reliability". They distinguished three designs, each with a single-rater and an average-rater version, giving six forms written ICC(1,1), ICC(2,1), ICC(3,1) and ICC(1,k), ICC(2,k), ICC(3,k), where the first number is the design and the second says whether the coefficient refers to one rater (1) or to the average of k raters. Kenneth McGraw and S. P. Wong (1996, Psychological Methods) made the choice between consistency and absolute agreement explicit within the two-way designs, and later guidelines count ten forms in all. Table 3.26 puts the main ones in plain words.

Table 3.26 The main forms of the intraclass correlation (after Shrout and Fleiss, 1979, and McGraw and Wong, 1996).
FormDesign in plain wordsRater differences counted as error?Typical use
ICC(1,1), ICC(1,k): one-way randomEach person is rated by a different set of ratersYes; rater and error variance cannot be separatedMulti-site studies where whoever is on duty does the rating
ICC(2,1), ICC(2,k): two-way randomThe same raters rate everyone and stand for all raters who might be usedYes, in the absolute-agreement formShowing that any trained rater would give the same score
ICC(3,1), ICC(3,k): two-way mixedThe same raters rate everyone and are the only raters of interestNo, in the consistency formRanking within one study rated by a fixed team
Single (1) versus average (k)One rater's score versus the mean of k raters' scoresAverage form is higher unless agreement is perfectReport the form that matches how scores will be used

How large should an ICC be? Terry Koo and Mae Li, in a widely used 2016 guideline in the Journal of Chiropractic Medicine, suggested that values below 0.50 indicate poor reliability, 0.50 to 0.75 moderate, 0.75 to 0.90 good and above 0.90 excellent, judged on the 95 percent confidence interval of the estimate rather than on the single number. They also asked researchers to report the model, the type (single or average) and the definition (consistency or absolute agreement), because an ICC without them cannot be interpreted. On those benchmarks Rajat's or Hiral's single mark (0.69) is moderate and the average of their two marks (0.81) is good.

Why prefer the ICC to Pearson's r for scorer reliability? Three reasons. It can count systematic differences between raters as error, which r cannot. It works for any number of raters, giving one coefficient instead of a pile of pairwise correlations. And it is a genuine ratio of true to total variance, so it means the same thing as the other reliability coefficients in this chapter and can be used in the standard error of measurement (Section 3.7).

Exam trap

The ICC is the statistic for continuous ratings by two or more raters. It is a ratio of variances (between-target variance over total variance), not a correlation between two variables. The consistency ICC ignores constant differences between raters; the absolute-agreement ICC counts them as error, so it is lower or equal. The average-measures ICC is higher than the single-measures ICC whenever agreement is less than perfect (Spearman-Brown again), so a study that will use one rater's score in practice must report the single-measures form. Koo and Li (2016): below 0.50 poor, 0.50 to 0.75 moderate, 0.75 to 0.90 good, above 0.90 excellent.

Agreement among rankings: Kendall's coefficient of concordance

Sometimes judges neither mark nor classify but put things in order: teachers rank the entries in a drawing competition, an interview board ranks candidates, an expert panel ranks test items. The statistic for several judges ranking the same objects is Kendall's coefficient of concordance, W, published by the British statistician Maurice Kendall (1907 to 1983) and Bernard Babington Smith (1905 to 1993) in 1939 in the Annals of Mathematical Statistics as "The problem of m rankings". W runs from 0 (no agreement) to 1 (every judge produces the same order).

The idea first. If the judges agree, the drawings they all like collect small ranks from everyone and the ones none of them likes collect large ranks, so the rank totals spread far apart; if they disagree, every drawing collects a mixture and the totals bunch near the middle. W compares the spread of the rank totals with the largest spread perfect agreement would produce. Bhuvan, Gargi and Madhuri rank five drawings, A to E, from best (1) to worst (5), as Table 3.27 shows.

Table 3.27 Three judges' rankings of five drawings (1 = best) and the rank total for each drawing.
DrawingBhuvanGargiMadhuriRank total (R)
A1214
B2136
C3429
D43512
E55414
  1. The mean rank total. Each judge's ranks add up to 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 15, so the three judges together hand out 45 rank points over five drawings, an average of 45 / 5 = 9 per drawing.
  2. The spread of the rank totals, S. The totals 4, 6, 9, 12 and 14 differ from 9 by −5, −3, 0, 3 and 5. Squared, these are 25, 9, 0, 9 and 25, and their sum is S = 68.
  3. The largest possible spread. If all three judges had agreed perfectly, the totals would have been 3, 6, 9, 12 and 15, with deviations of −6, −3, 0, 3 and 6, squares of 36, 9, 0, 9 and 36, and a sum of 90.
  4. W. W = 68 / 90 = 0.76.

The general formula does steps 3 and 4 in one line. With m judges and n objects,

W = 12S / [m2(n3 − n)]

where S is the sum of squared deviations of the rank totals from their mean, m is the number of judges and n is the number of objects ranked. For the drawings, W = (12 × 68) / [32 × (53 − 5)] = 816 / (9 × 120) = 816 / 1080 = 0.76, the same answer.

W is linked to Spearman's rho, the rank correlation between two rankings (the Research Methodology book): the average rho over all pairs of judges equals (mW − 1) / (m − 1), here (3 × 0.756 − 1) / 2 = 1.267 / 2 = 0.63. The pairs themselves show what W hides: Bhuvan agrees closely with Gargi and with Madhuri (rho 0.80 each), but Gargi and Madhuri agree only weakly (0.30). Kendall's W is used wherever judges rank: competition and interview panels, and expert panels judging items for content validity (Chapter 4).

Exam trap

Kendall's W (coefficient of concordance; Kendall and Babington Smith, 1939) measures agreement among several judges ranking the same objects, from 0 to 1. Do not confuse it with Kendall's tau (Kendall, 1938), a rank correlation between two variables or two judges, nor with other statistics that happen to be called W, such as the Shapiro-Wilk W test of normality. W is not used for categories (use kappa) or for marks (use the ICC).

Raising scorer reliability

Measuring scorer disagreement is only the first step; the point is to reduce it. Every practical remedy works by taking judgement out of the scoring, or, where judgement cannot be removed, by making everyone's judgement the same.

  • Objective items where they will do. Selected-response items (multiple choice, true-false, matching) scored with a key or by machine remove scorer error almost completely. Their cost is that some abilities, such as writing an argument or drawing a figure from memory, cannot be tested this way (Chapter 2).
  • Scoring keys, manuals and rubrics. Where responses must be judged, a detailed manual with examples of responses at each score level narrows the room for disagreement. Individual intelligence tests give sample answers that earn 2, 1 and 0 points (Chapter 6). A scoring rubric lists the criteria for each level of performance; an analytic rubric scores each criterion separately (content, organisation, language), a holistic rubric gives one overall judgement. Model answers and anchor scripts, real responses already scored by experts at each level, show markers what a "7" looks like.
  • Training and calibration. Raters score practice protocols, compare their scores with an expert's and with each other's, discuss the disagreements and repeat until they reach an agreed standard. Because raters drift away from the definitions over weeks and months (observer drift, Book 2, Section 2.8.7), calibration must be repeated, not done once.
  • Blind scoring. The scorer should not know the examinee's name, school, group (treatment or control) or the hypothesis of the study, so that expectations cannot leak into the scores (expectancy bias, Book 2, Section 2.8.7).
  • Double marking. Two scorers mark independently, their scores are averaged, and a third marker resolves any case where the two differ by more than a set amount. As the average-measures ICC showed, the average of two independent markers is more reliable than either alone. The double valuation and re-evaluation schemes of many Indian universities and examination boards exist for exactly this reason.

Raters also make characteristic errors, which Chapter 11 teaches with rating scales (and Book 2, Section 2.2 introduced). In one line each: the halo effect is letting an overall impression of a person colour ratings of specific, separate qualities, a tendency named by Edward Thorndike in 1920; a leniency error is rating everyone too favourably, and its mirror, a severity error, is rating everyone too harshly; a central tendency error is avoiding the extremes of the scale so that everyone ends up in the middle; and a contrast error is judging a person against the previous one rated (an average essay looks weak after a brilliant one) or against oneself. Connect these with Section 3.6.2. A rater's constant leniency or severity, like Hiral's 10 marks, damages absolute agreement but not consistency; halo, contrast and central tendency errors change people's relative positions and so damage both.

Exam trap

Scorer reliability is raised by objective scoring keys, detailed manuals and rubrics, rater training and repeated calibration, blind scoring and double marking. Lengthening the test does nothing for disagreement between scorers on each item, and a rubric that makes scorers agree does not make the test valid: two scorers can agree perfectly on a score that measures the wrong thing. Leniency and severity are constant errors (hurting absolute agreement); halo is an error in which a global impression contaminates specific ratings.

Where scorer reliability matters most: projective tests, examinations and diagnosis

The Rorschach is the classic test whose usefulness hangs on its scoring. Each free verbal response must be coded (which part of the blot was used, which features such as form, colour, shading or movement determined it, how common it is) before any interpretation can begin, and by the 1960s American clinicians were using five rival scoring systems. John E. Exner (1928 to 2006) combined them in The Rorschach: A Comprehensive System, whose first volume appeared in 1974, and made scorer agreement a hurdle: as his critics later quoted it, no scoring category was included unless it reached a minimum of .85 agreement between scorers. He also collapsed the earlier subtypes of unusual detail responses into one, partly because scorers disagreed about them.

In 1996 James Wood, M. Teresa Nezworski and William Stejskal argued in Psychological Science that the interrater reliability of most Comprehensive System scores had never been adequately demonstrated, and that much of the evidence offered was percentage agreement, uncorrected for chance, the weakness of Section 3.6.3. The reply came from data: in 2002 Gregory Meyer and colleagues, Exner among them, reported intraclass correlations for the Comprehensive System in eight relatively large data sets: protocols coded by students, by experienced researchers and by clinicians, a composite of these, and three test samples in which 10, 20 or 30 percent of the original codes had been deliberately replaced with randomly generated errors. Across 133 to 143 stable scores the median ICCs ranged from .82 (the sample with 30 percent errors) to .97, and summary scores were more reliable than single-response codes. Their conclusion: the system can be scored reliably, but because scores are the product of a coder's skill, clinicians must keep checking their own accuracy. Chapter 10 teaches the projective techniques, several of which are interpreted without any formal scoring system, so that their scorer reliability is often unknown.

Examinations are the second great home of scorer error, and the remedies of Section 3.6.8 are largely the examiner's answer to it. The third is clinical judgement. An unstructured interview leaves each clinician free to ask different questions and weigh the answers differently; structured and semi-structured interviews fix the questions and the rules for scoring each answer, raising inter-rater agreement for the same reason a rubric does (Book 2, Section 2.1.4 describes the schedules, Section 2.1.5 the trade-off). The kappas of the DSM field trials and the base-rate paradox are in Book 2, Section 2.10.5, and Book 3, Section 1.6.6 draws the lessons for diagnosis.

Clinical link

Before you trust a score that depends on judgement (a Rorschach index, a scored drawing, a clinician-rated depression scale, a Vocabulary subtest scored by a new trainee), ask three questions. What scorer reliability did the manual or the research report, and was it a chance-corrected statistic (kappa, ICC) or only percentage agreement? Were those scorers trained to a standard you have also reached? And how consistent are you with yourself? Rescoring a few of your own old protocols, blind to your first scores, is the cheapest reliability study a clinician can run, and supervisors in training clinics often insist on it.

Table 3.28 collects the statistics of this section in one place, as a guide to choosing among them.

Table 3.28 Choosing a statistic for agreement between scorers.
StatisticKind of judgementRatersWhat it allows for
Percentage agreementCategories (or intervals)TwoNothing; inflated by chance agreement
Pearson's r between scorersContinuous marksTwoConsistency only; blind to a constant difference
Cohen's kappa (1960)Nominal categoriesTwoChance agreement
Weighted kappa (1968)Ordered categoriesTwoChance; partial credit for near misses
Fleiss' kappa (1971)Nominal categoriesThree or moreChance agreement across many raters
Intraclass correlationContinuous ratingsTwo or moreRater differences as error (agreement form)
Kendall's W (1939)RankingsSeveral judgesAgreement in rank order, 0 to 1

Scorer agreement tells us how much of a score depends on who scored it. But whatever method we use, and whatever coefficient results, a coefficient is a statement about a group of scores. The psychologist sitting with one child and one score needs something else: a margin of error in the units of the test. That is the subject of Section 3.7.

3.7 The Standard Error of Measurement and Confidence BandsMedium yield · 6 Qs

Megha, a Class 12 student, has just scored 30 out of 40 on Rohini's Verbal Reasoning Test. Her teacher wants to know whether she is really one of the stronger students, Megha wants to know whether she would score 30 again, and Rohini wants to report the score honestly. How much can any of them trust that 30? The reliability coefficient of 0.84 from Section 3.5 does not answer them directly. It says that 84 percent of the variance among the 200 students' scores reflects true differences between students and 16 percent reflects error. That is a statement about a group. To say something about one student's score, the coefficient has to be turned into a margin of error expressed in the test's own units: points on the VRT, IQ points, marks. That margin is the standard error of measurement, and for anyone who reports scores to real people it is the most useful number in this chapter.

One person, tested again and again

Start with a thought experiment, the same one that defined the true score in Section 3.1. Imagine giving Megha the VRT not once but a thousand times, and imagine that some magic wipes out every memory of the test, every bit of practice and every trace of boredom between testings, so that each testing is a fresh, independent sample of her performance. Her scores would not all be 30. On a good day, with lucky items and a quiet hall, she might score 33; on a tired day, 27. If you plotted all thousand scores you would get a bell-shaped pile. Its centre, the mean of all those testings, is by definition her true score. Its spread is the subject of this section.

The standard error of measurement (SEM) is the standard deviation of the scores one person would obtain over a very large number of independent testings with the same test; equivalently, it is the standard deviation of the errors of measurement. A small SEM means the pile is narrow, so any single score lies close to the true score; a large SEM means the pile is wide, so a single score could be well away from it. The errors of classical test theory are random, with a mean of zero, and they are usually assumed to follow the bell-shaped normal curve, sometimes called the normal curve of error (the Research Methodology book teaches the normal curve, and Chapter 5 uses it for norms and standard scores).

Two features of this definition matter later. First, classical test theory assumes that the SEM is the same for everyone who takes the test, whether they score high, low or in the middle. That is a simplification: in reality a test usually measures more precisely in the range where most of its items sit, and Section 3.9 shows how item response theory replaces the single SEM with a standard error that changes from one ability level to another. Second, the SEM is a standard deviation, but not the one you are used to. Three different spreads are easy to confuse, and examiners exploit the confusion.

  • The standard deviation (SD) of a test describes how much different people's scores differ from one another: on the VRT, 6 points.
  • The standard error of measurement describes how much one person's scores would differ from one testing to another: on the VRT, 2.4 points.
  • The standard error of the mean, SD divided by the square root of the number of people, describes how much the mean of a sample would differ from one sample of people to another. It belongs to sampling and significance testing (the Research Methodology book), not to the measurement of one person.
Exam trap

The SEM is the standard deviation of the errors, or of one person's obtained scores around his or her true score over repeated testings. It is not the SD of the group's scores, and it is not the standard error of the mean (SD / √N, the precision of a sample mean). Classical test theory gives one SEM for the whole test; the idea that precision differs at different score levels (the conditional standard error) comes from item response theory (Section 3.9).

The formula, and why it has this form

Nobody can test Megha a thousand times, so the SEM has to be estimated from data we do have: the SD of the test and its reliability coefficient. In words: the SEM equals the standard deviation of the test multiplied by the square root of one minus the reliability. The reason takes two lines of algebra from Section 3.1. There, the reliability coefficient was defined as the proportion of observed-score variance that is true-score variance, rxx = σ2T / σ2X, and the observed variance was split into true variance plus error variance, σ2X = σ2T + σ2E. If the true part is the fraction rxx of the observed variance, the error part must be the rest, the fraction (1 − rxx):

σ2E = σ2X (1 − rxx)

The SEM is the standard deviation of the errors, so take the square root of both sides:

Remember

SEM = SD × √(1 − rxx)

where SEM is the standard error of measurement, SD is the standard deviation of the obtained scores in the group (σX), and rxx is the reliability coefficient of the test. The SEM is in the same units as the test scores. When rxx = 1 the SEM is 0; when rxx = 0 the SEM equals the SD.

Check it against the numbers of Section 3.1. The VRT's observed variance is 36 and its reliability 0.84, so the error variance is 36 × (1 − 0.84) = 36 × 0.16 = 5.76, the figure Section 3.1 gave. The square root of 5.76 is 2.4, and the formula gives the same thing in one step: 6 × √0.16 = 6 × 0.4 = 2.4.

The two limits make sense once you picture them. A perfectly reliable test (r = 1) has no error variance, so a person's scores would never vary: SEM = 0. A test with no reliability at all (r = 0) has nothing but error: the scores scatter around a person's true score as widely as different people's scores scatter around the group mean, so SEM = SD. Between those limits the relationship is not a straight line, and Figure 3.14 shows its shape. As reliability rises from 0 to about 0.5, the SEM falls only from 1 SD to 0.71 SD; it is still half the SD at a reliability of 0.75. Most of the gain comes at the top. To halve the SEM you must quarter (1 − r): from r = 0.84 (SEM 0.40 SD) to r = 0.96 (SEM 0.20 SD). This is why test developers fight so hard for the last few hundredths of reliability.

Two practical points follow from the formula. The first is that the SEM inherits the error source of whichever reliability coefficient is put into it. An SEM computed from coefficient alpha reflects content sampling error only and says nothing about how much a person's score would change from one week to the next; an SEM computed from a test-retest coefficient reflects time sampling error. Manuals usually compute SEMs from internal consistency coefficients, which are often the highest coefficients available, so their SEMs are best-case figures. The second is that the SEM is fairly stable from one group to another, more stable than the reliability coefficient itself. In a group with a narrow range of ability, both the SD and the reliability coefficient fall, and in the formula the two changes largely cancel. Section 3.8 explains why restriction of range lowers the coefficient; the SEM, which describes the test's precision for one person, is much less affected. That is one reason Singh's textbook and others recommend the SEM over the coefficient for interpreting individual scores.

Exam trap

The formula is SD × √(1 − r): subtract the reliability from 1, take the square root, multiply by the SD. Every distractor breaks one step. SD × √r forgets to subtract from 1; SD × r and SD / r drop the square root and the subtraction; SD × (1 + r) adds instead of subtracting; SD × (1 − r) forgets the root; √(1 − r) alone forgets the SD. "Reliability × validity" and "mean − SD" are not standard errors at all. Table 3.30 shows what each gives.

Working the formula

The past papers ask for the SEM again and again, and almost always with an SD of 15, the SD of the IQ scale (Chapter 5). Work each one slowly.

SD 15, reliability 0.91 (asked twice). Step 1: 1 − 0.91 = 0.09. Step 2: √0.09 = 0.3, because 0.3 × 0.3 = 0.09. Step 3: 15 × 0.3 = 4.5. The SEM is 4.5 IQ points.

SD 15, reliability 0.80. Step 1: 1 − 0.80 = 0.20. Step 2: √0.20 is not a round number; it is about 0.447, because 0.447 × 0.447 = 0.1998. Step 3: 15 × 0.447 = 6.708, which rounds to 6.7. Notice how much worse this test is than the last one for an individual: its reliability is only 0.11 lower, but its SEM is about 50 percent larger.

Rohini's VRT, SD 6, reliability 0.84. Step 1: 1 − 0.84 = 0.16. Step 2: √0.16 = 0.4. Step 3: 6 × 0.4 = 2.4 points.

Examiners like reliabilities that make the square root come out exactly, so it pays to know a few roots by heart: √0.01 = 0.1, √0.04 = 0.2, √0.09 = 0.3, √0.16 = 0.4, √0.25 = 0.5 and √0.36 = 0.6. These correspond to reliabilities of 0.99, 0.96, 0.91, 0.84, 0.75 and 0.64. For the others, √0.05 ≈ 0.224, √0.10 ≈ 0.316, √0.15 ≈ 0.387, √0.20 ≈ 0.447 and √0.30 ≈ 0.548. Table 3.29 uses them to show how the SEM of an IQ-type scale shrinks as reliability rises.

Table 3.29 The SEM of a scale with SD 15 at different reliabilities, and the half-width of the 95 percent band (1.96 × SEM). Computed, rounded to one decimal place at the end.
Reliability (r)1 − r√(1 − r)SEM = 15 × √(1 − r)95 percent band: score ±
0.700.300.5488.216.1
0.800.200.4476.713.1
0.850.150.3875.811.4
0.900.100.3164.79.3
0.910.090.3004.58.8
0.950.050.2243.46.6

Read the last column with a child's report in mind. On a scale with reliability 0.70, a reported IQ of 100 is honestly "somewhere between about 84 and 116", which covers everything from the low-average range to the high-average range (Chapter 6). At 0.95 the same report becomes "between about 93 and 107". This is why Section 3.8 asks for reliabilities of 0.90 or more when decisions are made about individuals.

Now the wrong formulas. Table 3.30 pushes SD 15 and r 0.91 through every distractor that has appeared in the papers, so that you can recognise them by their answers.

Table 3.30 The SEM formula and its distractors, for SD 15 and reliability 0.91.
OptionWorkingResultWhat went wrong
SD × √(1 − r)15 × √0.09 = 15 × 0.34.5Correct
SD × √r15 × √0.91 = 15 × 0.95414.3Uses true-score spread, not error
SD × r15 × 0.9113.65No subtraction, no root
SD / r15 / 0.9116.5Larger than the SD: impossible
SD × (1 + r)15 × 1.9128.65Adds reliability
SD × (1 − r)15 × 0.091.35Forgets the square root
√(1 − r)√0.090.3Forgets to multiply by the SD
Mean − SD100 − 1585A score one SD below the mean

The first wrong row is worth a second look, because it is not nonsense: SD × √r is the standard deviation of the true scores, the spread of the true part rather than the error part. "Reliability × validity" has no row because it does not even produce a number in score units; it multiplies two coefficients. In the multiple-choice versions of the question, 15.0 is the SD itself (the SEM only if reliability were zero), 12.0 is 15 × 0.80 (SD × r for the 0.80 test), 1.35 comes from forgetting the square root (1.5 is the same slip with 0.09 rounded to 0.1), and 10.0 matches no formula at all.

Confidence bands around a score

Knowing the SEM, we can put a band of uncertainty around any obtained score. The logic runs in two steps. First, because errors follow the normal curve, a person's obtained scores fall within one SEM of the true score about 68 percent of the time, within 1.96 SEM about 95 percent of the time and within 2.58 SEM about 99 percent of the time; these percentages are properties of the normal curve, which the Research Methodology book teaches. Second, turn the statement round. If an obtained score lies within 1.96 SEM of the true score in 95 cases out of 100, then a band stretching 1.96 SEM on each side of the obtained score will contain the true score in 95 cases out of 100. Such a band is called a confidence band (or confidence interval) for the true score.

Remember

68 percent band: X ± 1 SEM. 95 percent band: X ± 1.96 SEM. 99 percent band: X ± 2.58 SEM.

where X is the obtained score. The more confident you want to be that the band contains the true score, the wider the band must be.

Megha's score of 30 on the VRT, with its SEM of 2.4, gives:

  • a 68 percent band of 30 ± 2.4, that is, 27.6 to 32.4;
  • a 95 percent band of 30 ± (1.96 × 2.4) = 30 ± 4.704, which rounds to 30 ± 4.7, that is, 25.3 to 34.7;
  • a 99 percent band of 30 ± (2.58 × 2.4) = 30 ± 6.192, about 23.8 to 36.2.

Figure 3.15 draws the first two bands on the normal curve of error.

What does this mean in plain words? Megha's true verbal reasoning score is not known, and it never will be. But if Rohini builds a 95 percent band in this way around every student's score, about 95 of every 100 bands she builds will contain the student's true score. Strictly, it is the band that varies from one testing to another, not the true score, which is a fixed if unknown quantity; in everyday reporting this is shortened to "we can be 95 percent confident that Megha's true score lies between 25.3 and 34.7".

The same arithmetic works on the IQ scale. Take a child whose IQ comes out at exactly 100 on a test with SD 15 and reliability 0.91, so that the SEM is 4.5. The 68 percent band is 95.5 to 104.5. The 95 percent band is 100 ± (1.96 × 4.5) = 100 ± 8.82, which is 91.18 to 108.82, about 91 to 109. The 99 percent band is 100 ± 11.61, about 88 to 112. A single "IQ 100" sounds precise; "IQ 100, 95 percent band 91 to 109" says what the test actually knows.

Bands change decisions. Suppose a scholarship is offered to students who score 32 or more on the VRT. Megha's 30 is below the line, but her 95 percent band, 25.3 to 34.7, contains 32 comfortably: the test cannot tell whether her true score is above or below the cut-off. That does not mean the scholarship committee must give it to her, but it does mean that treating 30 and 32 as clearly different would be claiming more precision than the test has. Section 3.7.6 shows how to test properly whether two scores differ.

A practice paper asked what the SEM is used for in classical test theory. The answer is to estimate the range within which a person's true score is likely to fall, exactly what this subsection has done. Each wrong option names something else. "Determine the correlation between two different tests" describes a correlation coefficient, which, between two forms of one test, would be parallel-forms reliability (Section 3.3) and, between two different tests, would be evidence of validity (Chapter 4). "Measure the difficulty level of individual items" describes the item difficulty index p, the proportion of people passing an item (Section 3.5; Chapter 2). "Assess whether a test predicts future performance" describes predictive validity (Chapter 4).

Exam trap

The SEM is used to build a band within which the true score probably lies: ±1 SEM for about 68 percent, ±1.96 SEM for 95 percent, ±2.58 SEM for 99 percent. Some Indian textbooks, Singh's among them, round these to ±2 SEM for 95 percent and ±3 SEM for 99 percent; strictly, ±2 covers about 95.4 percent and ±3 about 99.7 percent, so use 1.96 and 2.58 when the question gives no other instruction. Higher confidence means a wider band; higher reliability means a narrower band. The SEM does not give the correlation between tests, the difficulty of items or the test's predictive power.

Where to centre the band: Kelley's estimated true score

So far the band has been centred on the obtained score. There is a subtle reason why that is not quite the best centre, and it was worked out by the American psychometrician Truman Lee Kelley (1884 to 1961) in his 1927 book The Interpretation of Educational Measurements. Think about everyone in a large group who obtains an IQ of 130 on one testing. Some of them have true scores above 130 and were unlucky that day; some have true scores below 130 and were lucky. But there are many more people with true IQs around 120 to 125 than around 135 to 140, because scores bunch towards the middle of the normal curve. So among the people who score 130, the lucky ones from below outnumber the unlucky ones from above, and the average true score of the group who obtained 130 is a little under 130. The same reasoning in reverse applies to very low scores: people who obtain 70 have, on average, true scores a little above 70. Extreme scores contain, on average, some luck in the extreme direction. On retesting they tend to move back towards the mean, the effect called regression towards the mean.

Kelley's formula estimates the true score by pulling the obtained score towards the group mean, by an amount that depends on the reliability. In words: the estimated true score is the mean plus the reliability times the distance of the obtained score from the mean.

Remember

T̂ = M + rxx(X − M)

where T̂ ("T-hat") is the estimated true score, M is the mean of the group, rxx is the reliability coefficient and X is the obtained score. A perfectly reliable test (r = 1) leaves the score where it is; a test with no reliability (r = 0) gives the mean as the best estimate for everyone.

Work two examples on the IQ scale (mean 100), with a reliability of 0.90.

  • Obtained IQ 130. The distance from the mean is 130 − 100 = 30. Multiply by the reliability: 0.90 × 30 = 27. Add back the mean: 100 + 27 = 127. The estimated true score is 127, three points closer to the mean than the obtained score.
  • Obtained IQ 70. The distance from the mean is 70 − 100 = −30. Multiply: 0.90 × (−30) = −27. Add the mean: 100 − 27 = 73. The estimated true score is 73, three points higher than the obtained score.

When the band is centred on the estimated true score, its width is set by a slightly different standard error, the standard error of estimation, SD × √[rxx(1 − rxx)], which is the formula Singh gives in the form σ√(r − r2). For SD 15 and r 0.90 it is 15 × √(0.90 × 0.10) = 15 × √0.09 = 15 × 0.3 = 4.5. The 95 percent band for the person who obtained 130 is then 127 ± (1.96 × 4.5) = 127 ± 8.8, that is, 118.2 to 135.8. Compare the band centred on the obtained score: the SEM is 15 × √0.10 = 4.74, and 130 ± (1.96 × 4.74) = 130 ± 9.3 gives 120.7 to 139.3. The estimated-true-score band is slightly narrower, is shifted towards the mean and is no longer symmetrical around the obtained score: it reaches 11.8 points below 130 but only 5.8 above it. Many test manuals, the Wechsler intelligence scales among them, build their tables of confidence bands this way, so when you copy a band from a manual into a report, check which method the manual used. When reliability is very high the two methods give almost the same band; the lower the reliability and the more extreme the score, the more they differ.

Exam trap

Kelley's estimated true score, M + r(X − M), is always closer to the mean than the obtained score (unless the score is at the mean or r = 1): an IQ of 130 with r 0.90 becomes 127, and an IQ of 70 becomes 73. The pull is larger when reliability is lower. This is regression towards the mean, not an error in the test, and it is why a child selected for an extreme score usually scores less extremely when retested, whatever happened in between.

Do two scores really differ? The standard error of the difference

Much of clinical testing is comparison. Is this child's verbal ability really higher than her nonverbal ability? Has this client's depression score really fallen since therapy began? Each of the two scores being compared carries its own error, so their difference carries the error of both. Because the errors on the two measures are independent (one of the assumptions of Section 3.1), their variances add. The standard error of the difference (SEdiff) is therefore the square root of the sum of the two squared SEMs.

Remember

SEdiff = √(SEM12 + SEM22), and when both scores are on the same scale with the same SD, SEdiff = SD × √(2 − r11 − r22)

where SEM1 and SEM2 are the standard errors of measurement of the two scores, SD is their common standard deviation, and r11 and r22 are their reliabilities. A difference larger than 1.96 × SEdiff is unlikely to be produced by measurement error alone (at the 0.05 level).

The second form follows from the first. SEM12 = SD2(1 − r11) and SEM22 = SD2(1 − r22); adding them gives SD2(2 − r11 − r22), and the square root gives the formula.

Gargi, the school psychologist from Section 3.6, has assessed a 10-year-old boy on an intelligence battery whose subtests are reported as scaled scores with a mean of 10 and an SD of 3 (Chapter 5 explains scaled scores). He scores 12 on a vocabulary subtest, whose reliability is 0.85, and 8 on a visual puzzles subtest, whose reliability is 0.80. Is the 4-point gap real?

  1. The SEMs. Vocabulary: 3 × √(1 − 0.85) = 3 × √0.15 = 3 × 0.387 = 1.16. Puzzles: 3 × √0.20 = 3 × 0.447 = 1.34.
  2. Square and add. 1.162 is about 1.35 (exactly 9 × 0.15) and 1.342 is about 1.80 (exactly 9 × 0.20); the sum is 3.15.
  3. Square root. SEdiff = √3.15 = 1.77. The shortcut gives the same: 3 × √(2 − 0.85 − 0.80) = 3 × √0.35 = 3 × 0.592 = 1.77.
  4. The critical difference. 1.96 × 1.77 = 3.48, about 3.5 scaled-score points.

The boy's gap of 4 points exceeds 3.5, so it is unlikely to be measurement error alone: Gargi can say, at the 0.05 level, that his vocabulary score is reliably higher than his visual puzzles score. A gap of 3 points would not have been enough; it could easily arise from error in two scores whose true values are the same. Two further points keep this in proportion. A reliable difference is not necessarily an unusual or a clinically important one: many children in the general population show gaps of this size, and test manuals give separate tables of how often each size of difference occurs, which Chapter 6 uses for Wechsler index and profile comparisons. And a common shortcut is wrong in an instructive way: adding the two SEMs (1.16 + 1.34 = 2.50) instead of adding their squares overstates the error of the difference, just as asking whether two 95 percent bands overlap is a needlessly strict test.

Clinical link: has my client really changed?

The same logic tells a therapist whether a change in a client's score is more than measurement error. Neil Jacobson and Paula Truax (1991, Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology) built it into the reliable change index: the change divided by the standard error of the difference, where for the same test given twice SEdiff = √2 × SEM. Madhuri's client scores 30 on a depression inventory at intake and 21 after eight sessions; the inventory has an SD of 8 and a reliability of 0.88. The SEM is 8 × √0.12 = 8 × 0.346 = 2.77, SEdiff = 1.414 × 2.77 = 3.92, and the change of 9 points gives 9 / 3.92 = 2.30. Because 2.30 is larger than 1.96, the improvement is unlikely to be measurement error alone; a fall of fewer than about 7.7 points (1.96 × 3.92) would not have been.

Exam trap

To compare two scores, add the squared SEMs and take the root: SEdiff = √(SEM12 + SEM22) = SD × √(2 − r11 − r22). The SE of a difference is always larger than either SEM, so a difference between two scores is less certain than either score. Do not add the SEMs themselves, and do not confuse a statistically reliable difference with a rare or clinically meaningful one.

The trouble with difference scores, and the strength of composites

The standard error of the difference concerns one person's two scores. A related question concerns a whole group: if we compute a difference score for everyone (post-test minus pre-test, verbal minus nonverbal, achievement minus ability), how reliable are those difference scores as a set? The answer is often "much less reliable than either test", and the formula shows why.

Remember

rdd = [(r11 + r22) / 2 − r12] / (1 − r12)

where rdd is the reliability of the difference scores, r11 and r22 are the reliabilities of the two tests (in standard-score form), and r12 is the correlation between the two tests.

Take two tests, each with a reliability of 0.80, that correlate 0.60 with each other. The average reliability is (0.80 + 0.80) / 2 = 0.80. Subtract the correlation: 0.80 − 0.60 = 0.20. Divide by 1 − 0.60 = 0.40. The reliability of the difference score is 0.20 / 0.40 = 0.50: two good tests have produced a difference score that is half error. If the two tests correlated 0.80, as highly as each correlates with itself, the reliability of the difference would be (0.80 − 0.80) / 0.20 = 0: every difference would be pure error.

The reason becomes clear if you follow the variance. Suppose both tests are expressed with an SD of 10, so each has a variance of 100, and each test's error variance is 100 × (1 − 0.80) = 20. Table 3.31 adds and subtracts the two tests for a group of people.

Table 3.31 The same two tests (each SD 10, reliability 0.80, correlating 0.60) combined as a sum and as a difference.
QuantitySum (X + Y)Difference (X − Y)
Total variance100 + 100 + (2 × 0.60 × 10 × 10) = 320100 + 100 − (2 × 0.60 × 10 × 10) = 80
Error variance20 + 20 = 4020 + 20 = 40
True variance320 − 40 = 28080 − 40 = 40
Reliability1 − 40 / 320 = 0.8751 − 40 / 80 = 0.50

The error variances add in both columns, because the errors on the two tests are independent and do not cancel. But the true variance behaves very differently. The two tests share a good deal of true variance (that is why they correlate 0.60). When you add them, the shared part counts twice and the total grows; when you subtract them, the shared part cancels out, leaving only the small amount of true variance that differs between the tests. The error, meanwhile, is all still there. So the difference score keeps all the error and only a little of the truth. The second column reproduces exactly the 0.50 given by the formula.

This has real consequences. Gain scores (post-test minus pre-test) on the same measure are difference scores between two highly correlated testings, so the gain of one person, or the ranking of people by how much they gained, is often unreliable, which is one reason Lee Cronbach and Lita Furby argued in a well-known 1970 paper in Psychological Bulletin, "How we should measure 'change': or should we?", that simple difference scores are often the wrong tool. Ability-achievement discrepancy scores, once the standard way of identifying a learning disability (a child's achievement well below what his IQ predicts), are difference scores between two correlated tests and inherit the same weakness, one of the reasons DSM-5 dropped the IQ-discrepancy rule (Book 3, Section 3.9.2; Chapter 7 covers the assessment). Profile interpretation, reading meaning into the peaks and troughs among a person's subtest scores, is the interpretation of many difference scores at once, and Chapter 6 shows why it must be done with the standard error of the difference in hand.

The left-hand column of Table 3.31 shows the reverse effect. A composite score, the sum or average of several positively correlated tests, is more reliable than its parts: two tests of reliability 0.80 make a total of reliability 0.875. The shared true variance accumulates while the independent errors partly average out, the same principle that makes a long test more reliable than a short one (Section 3.4) and two markers more reliable than one (Section 3.6.6). In general, the reliability of a composite is 1 minus the sum of the parts' error variances divided by the variance of the composite. This is why the full-scale IQ of an intelligence battery is more reliable, and has a smaller SEM relative to its SD, than any of the subtests from which it is built, and why important decisions should rest on composite scores rather than on single subtests.

Exam trap

A difference score is least reliable when the two tests correlate highly with each other, because their shared true variance cancels while their errors add. With reliabilities of 0.80 and a correlation of 0.60, rdd = 0.50; if the correlation reaches the reliabilities, rdd = 0. The opposite holds for composites: adding positively correlated tests gives a total more reliable than either part. "Difference scores are more reliable than the tests they come from" is always wrong.

Bands in practice: cut-offs, reports and the law

Nowhere does the SEM matter more than when a score is compared with a fixed cut-off, and the most important cut-off in clinical testing is the IQ criterion for intellectual disability. Book 3, Section 3.2.2 teaches the criteria themselves; here is the measurement logic behind them.

Clinical link: the SEM and the IQ cut-off for intellectual disability

DSM-5-TR describes people with intellectual developmental disorder as scoring "approximately two standard deviations or more below the population mean, including a margin for measurement error (generally ± 5 points)", which on a test with a mean of 100 and an SD of 15 means, in the manual's words, a score of 65 to 75 ("70 ± 5"). The 5 points are a confidence band: a full-scale IQ with an SEM of about 2.5 has a 95 percent band of about ±5 (1.96 × 2.5 = 4.9). Kelley's formula points the same way, since an obtained 70 on a test of reliability 0.90 has an estimated true score of 73. In Hall v. Florida, decided 5 to 4 on 27 May 2014, the United States Supreme Court considered Freddie Lee Hall, a man under sentence of death whose IQ scores included a 71. Florida's rule, as applied by its courts, barred anyone scoring above 70 from offering any further evidence of intellectual disability. The Court, building on Atkins v. Virginia (2002), which had barred the execution of people with intellectual disability, held that rule unconstitutional because it ignored the standard error of measurement: when a score falls within the test's margin of error, the person must be allowed to present other evidence, including evidence of adaptive deficits.

Indian context

Indian certification rules also use IQ lines. Under the disability certification guidelines summarised in Book 2, Section 9.9.8 and Book 3, Section 3.1.6, the specific tests for a learning disability are applied only after intelligence testing has placed the child's IQ at about 85 or above, one standard deviation below the mean. On a test with SD 15 and reliability 0.91 (SEM 4.5), a child who obtains 83 has a 95 percent band of 83 ± 8.8, about 74 to 92, which contains 85. The score alone cannot show that the child's true IQ is below the line, and a careful psychologist will say so in the report and look at the other evidence (Chapter 7).

The general lesson is an ethical one, and Chapter 1 sets it among the other ethical duties of testers: a score should be reported with its band, not as a bare number treated as exact. A report that says "Full-scale IQ 72 (95 percent confidence band 67 to 77)" tells a parent, a teacher or a court what the test knows and what it does not; a report that says "IQ 72" invites the reader to treat 72 and 70 as different kinds of child. The band also protects the psychologist, because it makes plain that the decision rests on clinical judgement and on all the evidence, not on a single number. With the SEM, the standard error of the difference and the reliability of difference scores, you can now say how precise one score is, whether two scores differ and how much a change can be trusted. Section 3.8 turns from individual scores back to the coefficient itself and asks what makes it rise and fall.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The degree to which two clinicians agree on the scoring or interpretation of the same test is called reliability.
  2. Two raters whose marks always differ by exactly 10 points have a Pearson correlation of 1.00 but poor absolute .
  3. Cohen's kappa is calculated as (po − pe) / (1 − pe), where pe is the agreement expected by .
  4. Two raters agree on 90 of 100 cases, and chance agreement is 0.60. Their kappa is .
  5. On the Landis and Koch benchmarks, a kappa of 0.85 indicates agreement.
  6. For ordered categories such as mild, moderate and severe, near misses earn partial credit in kappa; for agreement on continuous ratings the statistic is the correlation coefficient.
  7. Agreement among several judges who rank the same objects is measured by Kendall's coefficient of , W.
  8. A test has an SD of 15 and a reliability of 0.84. Its standard error of measurement is .
  9. Megha's 95 percent band on the VRT runs from 25.3 to 34.7; the 95 percent band is the obtained score plus or minus SEM.
  10. Two tests each with reliability 0.80 correlate 0.60; the reliability of the difference between them is .

Answers: (1) inter-rater (or scorer), (2) agreement, (3) chance, (4) 0.75, because (0.90 − 0.60) / (1 − 0.60) = 0.30 / 0.40, (5) almost perfect, (6) weighted; intraclass, (7) concordance, (8) 6.0, because 15 × √(1 − 0.84) = 15 × 0.4, (9) 1.96, (10) 0.50, because (0.80 − 0.60) / (1 − 0.60) = 0.20 / 0.40.

3.8 What Raises and Lowers Reliability, and How Much Is EnoughLower yield · 1 Q

By now Rohini has done everything this chapter has asked of her. She gave her 40-item Verbal Reasoning Test (VRT) to 200 Class 12 students, gave it again three weeks later, split it into odd and even halves, and worked out the Kuder-Richardson formulas. She has four numbers in her notebook: 0.80, 0.82, 0.84 and 0.75. When her supervisor asks the obvious question, "So, how reliable is your test?", she hesitates. Which of the four is the answer? And whichever she picks, is it good enough? Good enough for what?

Those two questions organise this section. The first is about the forces that push a reliability coefficient up or down: some of them live in the test (its length, its items, how it is scored), some live in the group that takes it (how widely its members differ), and some live in the testing situation (the interval, the time limit, the room, the person). The second is about standards: how high a coefficient must be before you may use the scores for research, for screening, or for a decision about one person's life. The section ends with the most important consequence of unreliability, the one that links this chapter to the next: a test cannot correlate with anything more strongly than it correlates with itself, so unreliability puts a ceiling on validity.

One test, several coefficients

Start with Rohini's four numbers, set side by side in Table 3.32. They are all estimates of the same quantity in the sense of Section 3.1, the proportion of observed-score variance that is true-score variance. Yet they differ, and they differ for a reason that is worth understanding once and for all: each method decides differently what counts as error. The true-score model says only that X = T + E. It does not say what is "true" and what is "error"; the design of the reliability study decides that, by deciding what is allowed to vary between the two measurements being compared.

Table 3.32 Rohini's four coefficients for the 40-item Verbal Reasoning Test, and why they differ.
MethodCoefficientWhat it counts as errorWhy it comes out where it does
Test-retest, three weeks (Section 3.2)0.80Changes in the students between the two sittings (time sampling)Day-to-day changes count as error; the particular 40 items are the same both times, so their luck counts as true
Odd-even split, stepped up by Spearman-Brown (Section 3.4)0.82Differences between two particular halves (content sampling)One split out of a vast number; another split would give a slightly different value
KR-20, equal to alpha here (Section 3.5)0.84Inconsistency among all 40 items (content sampling and item heterogeneity)Uses every item; the day's mood or fatigue affects all items alike, so it counts as true
KR-21 (Section 3.5)0.75The same as KR-20Assumes all items are equally difficult; the VRT's items are not, so it underestimates

Look at the first and third rows together, because they hold the key idea. In the retest design, each student answers the same 40 items twice. If a student happens to know the meaning of a rare word in item 17, she knows it on both occasions, so that lucky piece of item content helps her both times and ends up inside her "true" score. What can differ between the sittings is the student herself: she slept badly before one of them, or she was worried about a chemistry practical. The retest coefficient therefore treats time sampling as error and is blind to content sampling. In the internal consistency design the logic is reversed. All 40 items are answered in one sitting, so a student's tiredness that morning lowers her performance on every item alike; the items agree with each other about her, and KR-20 counts the tiredness as part of her true score. What can differ is the items: the rare word in item 17 helps on that item and not on the others. Internal consistency treats content sampling as error and is blind to time sampling.

So neither 0.80 nor 0.84 is "the" reliability of the VRT. Each answers its own question. The retest coefficient answers "if Rohini tested these students again with this very test in three weeks, how well would the ranks hold?" KR-20 answers "if she had written a different set of 40 items from the same domain and given it on the same morning, how well would the ranks hold?" A question that matters more in real life, "how well would the ranks hold with a different set of items on a different day?", is answered by neither. It is answered by the coefficient of stability and equivalence (Section 3.3), which counts both kinds of error and is usually the lowest estimate of all. You will see in Section 3.9 that generalizability theory was invented precisely so that several sources of error could be counted at once instead of one at a time.

The two remaining rows are easier. The split-half figure, 0.82, sits close to KR-20 because KR-20 is, in effect, an average over all possible ways of splitting the test, of which the odd-even split is one (Section 3.5). KR-21, 0.75, is lower because it makes an assumption the VRT breaks: it treats every item as equally difficult, and when difficulties differ KR-21 always comes out lower than KR-20, never higher. It is a quick, conservative estimate, not a rival to KR-20.

What, then, should Rohini write in her thesis? Not one number, but each number with its method, its sample and, for the retest, its interval: "KR-20 = 0.84 in 200 Class 12 students from four colleges; test-retest r = 0.80 over three weeks in the same students". A coefficient reported without its method is like a temperature reported without saying whether it is Celsius or Fahrenheit.

Exam trap

"The reliability of a test" is shorthand. Different methods count different sources of error, so the same test legitimately has several coefficients. Rules worth memorising: the retest coefficient is blind to content sampling; internal consistency is blind to time sampling; parallel forms given after an interval count both and usually give the lowest estimate; KR-21 is lower than KR-20 whenever item difficulties differ; and a split-half coefficient from a speeded test is spuriously high (Section 3.4).

The test itself: length, items and guessing

The first group of influences lives inside the test. The most powerful of them is length. Section 3.4 taught the Spearman-Brown prophecy formula and the reasoning behind it: each item contributes a little true score and a little random error; when items are added, the true-score parts pile up in the same direction while the random errors, pointing every which way, partly cancel. A 10-item scale with a reliability of 0.60 becomes 0.75 when it is doubled to 20 parallel items and about 0.86 when it is made four times as long. Notice the diminishing returns: the first doubling bought 0.15, the second only 0.11. Notice also the word parallel. The prophecy holds only if the new items measure the same thing as the old ones, with similar difficulty and similar correlations with the rest of the test. Adding ten items about something else, or ten items so confusing that people answer them at random, adds noise, not reliability. And a test can be made too long: a 300-item questionnaire tires and bores people, and tired, bored people answer carelessly, which is random error again.

The second influence is item difficulty. Section 3.5 showed that the variance of a right-or-wrong item is pq, where p is the proportion of people who pass it and q = 1 − p. That product is largest, 0.25, when p = 0.50, and shrinks towards zero as an item becomes very easy or very hard: an item passed by 95 percent of students has a variance of only 0.95 × 0.05 = 0.0475, about a fifth of the maximum, and an item passed by everyone has a variance of exactly zero. An item with no variance cannot tell anyone apart from anyone else, so it contributes nothing to the differences between people that reliability is all about. Items of middling difficulty, spread around the ability level of the people being tested, give a test the most room to show true differences. Chapter 2 shows how test developers use the difficulty index, and why the best difficulty moves somewhat above 0.50 when people can guess.

The third is item discrimination, how well an item separates people who are high on the trait from people who are low, usually indexed by its correlation with the total score (Chapter 2). An item that correlates well with the rest of the test is measuring what the rest of the test measures; an item with a near-zero item-total correlation is measuring something else, or nothing, and drags the internal consistency down. That is why item analysis so often raises a test's reliability: weeding out the non-discriminating items leaves a shorter test that is nonetheless more consistent. Closely related is the homogeneity of content. A test whose items all tap one trait has high internal consistency; a test that mixes, say, vocabulary, arithmetic and spatial items has lower internal consistency even if each kind of item is measured well, because the items disagree about who is high and who is low. Notice that homogeneity raises internal consistency specifically; it need not raise test-retest reliability at all, since a mixed test can be perfectly stable over time.

The fourth is guessing. Whenever someone does not know the answer to a selected-response item and picks one anyway, chance enters the score. Push a case through to see how much. Suppose Sapna, a Class 12 student, truly knows the answers to 20 items on a 40-item true-false test and blindly guesses the other 20. On each guessed item her chance of being right is one half. Over 20 such items she will get about 10 right on average, but the actual number varies from occasion to occasion with a standard deviation of √(20 × 0.5 × 0.5) = √5 = 2.24 items. (This is the standard deviation of a count of chance successes, a result the Research Methodology book derives; here you only need to see that it is not zero.) So two students with exactly the same knowledge can easily differ by three or four marks through luck alone, and the same student could gain or lose that much on a retest. If the same 20 items had four options instead of two, her expected gain from guessing would be 5 items, and the spread of her luck would shrink to √(20 × 0.25 × 0.75) = √3.75 = 1.94 items. The fewer the options, the more luck in the score: true-false items suffer most. The familiar correction for guessing (Chapter 2) subtracts a fraction of the wrong answers to remove the average gain from guessing, but it cannot remove the luck itself, so it is not a cure for the reliability problem.

Finally, ambiguity. An item that can reasonably be read in two ways, or instructions that leave students unsure whether to guess, whether to show working or how long they have, invite each examinee to resolve the ambiguity in his or her own way, and the way chosen has nothing to do with the trait. Clear wording and standard instructions are the cheapest reliability a test developer can buy.

Exam trap

By the Spearman-Brown formula, reliability is improved by increasing the number of parallel items. Not by decreasing the number of items (that lowers it), not by making items more ambiguous (that adds random error), and not by reducing the sample size: the number of people tested changes how precisely a coefficient is estimated, not how large it is expected to be. Two more traps: very easy and very hard items do not make a test more reliable (items near p = 0.50 do), and true-false items carry more guessing error than four- or five-option items.

The group: spread, restriction of range, floors and ceilings

Here is a fact that surprises almost everyone the first time: the very same test, scored the very same way, can be highly reliable in one group and much less reliable in another, without anything about the test changing. The reason lies in the definition from Section 3.1. Reliability is the ratio of true-score variance to observed-score variance, rxx = σ2T / (σ2T + σ2E). The error variance belongs largely to the test: it is set by how many items there are, how much guessing they invite and how they are scored. The true-score variance belongs to the group: it is set by how much the people really differ. Suppose a test's error variance is 4. In a broad group whose true-score variance is 36, the reliability is 36 / (36 + 4) = 36 / 40 = 0.90. Give the identical test to a narrow group whose true-score variance is only 9 and the reliability becomes 9 / (9 + 4) = 9 / 13 = 0.69. The errors are exactly as large as before; what has shrunk is the size of the real differences they are being compared with.

This is called group heterogeneity: the more the members of a group differ from one another on the trait, the higher the reliability coefficient. Its mirror image is restriction of range: whenever a group has been selected so that its scores fall within a narrow band (only the students admitted to an honours course, only patients sick enough to be admitted to a ward, only employees who survived the probation period), the reliability coefficient computed in that group falls. Figure 3.16 shows why with a scatterplot of the kind you met in Section 3.2. Across the whole group of 103 students, the points form a long, narrow cloud running from bottom left to top right, and the correlation between the two testings is 0.84. Now keep only the 24 students whose first score fell between 58 and 72, the shaded band. Their second scores still scatter by about as much as before (the error is unchanged), but their true differences now span only a small slice of the scale, so the cloud they form is a blob with only a weak upward slope, and the correlation falls to 0.44.

Can we predict how much a coefficient will change when a test moves from one group to another? Under one reasonable assumption, yes. If the test measures each person with the same amount of error in both groups, then the standard error of measurement (Section 3.7) is the same in both, and since the squared SEM equals SD2 × (1 − r), we can write SD2old(1 − rold) = SD2new(1 − rnew). Solving for the new coefficient gives:

Remember

rnew = 1 − (SD2old / SD2new) × (1 − rold)

where rold is the reliability in the group where it was estimated (for example the manual's standardisation sample), SDold is the standard deviation of scores in that group, SDnew is the standard deviation in the group you are testing, and rnew is the reliability to expect in your group. It assumes the error variance is the same in both groups.

Work it through with a real-looking case. Urmila is a counsellor at a selective Mysuru college that admits only students with high marks. She wants to use a published aptitude test whose manual reports a reliability of 0.90 in a broad standardisation sample of Class 12 students, where the standard deviation was 15. In her own students, who are all fairly able, the standard deviation turns out to be only 9. Put the numbers in:

SD2old = 15 × 15 = 225, and SD2new = 9 × 9 = 81.
Ratio: 225 / 81 = 2.778.
1 − rold = 1 − 0.90 = 0.10.
rnew = 1 − 2.778 × 0.10 = 1 − 0.278 = 0.722, which rounds to 0.72.

The manual's 0.90 has become about 0.72 in Urmila's college. Now check the assumption from the other side. The SEM in the standardisation sample was 15 × √0.10 = 15 × 0.316 = 4.74; in Urmila's college it is 9 × √(1 − 0.722) = 9 × √0.278 = 9 × 0.527 = 4.74. The two are the same, as they must be. This is the deep lesson of the example: the test measures each of Urmila's students exactly as precisely as before, give or take 4.7 points, but because her students differ from each other far less than the standardisation sample did, that same error is a much larger share of their differences, and ranking them is much harder.

The formula also works in the other direction, and that direction is a warning to anyone reading a test manual. Suppose a manual reports a reliability of 0.94 computed on a pooled sample of Class 8 to Class 12 students together, with a standard deviation of 20, and you will use the test within a single class, where the standard deviation is 10. Then rnew = 1 − (400 / 100) × (1 − 0.94) = 1 − 4 × 0.06 = 1 − 0.24 = 0.76. Pooling ages or grades in a reliability study inflates the coefficient, because the older students simply know more, and that real but irrelevant spread swells the true-score variance. The formula's assumption is worth remembering, too: it presumes equal error variance in both groups, which is reasonable when groups differ naturally in spread, and less safe when a group was selected on the very test being studied or when one group sits near the floor or ceiling of the test.

That last point brings in two more effects. A floor effect occurs when a test is too hard for the group, so that many people score at or near the lowest possible score (or, on a multiple-choice test, near the chance level); a ceiling effect occurs when it is too easy, so that many score at or near the maximum. Either way, people who truly differ receive the same score, the observed spread is squeezed and the coefficient falls. A reasoning test built for Class 12 given to Class 7 children, an adult memory test given to patients with advanced dementia, or a depression inventory given to a community sample in which most people score close to zero are typical cases. Choosing a test whose difficulty suits the group is therefore part of getting reliable scores, and Section 3.9 will show that item response theory makes the point exact: precision is highest where the items are.

Exam trap

A heterogeneous group (wide range of ability) raises the reliability coefficient; a homogeneous or selected group (restricted range) lowers it. A lower coefficient in a selected group does not mean the test got worse: the SEM can stay exactly the same. Do not confuse the size of the group with its spread: testing more people makes the estimate of r more precise but does not raise r. And a reliability study that pools several ages or grades flatters the test.

Clinical link

Clinical samples are often restricted samples. An inpatient ward holds only people sick enough to be admitted; a memory clinic sees mostly people who already have complaints. A symptom scale whose manual reports 0.90 in a general community sample may give a much lower coefficient among inpatients who all score high, simply because they differ less from one another. When you judge how much to trust one patient's score, the standard error of measurement (Section 3.7) is the safer guide, because it changes far less from group to group than the coefficient does.

Time, speed, scoring and the testing situation

The remaining influences have all appeared earlier in the chapter, and they need only to be gathered up and seen together. The interval between testings governs the test-retest coefficient (Section 3.2): the longer the interval, the more room there is for real change in the people, so the lower the coefficient; the shorter the interval, the more memory and practice carry over and inflate it. That is why a retest coefficient means little unless its interval is reported with it.

Speededness affects the single-session methods (Section 3.4). On a test where most people's scores depend on how many items they reach rather than how many they get right, odd and even halves come out almost identical, so a split-half or internal consistency coefficient computed from one timed sitting is spuriously high. The remedy, as Section 3.4 explained, is test-retest, parallel forms, or halves that are timed separately.

Objectivity of scoring matters whenever a human judgement stands between the response and the score (Section 3.6). An objectively keyed multiple-choice test has essentially no scorer error; an essay, a projective protocol or a behaviour rating may have a great deal. Whatever scorer disagreement there is becomes part of the error variance and lowers every kind of reliability computed on the scores.

The testing conditions add their share: a noisy hall, poor light, a generator starting up halfway through, an examiner who reads the instructions differently from one group to the next, or who builds rapport with some children and not with others.
