Standardised administration (Chapter 1) exists precisely to hold these conditions constant. Last come the examinee's temporary states: motivation, fatigue, illness, hunger, test anxiety, a quarrel at home that morning. Here one subtlety from Section 3.1 is worth repeating. These states lower reliability to the extent that they come and go unpredictably, so that they push a person's score up on one occasion and down on another. A person who is always anxious in every test situation is consistently disadvantaged; that constant handicap is not random error, it does not lower reliability, but it does bias the person's scores and so threatens validity.

Table 3.32 showed how one test can have several coefficients; Table 3.33 now gathers every influence on a coefficient in one place.

Table 3.33 What raises and lowers a reliability coefficient.
FactorEffect on reliabilityWhyTaught in
Test length (parallel items)Longer raises it, with diminishing returnsTrue parts add up; random errors partly cancel3.4
Group heterogeneityWide spread raises it; restricted range lowers itTrue variance grows or shrinks; error stays the same3.8
Item difficultyMiddling difficulty raises it; very easy or very hard items add nothingItem variance pq is largest at p = 0.503.5, Chapter 2
Item discrimination and homogeneityHigh item-total correlations and one shared content raise internal consistencyItems agree about who is high and who is low3.5, Chapter 2
GuessingLowers it, most on true-false itemsLuck adds random error3.8, Chapter 2
Floor and ceiling effectsLower itPeople who differ get the same score3.8
Interval between testingsLonger lowers retest r; very short inflates itReal change versus memory and practice3.2
SpeedednessInflates single-session estimatesHalves become almost identical3.4
Objectivity of scoringObjective keys raise it; subjective scoring lowers itScorer disagreement is error3.6
Testing conditions and examinee stateUnstandardised conditions and fluctuating states lower itThey vary unpredictably between occasions and people3.1, Chapter 1
Exam trap

Know the direction of each factor. Raise: more parallel items, a heterogeneous group, items of moderate difficulty, discriminating and homogeneous items, objective scoring, standard conditions. Lower: a restricted group, guessing (especially true-false), floor and ceiling effects, ambiguous items or instructions, subjective scoring, fatigue and fluctuating motivation, a long retest interval. Spuriously raise: speed (on split-half and internal consistency), and a very short retest interval (memory). A trait that is constant within a person, such as chronic test anxiety, biases scores rather than making them unreliable.

Reliability belongs to scores, not to tests

Put the last three subsections together and an important conclusion follows. If a coefficient depends on the method chosen, on the spread of the group and on the conditions of testing, then reliability is not a fixed property stamped on a test booklet like its price. It is a property of a particular set of scores, obtained from a particular group, on a particular occasion, by a particular method. "The test is reliable" is a convenient shorthand for "scores on this test have been found to be reliable in groups like these, under conditions like these".

The point became a research programme in 1998, when Tammi Vacha-Haase proposed reliability generalisation in the journal Educational and Psychological Measurement. Borrowing the logic of meta-analysis, she suggested collecting the reliability coefficients that many different studies had reported for the same test and asking three questions: what is the typical reliability of scores on this test, how much do the coefficients vary from study to study, and what features of the studies (the kind of sample, its spread, the language of administration, the method of estimation) explain that variation. Reliability generalisation studies of many popular scales have since been published, and they keep confirming the lesson of Figure 3.16: coefficients vary, and much of the variation follows the characteristics of the samples.

Three practical rules follow. First, when you report research, estimate reliability in your own sample whenever the design allows it, rather than simply quoting the manual. Second, when you read a manual, ask whether its reliability sample resembles the people you will test, in age, in range of ability, in language and in clinical status. Third, when a test travels from one culture to another, its reliability must be established again in the new population, as Section 3.1 noted for tests adapted for Indian students. A test standardised on English-medium urban students and then used with students taught in Kannada or Hindi in rural schools may differ in difficulty, in the spread of scores and in the meaning of some items; the original coefficient is a hope, not a guarantee.

How much reliability is enough?

Back to Rohini's second question. Is 0.84 good enough? There is no natural law that answers this, only a consideration of purpose, and the consideration runs like this. When you compare the average scores of two groups, the random errors of individuals are averaged too, and averages of random errors shrink towards zero as the groups get larger; a test of modest reliability can therefore compare groups quite well. When you make a decision about one person, there is no averaging to rescue you: that person's score carries its whole error with it. Decisions about individuals therefore need much higher reliability than research on groups, and the more serious the decision, the higher the standard.

The standards most often quoted come from Jum C. Nunnally's Psychometric Theory (second edition, 1978), repeated with Ira H. Bernstein in the third edition (1994). Nunnally suggested that in the early stages of research on a new measure, a reliability of about 0.70 will do; that for basic research, about 0.80 is adequate, and pushing much beyond it is often a waste of time and money; and that in applied settings where important decisions are made about individuals on the basis of their scores, 0.90 is the minimum that should be tolerated and 0.95 the desirable standard. Two decades earlier, in 1956, the American psychometrician Joy Paul Guilford (1897 to 1987) had given similar guidance, as A. K. Singh's widely used Indian textbook summarises it: 0.90 or above for diagnosing individuals, while coefficients as low as about 0.65 may serve for comparing group means or trends. These are conventions offered by experienced psychometricians, not laws, and they were not meant as rigid cut-offs. Table 3.34 lays them out by purpose.

Table 3.34 How much reliability is enough: commonly quoted standards by purpose.
PurposeReliability usually expectedSource of the convention
Early stages of research with a new scaleAbout 0.70Nunnally (1978); Nunnally and Bernstein (1994)
Comparing groups in researchAbout 0.80 (as low as about 0.65 for group trends)Nunnally (1978); Guilford (1956)
Screening, followed by fuller assessment of those who screen positiveAbout 0.80General convention
Decisions about an individual (diagnosis, placement, selection)0.90 at leastNunnally (1978); Guilford (1956)
Very important individual decisions (for example diagnosing intellectual disability)0.95 desirableNunnally (1978)

Why should the step from 0.80 to 0.95 matter so much? Translate the coefficients into error bands with the standard error of measurement of Section 3.7. On an IQ-type scale with a standard deviation of 15, a reliability of 0.70 gives an SEM of 15 × √0.30 = 15 × 0.548 = 8.2 points, so the 95 percent band around a score is about ±16 points (1.96 × 8.2 = 16.1), more than a full standard deviation each way. A reliability of 0.90 gives an SEM of 15 × √0.10 = 4.7 and a band of about ±9 points; 0.95 gives 15 × √0.05 = 15 × 0.224 = 3.4 and a band of about ±7 points. When a single score decides whether a child is called intellectually disabled, whether a patient is fit to stand trial or whether a candidate is selected, a band of ±16 points is useless and a band of ±7 is merely workable.

So Rohini's verdict is clear. With an internal consistency of 0.84 and a retest coefficient of 0.80, the VRT is good enough for research on groups of students and for screening, but it falls short of the 0.90 needed to admit or reject one student on the strength of the score. If she wants to reach 0.90, the rearranged Spearman-Brown formula of Section 3.4 tells her how much longer the test must be: n = 0.90 × (1 − 0.84) / [0.84 × (1 − 0.90)] = (0.90 × 0.16) / (0.84 × 0.10) = 0.144 / 0.084 = 1.71. Forty items multiplied by 1.71 is 68.6, so she would need about 69 items, some 29 more parallel items, provided they are as good as the ones she has.

The same logic explains the typical reliabilities of different kinds of tests. The composite scores of the major individually given intelligence tests usually report coefficients above 0.90 (Chapter 6), which is one reason they are used for weighty decisions; their individual subtests are less reliable, which is why careful clinicians base decisions on composites rather than on single subtests (Section 3.7). Personality inventory scales commonly fall from the 0.70s to the 0.80s or higher, depending on length (Chapter 9). Scores from projective techniques and from brief, single-item or unstructured ratings are often lower, which is one reason Section 3.6 spent so long on scorer reliability (Chapter 10). Remember, too, the question from Section 3.5: a Cronbach's alpha of 0.40 means that, by that estimate, 60 percent of the score variance is error. That is poor internal consistency by any standard, far below what any high-stakes use requires, and, being a reliability coefficient, it says nothing either way about construct validity.

There is a cost to chasing ever higher reliability, and it was named by Lee Joseph Cronbach (1916 to 2001) and Goldine C. Gleser (1915 to 2004) in Psychological Tests and Personnel Decisions (1957; second edition, 1965). They borrowed two terms from the information theory of the engineer Claude Elwood Shannon (1916 to 2001), set out for a wider readership with Warren Weaver in 1949. Bandwidth is the breadth of information a procedure collects, how many different things it tells you about; fidelity is the accuracy with which it conveys each of them, which for a test means its reliability. With a fixed amount of testing time, you cannot maximise both. This is the bandwidth-fidelity dilemma.

Make it concrete. Vinay, a clinical psychologist, has one hour for an intake assessment and can give about 60 questionnaire items. Suppose a 10-item scale of any one problem area has a reliability of 0.70. He could spend all 60 items on one problem, depression, and by Spearman-Brown (n = 6) obtain a reliability of 6 × 0.70 / (1 + 5 × 0.70) = 4.2 / 4.5 = 0.93. He could spend 30 items each on depression and anxiety, obtaining 3 × 0.70 / (1 + 2 × 0.70) = 2.1 / 2.4 = 0.88 for each. Or he could give six 10-item scales (depression, anxiety, anger, sleep, substance use, social functioning), obtaining six rough readings at 0.70 each. The first choice has high fidelity and narrow bandwidth; the last has wide bandwidth and low fidelity. Neither is right in general. A broad screening battery at the start of treatment favours bandwidth; a precise measure of one target symptom tracked across therapy sessions favours fidelity. The dilemma is about breadth against precision within a fixed amount of testing; do not confuse it with the reliability-validity trade-off discussed in Book 2, Section 2.1.5, although the two are related.

Exam trap

Nunnally's standards: about 0.70 for early research, about 0.80 for basic research, 0.90 minimum and 0.95 desirable for important decisions about individuals. A Cronbach's alpha of 0.40 indicates poor internal consistency: not "acceptable for high-stakes clinical use", not "excellent", and not evidence of construct validity, which reliability cannot show. The bandwidth-fidelity dilemma is Cronbach and Gleser's (1957, 1965), borrowed from Shannon's information theory: breadth of coverage against precision of each measurement.

Reliability limits validity: the ceiling and the correction for attenuation

Section 3.1 stated that reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity. You can now see exactly why, and by how much. A validity coefficient (Chapter 4) is a correlation between test scores and something else, a criterion such as examination marks, a supervisor's rating or a diagnosis. Each observed score is a true part plus an error part, and the error part, being random, correlates with nothing. So the test can correlate with a criterion only through its true part, and the less true part it has, the weaker its correlations with everything must be.

How strongly can a test correlate with its own true scores? Section 3.1 showed that the reliability coefficient is σ2T / σ2X; the correlation between observed and true scores is the square root of that ratio, σT / σX. This square root has its own name, the index of reliability:

Remember

rXT = √rxx (the index of reliability)

where rXT is the correlation between observed scores and true scores, and rxx is the reliability coefficient. No test can correlate with any criterion more highly than its index of reliability, and with a fallible criterion the ceiling is lower still: rxy can be at most √(rxx × ryy), where ryy is the reliability of the criterion.

For Rohini's VRT, with rxx = 0.84, the index of reliability is √0.84 = 0.917, about 0.92. However brilliant a measure of verbal reasoning the VRT may be, its scores cannot correlate above 0.92 with anything at all, not even with a perfect measure of verbal reasoning. A test with a reliability of 0.64 has an index of reliability of √0.64 = 0.80, so it cannot correlate above 0.80 with any criterion. If the criterion is itself unreliable, the ceiling drops further. Suppose Rohini checks the VRT against teachers' ratings of verbal reasoning whose reliability is only 0.60: the highest correlation she could possibly observe is √(0.84 × 0.60) = √0.504 = 0.71. If she then finds a correlation of 0.55, that is not a disappointing 0.55 out of 1.00 but a respectable 0.55 out of a possible 0.71.

That last thought leads to a formula as old as the true-score model itself. In his 1904 paper "The proof and measurement of association between two things", Charles Edward Spearman (1863 to 1945) pointed out that measurement error weakens, or attenuation, every correlation between two measures, and he showed how to estimate what the correlation would be between the two traits themselves, if both could be measured without error. This is the correction for attenuation:

Remember

rTxTy = rxy / √(rxx × ryy)

where rxy is the observed correlation between measures x and y, rxx and ryy are their reliabilities, and rTxTy is the estimated correlation between their true scores. Divide by the square root of the product of the reliabilities; do not multiply, and do not forget the square root.

Work the classic examination case. A predictor and a criterion correlate 0.40; their reliabilities are 0.64 and 0.81. First the product of the reliabilities: 0.64 × 0.81 = 0.5184. Its square root: √0.5184 = 0.72 (the same as √0.64 × √0.81 = 0.80 × 0.90 = 0.72, which is the quicker way when the reliabilities are perfect squares). Then divide: 0.40 / 0.72 = 0.556, which rounds to 0.56. The observed 0.40 has been dragged down from an estimated true-score correlation of about 0.56 by the unreliability of the two measures. The wrong answers that examiners offer each come from a specific slip: 0.77 is what you get if you forget the square root (0.40 / 0.5184 = 0.77); 0.29 is what you get if you multiply instead of dividing (0.40 × 0.72 = 0.29); and 0.40 is simply the uncorrected correlation.

In applied work the correction is often used on one side only. Suppose a counselling-aptitude test with a reliability of 0.90 correlates 0.42 with supervisors' ratings of trainee counsellors' skill, ratings whose reliability is only 0.60. Correcting both sides estimates how closely the two underlying qualities are related: √(0.90 × 0.60) = √0.54 = 0.735, and 0.42 / 0.735 = 0.57. But a selection committee will use the test as it is, error and all; what it wants to know is how well the actual test predicts true counselling skill, uncontaminated by the unreliable ratings. For that question we correct for the criterion only: 0.42 / √0.60 = 0.42 / 0.775 = 0.54. This one-sided correction for attenuation is the usual practice in validating selection tests, where the criterion is typically far less reliable than the test (Chapter 4).

Three cautions keep the correction honest. First, a corrected correlation is an estimate of a hypothetical quantity, the correlation that would hold if perfect measures existed; it does not change the accuracy of predictions made with the real, imperfect test. Second, it is only as good as the reliability estimates fed into it, and they must count the right kind of error: if the two measures were taken months apart, a retest coefficient suits better than alpha. Third, when the reliabilities are underestimated, or the samples are small, the correction can overshoot into impossible values. An observed correlation of 0.50 between two measures whose reliabilities are reported as 0.50 and 0.40 "corrects" to 0.50 / √0.20 = 0.50 / 0.447 = 1.12, a correlation greater than 1, which can only mean that something in the inputs is wrong.

Exam trap

The index of reliability is √rxx, the correlation between observed and true scores, and it is the ceiling for any validity coefficient: reliability 0.64 means validity can be at most 0.80; reliability 0.81, at most 0.90. The correction for attenuation (Spearman, 1904) is rxy / √(rxxryy): for 0.40 with reliabilities 0.64 and 0.81 the answer is 0.56, not 0.77 (no square root), 0.29 (multiplied) or 0.40 (uncorrected). It is not the Spearman-Brown formula, which predicts reliability from test length (Section 3.4).

3.9 Beyond Classical Test Theory: Generalizability Theory and Item Response TheoryLower yield · 1 Q

Ankur is a school psychologist in Bengaluru. Teachers keep referring children to him for "poor attention", and he has built a short Classroom Attention Rating: a teacher watches a child through one class period and rates the child's attention on a scale from 0 to 20. Before he uses it to decide which children need a fuller assessment, he wants to know how dependable a rating is. But "dependable" turns out to be several questions at once. Would a different teacher, watching the same child in the same period, give the same rating? Would the same teacher, watching on a different day, give the same rating? And the practical question that matters most to him: how many teachers, and how many days, does he need before he can trust a child's average rating?

Classical test theory, the theory of the first eight sections of this chapter, can answer each of the first two questions separately, with an inter-rater coefficient (Section 3.6) and a retest coefficient (Section 3.2). It has no way to answer them together, and no direct way at all to answer the third. This section introduces two theories that go beyond the classical one. Generalizability theory keeps the classical idea of reliability as a ratio of variances but splits the error into its separate sources, so that Ankur can see which source is largest and design his measurement accordingly. Item response theory replaces the single reliability coefficient with a precision that changes from one level of the trait to another, and so explains why a test can be precise for average students and imprecise for the very able or the very weak. The section ends by gathering every method in the chapter into one table.

What classical test theory cannot do

Classical test theory has served psychology for more than a century, and it remains the theory behind most test manuals. Its strength is its simplicity: X = T + E, with a few modest assumptions. That same simplicity sets four limits, and each of them has appeared somewhere in this chapter already.

  1. It has one undifferentiated error term. E is simply "everything that is not true score". Whether a rating is off because of the rater, the day, the items or the room, the classical model lumps it all into one E. That is why Table 3.32 found several different coefficients for one test: each method lets one source of error vary and quietly holds the others constant, and the theory offers no way to estimate several sources at once.
  2. Its coefficients depend on the sample. As Section 3.8 showed, the same test gives a high coefficient in a heterogeneous group and a low one in a restricted group. A classical reliability describes scores in a group, not the test.
  3. It gives one standard error of measurement for everybody. The SEM of Section 3.7 is computed once for the whole group and applied to every score, high or low. Yet common sense, and the floor and ceiling effects of Section 3.8, suggest that a test cannot be equally precise for people at every level of ability.
  4. Its item and person statistics depend on each other. An item's difficulty, the proportion who pass it, depends on who took it: the same item is "easy" in a strong group and "hard" in a weak one. A person's score depends on which items were given: the same student scores higher on an easy form than on a hard one. To compare people who took different items, classical theory needs a separate equating study (Chapter 5).

Generalizability theory answers the first limit directly and helps with the second. Item response theory answers the third and fourth. Take them in that order.

Generalizability theory: from true score to universe score

The idea grew out of a long project at the University of Illinois led by Lee Cronbach, whose coefficient alpha you met in Section 3.5, together with Goldine Gleser, Cronbach's partner in the bandwidth-fidelity work of Section 3.8. Its first full statement was a 1963 paper by Cronbach, Nageswari Rajaratnam and Gleser in the British Journal of Statistical Psychology, with a title that announced its ambition: "Theory of generalizability: a liberalization of reliability theory". The complete theory appeared in 1972 as a book by Cronbach, Gleser, Harinder Nanda and Rajaratnam, The Dependability of Behavioral Measurements: Theory of Generalizability for Scores and Profiles. Its preface explains the motive in one sentence: "The tidy theory of error laid down for psychology at the start of the century by Spearman and Brown has always seemed just a little too tidy to describe the perverse behavior of real data."

Key study: who built generalizability theory (1957 to 1972)

The preface of the 1972 book tells the story. The intensive work began in 1957 at the University of Illinois, supported by the university's Bureau of Educational Research and the US National Institute of Mental Health, with Cronbach and Gleser as principal investigators. Nageswari Rajaratnam was a full-time associate from 1957 to 1960; she shared authorship of the basic technical reports and, in the authors' words, contributed many of the key ideas. She later moved to the University of British Columbia and then to a research post at the University of Minnesota, and died before the book was written up (the preface gives 1964; a journal footnote gives December 1963). The title page lists her as deceased.

When Cronbach, by then at Stanford University, obtained funds to turn the scattered papers into a monograph, his assistant throughout 1966 was Harinder Nanda, whose studies of reliability across batteries of tests (together with studies by Kenneth W. Travers) opened the way to the book's multivariate chapters. On the title page of the 1972 book, Nanda's affiliation is given as the A. N. Sinha Institute of Social Studies, the social science research institute in Patna, so one of the four authors of the standard reference on generalizability theory wrote from an Indian institute. The book's Venn diagrams of variance components, which you will meet in Figure 3.17, were suggested to the authors by Haruo Yanai.

The central move of generalizability theory (G theory) is to change the question. Classical theory asks: how much of this observed score is true score? G theory asks: how accurately can we generalise from this observed score to the score the person would obtain under all the other conditions we would have been equally happy to use? When Ankur obtains one rating of a child from one teacher on one Tuesday, he does not care about that teacher or that Tuesday in particular. Any trained teacher on any ordinary school day would have been acceptable to him. The set of all the observations he would accept as interchangeable (every trained teacher, every ordinary day) is called the universe of admissible observations. The average score a child would obtain over that whole universe is the child's universe score, G theory's counterpart of the classical true score. A child's rating is dependable to the extent that it lets us generalise to that universe score.

The dimensions along which the acceptable observations can vary are called facets, and the particular values of a facet are called its conditions. In Ankur's study there are two facets: raters (the conditions are the individual teachers: the class teacher, the mathematics teacher, the English teacher and so on) and occasions (Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday). In other studies the facets might be items, forms of a test, settings, tasks or scorers. The people whose differences we actually want to measure, here the children, are called the object of measurement. Their variation is the signal; variation attached to every facet is potential noise.

Two more distinctions complete the vocabulary. A design is crossed when every condition of one facet meets every condition of another, as when every teacher rates every child on every day (written p × r × o, read "persons crossed with raters crossed with occasions"). It is nested when each person has his or her own conditions, as when each child is rated by a different pair of teachers (written r : p, "raters nested within persons"); nesting saves effort but makes some sources of error impossible to separate. And a facet is random when its conditions are a sample from a larger set we want to generalise to (any trained teacher), and fixed when we care only about the conditions actually used (these two particular subjects, English and mathematics, and no others).

How does G theory find out how much each facet contributes? With the analysis of variance (ANOVA), the technique that divides the total variation in a set of scores into parts attributable to different sources; the Research Methodology book teaches it in full. G theory did not invent the idea of applying ANOVA to reliability. Cyril Hoyt had done so in 1941 for a table of persons by items, and his coefficient turned out to equal KR-20 (Section 3.5). What G theory added was the generalisation: any number of facets, crossed or nested, random or fixed, each with its own estimated share of the variance.

The G study: how big is each source of error?

The first step is a G study (generalizability study), whose sole purpose is to estimate how much variance each source contributes. Ankur runs a fully crossed one. He asks three teachers each to rate the same 30 Class 4 children, on each of two days. Every child therefore receives six ratings, one from each teacher on each day. An ANOVA of this persons × raters × occasions table gives a mean square for every source, and each mean square is a known mixture of the underlying variance components. A little algebra separates them. In the simplest possible case, a study with only persons and two raters, the person component is (mean square for persons − residual mean square) / 2; if those mean squares were 14 and 2, the person variance would be (14 − 2) / 2 = 6. With two facets there are seven sources and the algebra is longer, so it is left to the computer, but the principle is the same. The result is a set of variance components, one for each source, each describing the variance contributed to a single rating. Ankur's are in Table 3.35.

Table 3.35 Ankur's G study: variance components for a single rating on the Classroom Attention Rating (persons × raters × occasions, all crossed).
SourceVariance componentPercent of totalWhat it means
Persons (p)6.050.0Children really differ in attention: the signal
Raters (r)0.54.2Some teachers are more lenient with everyone
Occasions (o)0.32.5On some days every child is rated higher
Persons × raters (p × r)1.210.0Teachers disagree about which children attend better
Persons × occasions (p × o)1.815.0A child's attention, relative to others, changes from day to day
Raters × occasions (r × o)0.21.7A teacher's leniency changes from day to day
Residual (p × r × o, plus unmeasured error)2.016.7Everything else, which this design cannot separate
Total12.0100.0Variance of a single rating (percentages rounded to one decimal)

Read the table as a story about Ankur's rating scale. Half the variance of a single rating, 6.0 out of 12.0, reflects real differences between children; that is the good news. The rest is spread over six sources of error, and they are far from equal. The largest single source after the residual is the persons × occasions interaction, 1.8: children's attention, relative to their classmates, really does vary from day to day, so a rating on one day is a shaky guide to a child's typical attention. Next comes persons × raters, 1.2: teachers do not fully agree on which children are attentive. The main effects of raters (0.5) and occasions (0.3) are small: some teachers are a little more lenient overall, and some days a little better overall, but these shifts move everyone together. The residual, 2.0, mixes the three-way interaction (this teacher saw this child differently on this day) with every source the design did not include, such as which lesson was being taught; with only one rating per teacher per child per day, the two cannot be told apart.

Figure 3.17 shows the same components in the two ways G theory has traditionally drawn them. Panel A is a Venn diagram of the kind the 1972 book popularised: one circle for each source of variation, with each region of overlap standing for an interaction. Panel B anticipates the next two subsections.

Relative and absolute decisions

Before the variance components can be turned into a coefficient, G theory insists on a question classical theory never asked: what kind of decision will the scores be used for? There are two kinds.

A relative decision depends only on where people stand compared with one another: choosing the ten least attentive children in a class for a small-group programme, or correlating attention with reading marks in research. For such decisions, only the sources of error that can change people's rank order matter. Those are the interactions with persons: p × r (teachers disagree about who is more attentive), p × o (children's relative standing shifts from day to day) and the residual. A main effect of raters does not matter for ranking: if one lenient teacher rates every child two points higher, the ranks are untouched. Their sum is the relative error variance, written σ2δ (sigma squared delta).

An absolute decision depends on the score's actual level: referring every child whose average rating falls below 8 for a fuller assessment for attention problems, or deciding whether a student has reached a mastery standard. Now everything that moves a score up or down matters, including whether the child happened to be rated by a lenient teacher or on a good day. The absolute error variance, σ2Δ (sigma squared capital delta), adds the main effects of the facets and their interaction to the relative error. Absolute error is therefore always at least as large as relative error.

Each kind of error gives its own coefficient, built exactly like the classical reliability coefficient: signal over signal plus noise.

Remember

Generalizability coefficient (relative decisions): Eρ2 = σ2p / (σ2p + σ2δ)

Dependability coefficient (absolute decisions): Φ = σ2p / (σ2p + σ2Δ)

where σ2p is the universe-score (persons) variance, σ2δ is the relative error variance (the person interactions), and σ2Δ is the absolute error variance (the person interactions plus the facet main effects and their interactions). Eρ2 is read "E rho squared"; Φ is the Greek capital phi.

The generalizability coefficient Eρ2 plays the part of the classical reliability coefficient; indeed classical reliability is simply the generalizability coefficient of a design with a single facet. The dependability coefficient Φ (also called the index of dependability or the phi coefficient; do not confuse it with the phi correlation for two-by-two tables) grew out of work on criterion-referenced and mastery tests within G theory, notably by Robert L. Brennan and Michael T. Kane in 1977.

Now push Ankur's numbers through for a single rating: one teacher, one day.

Relative error: σ2δ = p × r + p × o + residual = 1.2 + 1.8 + 2.0 = 5.0.
Eρ2 = 6.0 / (6.0 + 5.0) = 6.0 / 11.0 = 0.545, which rounds to 0.55.
Absolute error: σ2Δ = 5.0 + r + o + r × o = 5.0 + 0.5 + 0.3 + 0.2 = 6.0.
Φ = 6.0 / (6.0 + 6.0) = 6.0 / 12.0 = 0.50.

A single rating, then, is a poor basis for any decision about a child: barely more than half of its rank-order variance, and exactly half of its total variance, reflects the child's typical attention.

Here is the moment G theory earns its keep. Suppose Ankur had instead done two classical studies. In an inter-rater study, two teachers rate the same children in the same period, and he correlates their ratings. What do two teachers watching the same lesson share? The child's universe score (6.0), and also that day's particular performance, the p × o component (1.8), because both saw the same day. So the inter-rater coefficient would come out at about (6.0 + 1.8) / 11.0 = 7.8 / 11.0 = 0.71. In a retest study, one teacher rates the same children on two days. What do those two ratings share? The universe score again, and also that teacher's personal view of each child, the p × r component (1.2). So the retest coefficient would be about (6.0 + 1.2) / 11.0 = 7.2 / 11.0 = 0.65. Both classical coefficients look respectable, 0.71 and 0.65, and both are too optimistic, because each treats one source of error as if it were true score. Only by counting the rater and the day errors together does Ankur see that a single rating's dependability for ranking children is 0.55. A source of error that a design holds constant, and therefore cannot see, is sometimes called a hidden facet; G theory's great service is to bring hidden facets into the open.

Exam trap

G theory distinguishes relative decisions (ranking people: generalizability coefficient Eρ2, error from the person interactions only) from absolute decisions (meeting a cut-off: dependability coefficient Φ, which also counts facet main effects). Φ is never larger than Eρ2. The universe score is G theory's counterpart of the classical true score, and classical reliability is a special, one-facet case of G theory, not a rival to it.

The D study: designing a better measurement

Ankur's real question was practical: how many teachers and how many days? This is the job of the second step, the D study (decision study). A D study takes the variance components from the G study and asks what the coefficients would be if each child's score were the average of n'r raters and n'o occasions. The rule is simple. Averaging over conditions shrinks every error component that involves those conditions: a component involving raters is divided by the number of raters, a component involving occasions by the number of occasions, and a component involving both by their product. The persons component is not divided at all, because every child keeps the same universe score however many ratings are averaged.

Remember

σ2δ = σ2pr / n'r + σ2po / n'o + σ2pro,e / (n'r × n'o)

σ2Δ = σ2δ + σ2r / n'r + σ2o / n'o + σ2ro / (n'r × n'o)

where n'r and n'o are the numbers of raters and occasions to be averaged in the real use, and each σ2 is a variance component from the G study (pr for persons × raters, po for persons × occasions, pro,e for the residual, ro for raters × occasions).

Work the design Ankur is considering: each child rated by two teachers, each on two days, the four ratings averaged.

Relative error: 1.2 / 2 + 1.8 / 2 + 2.0 / (2 × 2) = 0.6 + 0.9 + 0.5 = 2.0.
Eρ2 = 6.0 / (6.0 + 2.0) = 6.0 / 8.0 = 0.75.
Absolute error: 2.0 + 0.5 / 2 + 0.3 / 2 + 0.2 / (2 × 2) = 2.0 + 0.25 + 0.15 + 0.05 = 2.45.
Φ = 6.0 / (6.0 + 2.45) = 6.0 / 8.45 = 0.710, which rounds to 0.71.

Four ratings instead of one have lifted the generalizability coefficient from 0.55 to 0.75 and the dependability coefficient from 0.50 to 0.71. Panel B of Figure 3.17 shows how: the persons part of the bar stays at 6.0 while the error part shrinks from 6.0 to 2.45. Table 3.36 extends the same arithmetic to other designs.

Table 3.36 Ankur's D study: predicted coefficients when each child's score is the average of several ratings (computed from Table 3.35, rounded to two decimals).
RatersDaysRatings per childEρ2 (ranking)Φ (cut-off)
1110.550.50
2120.640.60
1220.660.61
4140.700.66
1440.740.68
2240.750.71
2360.800.76
2480.820.78
3390.830.80

Three lessons leap out of the table. First, averaging works, exactly as the Spearman-Brown formula promised in Section 3.4; in fact the Spearman-Brown formula is nothing but a D study with a single facet. Second, it matters which facet you spend your effort on. Four ratings can be obtained as four teachers on one day (0.70), one teacher on four days (0.74), or two teachers on two days (0.75). More days beat more teachers here because the persons × occasions component (1.8) is larger than the persons × raters component (1.2): children's day-to-day variation is a bigger problem than teachers' disagreement. A classical analysis, with its single E, could never have told Ankur that. Third, the table answers his question directly. For ranking children for a programme, two teachers on three days (0.80) meet the research standard of Section 3.8. For referral decisions against a cut-off, where Φ is the right coefficient, he needs about three teachers on three days to reach 0.80, and if the referral were a weighty individual decision he would want more still.

The D study also teaches what not to spend money on. If Ankur had planned to improve dependability by training teachers more intensively, the G study tells him that, at best, training could shrink the rater components (0.5, 1.2 and 0.2); it could do nothing about children's day-to-day variation, which only more days of observation can average away. A G study is, in this sense, a diagnosis of a measurement procedure, and a D study is the treatment plan.

Exam trap

Generalizability theory (Cronbach and colleagues, 1963 paper, 1972 book) extends classical test theory by decomposing observed-score variance into several facets (raters, items, occasions and so on) with analysis of variance, so that different sources of error are distinguished. The wrong options: "treating measurement error as undifferentiated" describes classical theory, the very thing G theory improves on; "eliminating the need for any reliability estimate" is false, because G theory produces reliability-like coefficients (Eρ2 and Φ); and "modelling response curves like IRT" describes item response theory, a different framework. Keep the two steps apart: the G study estimates the variance components; the D study uses them to forecast the coefficients of different designs. And watch the word itself: when a question asks which term refers to the consistency of a measure, "generalizability" is a distractor in its everyday sense (findings carrying over to other people and settings, Section 3.1) and the key is reliability; in G theory, by contrast, the generalizability coefficient is a reliability coefficient.

Clinical link

Clinical psychologists rely on exactly the kind of measurement Ankur is studying: behaviour ratings by parents and teachers, structured observation in the clinic, symptom ratings by interviewers, and scores on performance tasks in skills examinations. Each involves facets (raters, occasions, settings, tasks), and each can mislead if its dependability is judged from a single inter-rater coefficient. When you read that two observers agreed well, ask whether they watched the same session; if they did, the session-to-session variation of the behaviour is hidden inside their agreement. Book 2, Section 2.8.7, describes the observer errors that G theory helps to measure.

Item response theory: precision that changes with ability

The second modern theory starts from a different place. Instead of the total score, it models each item. Chapter 2 teaches item response theory (IRT) in full; here is the two-paragraph recap you need. For each item, IRT draws an item characteristic curve: the probability of answering the item correctly, plotted against the person's level on the underlying trait, written θ (theta) and usually scaled so that the population has a mean of 0 and a standard deviation of 1. The curve is S-shaped, rising from near zero for people far below the item's level to near one for people far above it. Its position along the θ axis is the item's difficulty, b: the ability level at which a person has a 50 percent chance of passing (in models without a guessing parameter). Its steepness is the item's discrimination, a: a steep curve separates people just below b from people just above it sharply. In the two-parameter logistic model, the probability is P(θ) = 1 / (1 + e−a(θ − b)), where e is the constant 2.718; a three-parameter model adds a guessing parameter c, a floor for people who know nothing.

The simplest member of the family, with every item equally discriminating, was published in 1960 by the Danish mathematician Georg Rasch (1901 to 1980) in Probabilistic Models for Some Intelligence and Attainment Tests, and is called the Rasch model. Much of the wider theory was developed in the United States by Frederic M. Lord (1912 to 2000) of the Educational Testing Service, and gathered in Lord and Melvin Novick's Statistical Theories of Mental Test Scores (1968), which included chapters by Allan Birnbaum on the logistic models and on how much information an item provides. That last idea is the one this chapter needs.

An item's item information function describes how well the item distinguishes between people at each level of θ. An item is informative where its characteristic curve is steep, because there a small difference in ability makes a large difference in the chance of success; it is uninformative where the curve is flat, far below or far above its difficulty, because there almost everyone fails or almost everyone passes. For the two-parameter logistic model the information is:

Remember

Ii(θ) = a2 × P(θ) × [1 − P(θ)]

where Ii(θ) is the information of item i at ability θ, a is the item's discrimination, and P(θ) is the probability of passing the item at that ability. Information is greatest at θ = b, where P = 0.50, and there equals a2 / 4. (Some books write the model with a scaling constant of 1.7 inside; the shape is the same.)

Push one item through. Take an item with discrimination a = 1.5 and difficulty b = 0. For an average person, θ = 0, the probability of passing is exactly 0.50, so the information is 1.52 × 0.50 × 0.50 = 2.25 × 0.25 = 0.5625, about 0.56. For a very able person at θ = 2, first find P: a(θ − b) = 1.5 × 2 = 3, and e−3 = 0.0498, so P = 1 / (1 + 0.0498) = 1 / 1.0498 = 0.9526, and 1 − P = 0.0474. The information is 2.25 × 0.9526 × 0.0474 = 2.25 × 0.0452 = 0.102, about 0.10. The same item gives roughly five and a half times as much information about average people as about very able ones, which makes perfect sense: almost every very able person passes it, so it can hardly tell them apart.

Now the decisive property. Item informations simply add up. The test information function, I(θ), is the sum of the information functions of all the items in the test, and it is related to precision by one of the most useful formulas in modern measurement:

Remember

SE(θ) = 1 / √I(θ)

where I(θ) is the test information at ability θ and SE(θ) is the conditional standard error of measurement, the standard error of a person's estimated ability at that level. More information means a smaller standard error; four times the information halves the error.

Consider a 30-item reasoning test calibrated under the two-parameter model, every item with a = 1.5, and the difficulties spread evenly from −1.5 to +1.5. Adding the 30 item informations at each level of θ gives the curve in panel A of Figure 3.18. At θ = 0 the test information is 11.92, so the standard error there is 1 / √11.92 = 1 / 3.45 = 0.29. At θ = 2.5, far above most of the items, the information has fallen to 2.78, so the standard error is 1 / √2.78 = 1 / 1.67 = 0.60, about twice as large. At θ = 3 the information is only 1.47 and the standard error 1 / 1.21 = 0.83, nearly three times as large as in the middle. In practical terms, the 95 percent band around an average student's ability estimate is about ±0.57 (1.96 × 0.29), while around a very able student's it is about ±1.18 (1.96 × 0.60). The same test measures some people twice as precisely as others.

Panel B also shows what the classical picture hides. A classical analysis of this test, given to a normal population, would report one figure for everybody. Averaging the squared standard errors over such a population gives a single typical error of about 0.33, the flat line, corresponding to an overall reliability of about 0.89. That single figure is too pessimistic for the students in the middle and far too optimistic for the students at the extremes, which is the third limit of classical theory, listed at the start of this section, made visible. A handy translation between the two languages follows from the formulas: when θ has a standard deviation of 1 in the population, a test information of I behaves like a reliability of about 1 − 1 / I at that level. Information of 5 corresponds to a reliability of about 0.80, information of 10 to about 0.90, information of 20 to about 0.95. So this test behaves like a 0.92-reliable test for average students (1 − 1 / 11.92 = 0.92) and like a 0.64-reliable test for students at θ = 2.5 (1 − 1 / 2.78 = 0.64).

Why does this matter? Because a test is precise where its items are. A test meant to pick out gifted students needs many hard items; a screening test meant to detect severe impairment needs many items near the cut-off for impairment, and a test whose items cluster around the average will be imprecise exactly where the decision is made. This is the exact form of the floor and ceiling problem of Section 3.8, and it tells a test developer what kind of items to add, not merely how many.

The most striking use of information is computerised adaptive testing (CAT). The items are first calibrated into an item bank, a large pool of items whose parameters are known. The computer starts with an item of middling difficulty, estimates the person's θ from the answer, then picks from the bank the item that would give the most information at that estimate, and repeats. A person who keeps answering correctly is soon given hard items; one who keeps failing is soon given easy ones. Each person thus spends the testing time on the items that are informative for him or her, and testing can stop when the standard error falls below a target. Adaptive tests typically reach the precision of a much longer fixed test with far fewer items, and because every item is on the same θ scale, people who answered completely different items can still be compared directly, which answers the fourth limit of classical theory. The Graduate Record Examination (GRE) General Test, for example, became computer-adaptive in 1993; since its 2011 revision it adapts section by section rather than item by item. In health care, item banks such as the United States' Patient-Reported Outcomes Measurement Information System (PROMIS) offer adaptive tests of symptoms such as depression and fatigue. Table 3.37 sets the classical and IRT views of precision side by side.

Table 3.37 Precision in classical test theory and in item response theory.
FeatureClassical test theoryItem response theory
What is modelledThe total score: X = T + EEach item's response curve
PrecisionOne reliability coefficient and one SEM for everybodyInformation and SE(θ) at each level of ability
Dependence on the sampleItem difficulty and reliability change with the groupItem parameters stay the same across groups when the model fits (up to the choice of scale)
People who took different itemsNeed an equating study (Chapter 5)Scored on the same θ scale
Effect of adding itemsLonger test, higher reliability (Spearman-Brown)Information rises where the new items are located
AssumptionsWeak: random error, uncorrelated with true scoresStrong: one dimension, local independence, a model that fits
Data neededModest samplesLarge samples, often several hundred or more
Exam trap

In IRT, precision is not the same for everyone: the standard error depends on θ, SE(θ) = 1 / √I(θ), and is usually smallest in the middle of the ability range covered by the items. Item informations add to give the test information. Information is greatest where the item characteristic curve is steepest, at θ = b. Classical theory's single SEM is an average that hides this. Computerised adaptive testing chooses each next item to give the most information at the current ability estimate. Do not confuse IRT with G theory: IRT models item response curves; G theory partitions variance into facets.

Every method in one table

You have now met every way psychologists estimate reliability, from a correlation between two sittings to the information curve of an adaptive test. They can look like a bewildering menu, but they all answer one question, how much of the variation in these scores is consistent and how much is error, and they differ only in which source of error they let into view. The practical rule that unites the chapter is therefore simple: first name the error that matters for your decision, then choose the method that counts it. If you will retest patients after therapy, time sampling matters, so you need a retest coefficient over a suitable interval. If you will compare a child's score with a cut-off, content sampling and scorer error matter, and the standard error of measurement must be reported with the score. If several raters and occasions are involved, a G study is the honest way to count them all. Table 3.38 gathers every method.

Table 3.38 Every method of estimating reliability in this chapter.

Remember

Every reliability method answers "how much of the score is consistent?" by choosing what may vary between the measurements it compares. Time varies: test-retest. Items vary: parallel forms, split-half, KR-20, alpha. Scorers vary: inter-rater methods. Several facets vary at once: generalizability theory. Precision varies with ability: item response theory.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Giving a test to a group with a narrow range of ability, such as students already selected for an honours course, makes the reliability coefficient , even though the standard error of measurement may stay the same.
  2. A test manual reports r = 0.91 in a sample whose SD was 15. In a group whose SD is 7.5, the expected reliability is .
  3. According to Nunnally, important decisions about individuals need a reliability of at least , with 0.95 desirable.
  4. Cronbach and Gleser's name for the trade-off between how much a test covers and how precisely it measures each thing is the dilemma.
  5. The correlation between observed scores and true scores, √rxx, is called the ; a test with reliability 0.81 cannot correlate above with any criterion.
  6. A predictor and a criterion correlate 0.30, and their reliabilities are 0.81 and 0.64. Corrected for attenuation, their true scores correlate about .
  7. In generalizability theory, the study that estimates the variance components is the study, and the study that forecasts coefficients for designs with different numbers of raters or occasions is the study.
  8. For a decision against a fixed cut-off, generalizability theory uses the dependability coefficient, written , which also counts the main effects of the facets as error.
  9. In a persons × raters design, the person variance is 4 and the residual (person-by-rater plus error) variance is 4. One rater gives a generalizability coefficient of 0.50; averaging four raters gives .
  10. If the test information at some ability level is 16, the standard error of measurement at that level is .

Answers: (1) lower; (2) 0.64 [1 − (225 / 56.25) × (1 − 0.91) = 1 − 4 × 0.09 = 1 − 0.36 = 0.64]; (3) 0.90; (4) bandwidth-fidelity; (5) index of reliability, 0.90 [√0.81]; (6) 0.42 [√(0.81 × 0.64) = 0.90 × 0.80 = 0.72, and 0.30 / 0.72 = 0.417]; (7) G (generalizability), D (decision); (8) Φ (phi); (9) 0.80 [relative error 4 / 4 = 1, so 4 / (4 + 1) = 0.80]; (10) 0.25 [1 / √16 = 1 / 4].

The case of the two English papers and the geometry paper, revisited

Go back to the 142 English teachers with the vocabulary of this chapter. Without using the name, Starch and Elliott had designed a study of scorer reliability (Section 3.6): the scripts, the questions and the pupils' work were the same for every marker, so the only thing allowed to vary was the scorer, and every difference between the marks was scorer error. Section 3.6.2 distinguished two kinds of disagreement, and both can be read into their design. A teacher who marks every script high, or every script low, commits a constant leniency or severity error, which leaves a correlation untouched but ruins absolute agreement. A teacher who disagrees about the order of the scripts lowers even the correlation, and 19 teachers marked paper B above paper A although on average it was marked 8 points lower. Against a pass mark both kinds count, which is why 22 of the 142 teachers failed a pupil whom his own teacher had given 75. The geometry paper adds a lesson from Section 3.1. Teachers who took heavy marks off for untidiness were not only disagreeing with the others; they were partly measuring neatness instead of geometry, a reminder that agreement among markers is necessary for valid marks but not sufficient. And the five geometry teachers of the pupil's own school let you put a number on the scorer part of the error. Treat their marks, 70, 65, 60, 70 and 59, as five independent measurements of one script. Their mean, 324 / 5 = 64.8, estimates the mark the script would earn from markers in general; their standard deviation, about 4.7 points (dividing by the number of marks, as this chapter's worked examples do), is a rough, five-marker estimate of the standard error of measurement due to scorers alone (Section 3.7). The 95 percent band, 64.8 ± (1.96 × 4.7) = 64.8 ± 9.2, runs from about 55.6 to 74.0 and contains the pass mark of 70. The marks cannot tell you on which side of the line this pupil's geometry truly lay, and that is before content sampling (which questions were set) and time sampling (which day the pupil wrote) add their share of error.

The remedies that followed are the ones this chapter has taught. The "new-type" objective examinations scored with a key removed scorer error almost completely, because objectivity is a property of the scoring procedure (Section 3.6.1). But they left content sampling and time sampling error untouched, so an objective paper still needs enough items, and the Spearman-Brown formula (Sections 3.4 and 3.8) says how many: a paper of a few long answers is a short test, and adding parallel items raises reliability, with diminishing returns. Where judgement cannot be removed, as in an essay, rubrics, anchor scripts, rater training and double marking narrow the disagreement, and averaging two independent markers is the Spearman-Brown formula applied to raters: it is how the single-marker absolute-agreement coefficient of 0.69 for Rajat and Hiral became 0.81 for the average of their two marks (Section 3.6.6). Generalizability theory (Section 3.9) gives Starch and Elliott's design its modern reading: two papers, the objects of measurement, crossed with a rater facet of 142 teachers. The rater main effect, some teachers marking everything high and others everything low, matters for an absolute decision such as pass or fail, and the dependability coefficient Φ counts it as error; the paper-by-rater interaction, the teachers who reversed the order of the two papers, matters even for ranking, and the generalizability coefficient Eρ2 counts it too. A D study would then ask how many markers must be averaged before one script's mark can be trusted against a pass mark, the question behind the double valuation, moderation and re-evaluation of examination scripts in India today. A mark means something, Starch and Elliott showed, only when you know how much of it belongs to the marker, and this chapter has given you the tools to find out.

Chapter summary

3.1 What Reliability Means: True Scores, Error and Classical Test Theory. Reliability is the consistency of measurement across occasions, items, forms or scorers, or equally its freedom from random measurement error; consistency, stability, dependability and precision are faces of one idea. It is not validity, generalisability or replicability, and it is necessary but not sufficient for validity. Random error varies unpredictably, averages out and lowers reliability; systematic (constant) error pushes every score the same way, leaving reliability intact while damaging validity. Classical test theory, founded on Charles Edward Spearman's work of 1904 and set out by Harold Gulliksen (1950) and by Frederic M. Lord and Melvin R. Novick (1968), writes X = T + E, with errors that average zero and are uncorrelated with true scores and with each other. The reliability coefficient, the true-score share of observed variance, is read without squaring. Parallel tests correlate at that value, and the domain-sampling model treats a test as a sample of items.

3.2 Test-Retest Reliability: Stability over Time. Test-retest reliability gives the same test to the same people on two occasions and correlates the scores; the result is the coefficient of stability, a name Lee Joseph Cronbach set out in 1947. It detects time sampling error but not content sampling error, because the same items appear twice; an equal gain for everyone leaves the correlation unchanged. Too short an interval inflates the coefficient through memory, practice and carry-over; too long an interval lowers it through real change; two weeks to a month is usual. Reactivity, shown by Jim McCambridge and Kypros Kypri (2011), means being tested can change people. The method suits stable traits such as intelligence and personality, not changing states, as the trait and state scales of Charles Donald Spielberger's State-Trait Anxiety Inventory (1970) show. Over decades, a retest describes the stability of the attribute itself (Ian Deary and colleagues; Brent Roberts and Wendy DelVecchio).

3.3 Alternate and Parallel Forms: Equivalence. A second form allows retesting without memory for items, before-and-after measurement, make-up examinations and test security. Strictly parallel forms give everyone the same true score with equal error variance, so they have equal means, variances and correlations with other measures; Harold Gulliksen added the psychological criterion of the same content and format, and Frank S. Freeman's practical criteria describe alternate forms built to one blueprint. Looser relations are tau-equivalent, essentially tau-equivalent and congeneric (Karl Jöreskog, 1971). Forms are built from a matched item pool, checked on data, and equated by linear or equipercentile methods when they differ. Two forms at one sitting give the coefficient of equivalence, which detects content sampling error; two forms after an interval give the coefficient of stability and equivalence, which counts both errors and is usually the lowest. Counterbalancing spreads order effects. Forms are costly and reduce practice effects without removing them.

3.4 Split-Half Reliability and the Spearman-Brown Prophecy Formula. Split-half reliability scores one sitting twice, on two halves, and correlates them, so it detects content sampling error only. First-half against second-half splits are poor, odd-even splits are usual and matched random halves are best; the major challenge is making the halves nearly equivalent. Because each half is short, the half correlation is stepped up with the Spearman-Brown prophecy formula of Charles Spearman and William Brown (1910), 2r / (1 + r), so 0.60 becomes 0.75. Its general form predicts reliability when length changes with parallel items, with diminishing returns, and rearranged it gives the length needed. The formulas of Phillip Justin Rulon (1939) and John Clemans Flanagan (1937; Louis Guttman's lambda 4) need no step-up and allow unequal half variances. The odd-even method suits power tests; on a pure speed test a split from one timed sitting is spuriously high, so halves must be timed separately.

3.5 Internal Consistency: The Kuder-Richardson Formulas and Coefficient Alpha. Internal consistency is the agreement of all the items at one sitting, judged by comparing the item variances with the total-score variance; a right-wrong item's variance is pq, largest at p = 0.50. G. Frederic Kuder and Marion Webster Richardson (1937) gave KR-20 for dichotomous items of any difficulty and KR-21, which assumes equal difficulty and otherwise gives a lower estimate. Cyril Hoyt's analysis of variance (1941) gives the same value as KR-20. Lee Joseph Cronbach's coefficient alpha (1951) extends KR-20 to multi-point items, equals it for items scored 0 or 1 and is the mean of all possible split-half coefficients. Alpha measures internal consistency reliability, not validity, stability or rater agreement. It rises with the number of items, does not prove unidimensionality and, with unrelated errors, is a lower bound to reliability; McDonald's omega relaxes its assumption. Jum Nunnally's standards guide its interpretation, and 0.40 is poor.

3.6 Inter-Rater and Scorer Reliability. When judgement enters scoring, the scorer becomes a source of error. Scorer or inter-rater reliability is the agreement of independent scorers on the same responses, intra-rater reliability is one scorer's agreement with himself, and objectivity removes scorer error but not content or time sampling error. Pearson's r between scorers shows consistency, not absolute agreement. Percentage agreement is inflated by chance, which Jacob Cohen's kappa (1960) corrects; J. Richard Landis and Gary Koch (1977) labelled its ranges, and Joseph Fleiss offered a three-band scheme. Weighted kappa credits near misses on ordered categories, Fleiss' kappa serves three or more raters, the intraclass correlation (Patrick Shrout and Fleiss, 1979) serves continuous ratings, and Kendall's W (Maurice Kendall and Bernard Babington Smith, 1939) serves rankings. Keys, rubrics, training, blind scoring and double marking raise agreement, and the debate over John E. Exner's Rorschach Comprehensive System shows why it matters.

3.7 The Standard Error of Measurement and Confidence Bands. The standard error of measurement is the standard deviation of one person's scores over many independent testings, not the group's SD or the standard error of the mean. It equals SD × √(1 − r): SD 15 with reliability 0.91 gives 4.5, and 0.80 gives about 6.7. Bands of ±1, ±1.96 and ±2.58 SEM contain the true score with about 68, 95 and 99 percent confidence. Truman Lee Kelley's estimated true score, M + r(X − M), pulls extreme scores towards the mean (regression towards the mean). Two scores differ reliably when their difference exceeds 1.96 times the standard error of the difference, and the reliable change index of Neil Jacobson and Paula Truax (1991) applies this to change. Difference scores are unreliable when the two tests correlate highly, while composites are more reliable than their parts. Bands matter most at cut-offs, as Hall v. Florida (2014) showed.

3.8 What Raises and Lowers Reliability, and How Much Is Enough. One test has several coefficients because each method counts a different error: Rohini's retest gave 0.80, the split-half 0.82, KR-20 0.84 and KR-21 0.75. Reliability rises with more parallel items, middling difficulty, discriminating and homogeneous items, a heterogeneous group, objective scoring and standard conditions; it falls with guessing, ambiguity, restriction of range, floor and ceiling effects and long retest intervals, while speed and very short intervals inflate it. Reliability belongs to scores, not tests, which is what reliability generalisation (Tammi Vacha-Haase, 1998) studies. Jum Nunnally's standards are about 0.70 for early research, 0.80 for basic research and 0.90, with 0.95 desirable, for decisions about individuals; Joy Paul Guilford (1956) gave similar guidance. Cronbach and Goldine C. Gleser named the bandwidth-fidelity dilemma. The index of reliability, √r, caps validity, and Spearman's correction for attenuation estimates the correlation between true scores.

3.9 Beyond Classical Test Theory: Generalizability Theory and Item Response Theory. Classical theory has one undifferentiated error term, sample-dependent coefficients, one SEM for everybody and item and person statistics that depend on each other. Generalizability theory, from Lee Joseph Cronbach, Nageswari Rajaratnam and Goldine C. Gleser (1963) and the 1972 book written with Harinder Nanda, uses analysis of variance to split error among facets such as raters, occasions and items, and generalises to a universe score. A G study estimates the variance components; a D study forecasts the coefficients of designs with more raters or occasions. The generalizability coefficient serves relative decisions and the dependability coefficient, Φ, absolute ones. Item response theory, from Georg Rasch's model (1960) and the logistic models in Lord and Novick's book with chapters by Allan Birnbaum, replaces one SEM with test information that varies with ability, so that SE(θ) = 1 / √I(θ), the principle behind computerised adaptive testing.
