function, conditional standard error of measurement, computerised adaptive testing, item bank

From the past papers

  1. Which term refers to the consistency of a measure? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Validity (b) Reliability (c) Generalizability (d) Sensitivity

    Answer: (b) Reliability. Reliability is the consistency of measurement across occasions, items, forms or scorers (Section 3.1). Validity asks whether a test measures what it claims to measure; generalizability in its everyday sense is how far findings extend to other people and settings (in generalizability theory the generalizability coefficient is itself a reliability coefficient, but the plain term for consistency is still reliability); sensitivity is the proportion of people with a condition whom a test detects, or a scale's responsiveness to change.

  2. What is psychometric reliability primarily concerned with? (RML 2025)

    (a) Free from measurement error (b) Consistency of a test (c) Dependability of a test scores (d) All of the above

    Answer: (d) All of the above. Freedom from random measurement error, consistency and dependability are three faces of the one property (Section 3.1.2), so no single option is complete on its own. The same paper also asked what psychometric reliability "refers to the degree to which a test is", with free from measurement error, consistent and dependable as the options before "all of the above", and the key was again (d).

  3. In psychological biostatistics, what does reliability of a test refer to? (RML 2025)

    (a) The test measures what it is supposed to measure (b) The consistency of test results over time or samples (c) The fairness of the test to all groups (d) The test's ability to predict future behavior

    Answer: (b) The consistency of test results over time or samples. Option (a) is the definition of validity, (c) describes fairness (scores mean the same for every group) and (d) is predictive validity; all three are taught in Chapter 4, and none of them is consistency.

  4. If a study is 'reliable' this means that: (RML 2020)

    (a) It was conducted by a reputable researcher who can be trusted (b) The measures devised for concepts are stable on different occasions (c) The findings can be generalised to other social settings (d) The methods are stated clearly enough for the researcher to be replicated

    Answer: (b) The measures devised for concepts are stable on different occasions. In Alan Bryman's sense a study is reliable when its measures are consistent. The researcher's reputation says nothing about the measures, (c) is generalisability (external validity) and (d) is replicability; a study can have all three and still rest on an unreliable measure.

  5. What is test-retest reliability (RML 2021)

    (a) The test is measuring what it claims to be measuring (b) The test will produce consistent results (c) The client will improve performance second time round (d) All of the above

    Answer: (b) The test will produce consistent results. Test-retest reliability asks whether the same test gives consistent results when the same people take it again (Section 3.2). Option (a) is validity, and (c) is a practice effect, a threat to a retest study rather than its meaning, so "all of the above" is wrong.

  6. A test developer administers a new anxiety scale to a group of college students and, two weeks later, administers the same scale again to the same group. She then correlates the two sets of scores. This process is used to establish: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Split-half reliability (b) Inter-rater reliability (c) Test-retest reliability (d) Parallel forms reliability

    Answer: (c) Test-retest reliability. One form, the same people, two occasions, the scores correlated: that is the design, and the result is the coefficient of stability. Split-half needs only one sitting, inter-rater reliability needs two scorers of the same responses, and parallel forms need a second, separately built form, which this developer never wrote.

  7. The correlation between the odd and even halves of a test is .60. By the Spearman-Brown formula, the reliability of the full test is: (Recall set)

    (a) .80 (b) .60 (c) .75 (d) .36

    Answer: (c) .75. rfull = 2rhalf / (1 + rhalf) = 1.20 / 1.60 = 0.75. Option (b) forgets that .60 describes a half-length test, (d) squares it (reliability coefficients are never squared) and (a) has no route to it; the stepped-up value must lie between the half correlation and 1.

  8. The Spearman-Brown prophecy formula is used to: (Recall set)

    (a) Estimate agreement between raters on categorical judgements (b) Correct a validity coefficient for unreliability in the test and the criterion (c) Estimate the reliability of a speed test from a single sitting (d) Estimate full-test reliability from a split-half correlation, or predict reliability when test length changes

    Answer: (d). The formula of Charles Spearman and William Brown (1910) does one job in two guises: stepping up a split-half correlation and predicting the effect of a change of length. Agreement on categories is Cohen's kappa (Section 3.6), correcting validity for unreliability is Spearman's correction for attenuation (Section 3.8), and no formula can rescue a speed test split within one timed sitting, because the half-scores are equal by construction (Section 3.4.9).

  9. A test has a reliability of .60. How many times longer must it be made, with parallel items, to reach a reliability of .90? (Recall set)

    (a) 3 times (b) 6 times (c) 1.5 times (d) 9 times

    Answer: (b) 6 times. n = rtarget(1 − r) / [r(1 − rtarget)] = (0.90 × 0.40) / (0.60 × 0.10) = 0.36 / 0.06 = 6. Check: 3.60 / 4.00 = 0.90. Three times the length gives only 1.80 / 2.20 = 0.82, one and a half times 0.90 / 1.30 = 0.69, and nine times 5.40 / 5.80 = 0.93, more than is needed.

  10. A test with a reliability of .80 is cut to half its length. The predicted reliability of the short form is: (Recall set)

    (a) .40 (b) .67 (c) .89 (d) .80

    Answer: (b) .67. With n = 0.5, rnew = (0.5 × 0.80) / [1 + (0.5 − 1) × 0.80] = 0.40 / 0.60 = 0.67. Option (a) simply halves the coefficient, (c) is what doubling the test would give, and (d) assumes that length makes no difference.

  11. The Spearman-Brown correction of a split-half coefficient is accurate only when the two halves: (Recall set)

    (a) Are parallel: equal means and equal variances, measuring the same content (b) Differ in difficulty, so the range of scores is wider (c) Are scored by two different examiners (d) Are given on two different days

    Answer: (a). The formula assumes parallel halves; when their variances differ it overestimates, which is why Rulon's and Flanagan's formulas exist (Section 3.4.7). Halves of different difficulty violate the assumption, two examiners bring in scorer error, and two days bring in time sampling error, turning the design into a retest or alternate-forms study.

  12. Rulon's formula estimates split-half reliability from: (Recall set)

    (a) The variance of the differences between the two half-scores, relative to the total-score variance (b) The correlation between two administrations of the same test (c) The correlation between the halves, stepped up with Spearman-Brown (d) The proportion of examinees passing each item

    Answer: (a). Phillip Justin Rulon (1939): r = 1 − σ2d / σ2X, because the difference between two halves that share a true score is pure error. Option (b) is test-retest reliability, (c) is the two-step procedure Rulon made unnecessary, and (d) is item difficulty, p, the raw material of KR-20.

  13. In a split-half analysis, the standard deviation of the difference scores is 3 and the standard deviation of total scores is 6. Rulon's reliability coefficient is: (Recall set)

    (a) .75 (b) .86 (c) .50 (d) .25

    Answer: (a) .75. Rulon's formula needs variances, so square the SDs first: 1 − 9 / 36 = 1 − 0.25 = 0.75. Option (c) plugs in the SDs unsquared (1 − 3 / 6), (d) is the error share, and (b) wrongly steps 0.75 up with Spearman-Brown (1.50 / 1.75), although a Rulon coefficient already refers to the whole test.

  14. Odd-even method of splitting the test to estimate split-half reliability is MOST appropriate for : (RML 2022)

    (a) Projective tests (b) Neuropsychological tests (c) Power tests (d) Speed tests

    Answer: (c) Power tests. Where everyone has time to attempt every item they can, the odd and even items are two fair samples of the same ability. On a speed test the halves are equal by construction and the coefficient is spuriously high; the free responses to the ten different Rorschach cards cannot be divided into equivalent halves, and what matters there is scorer agreement; many neuropsychological tests are single, often timed tasks that cannot be cut into matching halves.

  15. For a pure speed test, the LEAST appropriate way to estimate reliability is: (Recall set)

    (a) Alternate (parallel) forms (b) Test-retest (c) Two separately timed half-tests (d) Odd-even split-half from a single timed sitting

    Answer: (d). On a pure speed test almost nobody makes errors, so a candidate's odd and even scores both just count how far she got and agree almost perfectly (Ranjit's 0.998). Alternate forms, retesting and separately timed halves (stepped up with Spearman-Brown) all make the two scores separate performances, so all three are acceptable.

  16. Kuder-Richardson Formula 20 (KR-20) is the appropriate reliability estimate when: (Recall set)

    (a) The same test is given twice, two weeks apart (b) Items are scored right/wrong (dichotomous) and vary in difficulty (c) The test is a pure speed test (d) Items are on a 5-point Likert scale

    Answer: (b). KR-20 (G. Frederic Kuder and Marion Webster Richardson, 1937) is built from pq, the variance of a 0 or 1 item, and does not assume equal difficulty. A repeat after two weeks calls for test-retest reliability, a speed test for separately timed halves, retesting or alternate forms, and Likert items for coefficient alpha.

  17. A 10-item right/wrong test has Σpq = 1.8 and a total-score variance of 6. Its KR-20 reliability is about: (Recall set)

    (a) .30 (b) .78 (c) .70 (d) .63

    Answer: (b) .78. KR-20 = [k / (k − 1)] × [1 − Σpq / σ2X] = (10 / 9) × (1 − 1.8 / 6) = 1.111 × 0.70 = 0.78. Each wrong option stops early or slips: (a) is the ratio before subtracting from 1, (c) forgets the k / (k − 1) correction, and (d) applies it upside down (0.70 × 9 / 10).

  18. A 20-item test has a mean of 12 and a variance of 16. Its KR-21 reliability is about: (Recall set)

    (a) .30 (b) .70 (c) .85 (d) .74

    Answer: (d) .74. KR-21 = [k / (k − 1)] × [1 − M(k − M) / (kσ2X)]: M(k − M) = 12 × 8 = 96, kσ2 = 20 × 16 = 320, 96 / 320 = 0.30, 1 − 0.30 = 0.70, and (20 / 19) × 0.70 = 0.74. Option (a) is the ratio, (b) forgets the 20 / 19 correction, and (c) matches no step.

  19. When the items of a test differ in difficulty, KR-21 compared with KR-20 gives: (Recall set)

    (a) Exactly the same estimate (b) An estimate of test-retest stability instead (c) A lower estimate (d) A higher estimate

    Answer: (c) A lower estimate. KR-21 assumes every item has the average difficulty; because the pq curve is an arch, items of mixed difficulty always have a smaller Σpq than KR-21 assumes, so KR-21 subtracts too much and comes out lower (Section 3.5.4). The two agree only when all items are equally difficult, and both come from one sitting, so neither estimates stability.

  20. Cronbach's coefficient alpha is identical to KR-20 when: (Recall set)

    (a) The test is a speed test (b) All items are scored dichotomously (0 or 1) (c) The test is given twice (d) The two halves have equal variances

    Answer: (b). For a 0 or 1 item the item variance is exactly pq, so the alpha formula turns into KR-20 term for term. No single-sitting coefficient should be used on a speed test, a test given twice calls for test-retest reliability, and equal half variances are the condition under which Spearman-Brown agrees with the Flanagan-Rulon formulas.

  21. Which of the following is assessed by Cronbach's alpha? (RML 2021)

    (a) Concurrent validity (b) Inter-rater reliability (c) Test retest reliability (d) Internal consistency

    Answer: (d) Internal consistency. Alpha (Lee Joseph Cronbach, 1951) measures how well the items of a multi-item scale, answered at one sitting, agree with one another. Concurrent validity is agreement with a criterion measured at the same time, inter-rater reliability is agreement between scorers (kappa, ICC), and test-retest reliability needs two occasions.

  22. Cronbach's alpha assesses : (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Validity (b) Reliability (internal consistency) (c) Variance (d) Regression

    Answer: (b) Reliability (internal consistency). Alpha is a reliability coefficient, so it says nothing about validity: a scale can be perfectly consistent and measure the wrong thing. Variance is an ingredient of the alpha formula, not what it measures, and regression is the prediction of one variable from another.

  23. Which of the following refers to Inter-rater reliability? (RML 2021)

    (a) The degree to which two tests measure the same construct. (b) The degree to which a clinician can predict future behaviour. (c) The degree to which two clinicians will agree on interpretation or scoring of a test. (d) The degree to which the items in the test relate to each other

    Answer: (c). Inter-rater reliability is agreement between people who independently score or interpret the same responses (Section 3.6). Two tests measuring one construct is convergent validity (or parallel forms, if built as equivalent forms), predicting future behaviour is predictive validity, and items relating to each other is internal consistency.

  24. A test has an SD of 15 and a reliability of .91. The standard error of measurement (SEM) is: (Recall set)

    (a) 0.3 (b) 1.35 (c) 13.65 (d) 4.5

    Answer: (d) 4.5. SEM = SD × √(1 − r) = 15 × √0.09 = 15 × 0.3 = 4.5. Option (a) is √(1 − r) without the SD, (b) forgets the square root (15 × 0.09), and (c) is SD × r. A 95 percent band around an IQ of 100 is then about 91 to 109.

  25. A predictor and a criterion correlate .40. Their reliabilities are .64 and .81. Corrected for attenuation, the correlation between their true scores is about: (Recall set)

    (a) .77 (b) .29 (c) .56 (d) .40

    Answer: (c) .56. Spearman's correction (1904): rxy / √(rxx × ryy) = 0.40 / (0.80 × 0.90) = 0.40 / 0.72 = 0.56. Option (a) forgets the square root (0.40 / 0.5184), (b) multiplies instead of dividing (0.40 × 0.72), and (d) is the uncorrected correlation.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Cronbach's alpha (Lee Joseph Cronbach, 1951) measures internal consistency reliability: how well the items of a multi-item scale, answered at one sitting, agree. It is not validity of any kind, not test-retest or inter-rater reliability, and not variance or regression. asked 10 times
  2. Test-retest reliability: the same test given to the same people on two occasions and the two sets of scores correlated (an anxiety scale given twice, two weeks apart); the result is the coefficient of stability, which shows stability of scores over time. asked 7 times
  3. Standard error of measurement = SD × √(1 − r): SD 15 and reliability 0.91 give 15 × 0.3 = 4.5; reliability 0.80 gives 15 × 0.447 = about 6.7. Not SD × √r, SD × r, SD / r or SD × (1 + r). asked 5 times
  4. Rulon's formula (Phillip Justin Rulon, 1939): r = 1 − σ2d / σ2X, from the variance of the half-score differences over the total variance, with no Spearman-Brown step. Variances 4 and 20 give 0.80; SDs 3 and 6 must be squared first (1 − 9 / 36 = 0.75). asked 3 times
  5. The odd-even split is most appropriate for power tests. For a pure speed test an odd-even split from one timed sitting is the least appropriate estimate (spuriously near 1.00); time the halves separately and step up with Spearman-Brown, or use test-retest or alternate forms. asked 3 times
  6. Reliability is the term for the consistency of a measure: a test that gives consistent results on repeated administration, over time or samples, is reliable (not valid, standardised, normed, generalizable or sensitive). asked 3 times
  7. KR-20 (G. Frederic Kuder and Marion Webster Richardson, 1937) is for dichotomously scored items (right or wrong, yes or no) that vary in difficulty; coefficient alpha generalises it to multi-point items and is identical to it when every item is scored 0 or 1. asked 3 times
  8. The Spearman-Brown prophecy formula (Charles Spearman and William Brown, 1910) estimates full-test reliability from a split-half correlation and predicts reliability when test length changes by a factor n: reliability rises with more parallel items. It is not kappa, not the correction for attenuation and not a cure for a speed test. asked 3 times
  9. Psychometric reliability means freedom from measurement error, consistency and dependability of scores, all at once, so the key is "all of the above". asked twice
  10. General Spearman-Brown: rnew = n r / [1 + (n − 1) r]. A test of 0.50 made three times as long gives 1.50 / 2.00 = 0.75; a test of 0.80 cut to half its length gives 0.40 / 0.60 = 0.67. asked twice
  11. KR-21 = [k / (k − 1)] × [1 − M(k − M) / (kσ2X)] assumes equal item difficulty and gives a lower estimate than KR-20 when difficulties differ; 20 items, mean 12 and variance 16 give 0.74. asked twice
  12. Cohen's kappa (Jacob Cohen, 1960) measures inter-rater agreement on categorical judgements, corrected for chance: κ = (po − pe) / (1 − pe). Not test-retest, internal consistency, validity or an ANOVA effect size. asked 3 times
  13. Split-half step-up: halves correlating 0.60 give a full-test reliability of 2 × 0.60 / (1 + 0.60) = 1.20 / 1.60 = 0.75. asked twice
  14. Test-retest suits stable traits, such as intelligence and personality, that should not change over the interval; it is inappropriate for rapidly changing states such as current mood, where a low coefficient is expected. asked twice
  15. Inter-rater reliability is the degree to which two clinicians agree on the scoring or interpretation of the same test. asked once
  16. A reliable study is one whose measures are stable on different occasions; a reputable researcher, generalisable findings and replicable methods are not reliability. asked once
  17. Length needed: n = rtarget(1 − r) / [r(1 − rtarget)]; raising 0.60 to 0.90 needs 0.36 / 0.06 = 6 times the length. asked once
  18. The Spearman-Brown step-up is accurate only when the halves are parallel: equal means and equal variances, measuring the same content. asked once
  19. KR-20 worked: 10 items, Σpq = 1.8, total variance 6 give (10 / 9) × (1 − 0.30) = 1.111 × 0.70 = 0.78. asked once
  20. Item variance of a right-wrong item is pq: an item passed by 30 percent has 0.30 × 0.70 = 0.21; the maximum, 0.25, is at p = 0.50. asked once
  21. Correction for attenuation (Spearman, 1904): rxy / √(rxx ryy); 0.40 with reliabilities 0.64 and 0.81 gives 0.40 / 0.72 = 0.56. The index of reliability, √rxx, caps any validity coefficient. asked once
  22. Kappa bands (J. Richard Landis and Gary Koch, 1977): 0 to 0.20 slight, 0.21 to 0.40 fair, 0.41 to 0.60 moderate, 0.61 to 0.80 substantial, 0.81 to 1.00 almost perfect, so 0.85 is almost perfect. asked once
  23. The SEM estimates the range within which a person's true score probably falls: obtained score ± 1 SEM for about 68 percent, ± 1.96 SEM for 95 percent and ± 2.58 SEM for 99 percent. asked once
  24. The major challenge of split-half reliability is dividing the test into two nearly equivalent halves, not computing the Spearman-Brown step-up. asked once
  25. Alpha is the mean of all possible split-half coefficients (Cronbach, 1951); averaging over every split increases the accuracy of the estimate. asked once
  26. The limitation of every internal consistency method is that it rests on a single administration, so it says nothing about stability over time. asked once
  27. An alpha of 0.40 is poor. Jum Nunnally's standards: about 0.70 for early research, 0.80 for basic research, 0.90 at least (0.95 desirable) for important decisions about individuals. asked once
  28. Generalizability theory (Lee Joseph Cronbach, Goldine C. Gleser, Harinder Nanda and Nageswari Rajaratnam, 1972) decomposes observed-score variance into facets such as raters, items and occasions by analysis of variance; the G study estimates the components, the D study forecasts coefficients for new designs. asked once
